# DeepSeek Brainstorming — Research Corpus for the Compact Free-Space Spatial Telepresence Node

> Working concept: two identical ~10 cm × 10 cm × 10 cm autonomous cubes capture their local humans,
> compute compact dynamic representations, transmit them over a low-latency network, and reconstruct
> the remote human directly into free space (no wall, screen, headset, or external projector).
>
> Pipeline: capture → compact dynamic human representation → network transport → reconstruction →
> free-space optical emission. Three frontiers: computational representation, network/temporal
> transport, free-space optical engine (volumetric, holographic, light-field, aerial-imaging, or hybrid).
>
> Central hypothesis: *a remote human can be perceptually reconstructed in free space using a
> physically compact optical engine if the human is represented by a sufficiently efficient dynamic
> neural representation and the optical system generates only perceptually necessary spatial and
> angular information.*

## Method

- Corpus: arXiv 2022-01 → 2026-08, built by `research/arxiv/build_telepresence.py` (per-category
  keyword-cluster queries across 14 categories: physics.optics, physics.app-ph, eess.IV, eess.SP,
  cs.GR, cs.CV, cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML, cs.NI, cs.MM, cs.IT, cs.HC, cs.SD).
- Each paper was read from its arXiv e-print source (LaTeX) or PDF text in full, then documented
  here with ID, title, and a long paragraph: what the paper does (methods, math, results) and how
  it can serve this project. Sources are deleted from disk immediately after documentation.
- Section 5 contains Agent 1's independent web research (vendors, patents, standards, non-arXiv works).


## Track 1 — Free-Space Optical Engine (volumetric / holographic / light-field / aerial)

### 2201.07785 — Enhanced detection techniques of Orbital Angular Momentum states in the classical and quantum regimes

Paper from the quantum-optics groups of Sciarrino's Sapienza lab, Queen's University Belfast and Palermo demonstrating that modeling OAM-carrying beams with Hypergeometric-Gaussian (HyGG) modes, rather than the standard Laguerre-Gauss (LG) assumption, markedly improves two detection techniques. The states are engineered by a one-dimensional discrete-time quantum walk of five cascaded q-plates interleaved with waveplates, generating OAM states with m in {+-1,+-3,+-5} and vector vortex beams, both classically and at the single-photon level. The HyGG model results from solving Fresnel propagation through each q-plate, expanding the output as a superposition of LG modes truncated at k=3. Holographic detection (SLM projection + single-mode fiber) gives average state fidelities of 0.9831+-0.0005 (HyGG) vs 0.9671+-0.0010 (LG), and up to ~3x higher fiber coupling efficiencies (e.g., 3.06+-0.16 for |m|=5). A CNN (4 conv layers, 32 3x3 ReLU filters, 2x2 max-pooling, 128-node fully-connected layer + softmax) trained only on 400 simulated images per class reaches 0.815+-0.065 accuracy on 1500 experimental VVB images with HyGG data vs 0.632+-0.097 with LG data; injecting up to 3 degrees of waveplate-angle error into training improves both (0.765 vs 0.605). For the telepresence cube this is relevant to display and free-space transport of OAM-encoded light: it shows SLM-hologram-based detection and efficiency gains available by using the physically correct beam model, directly applicable to an OAM-multiplexed free-space link between nodes and to SLM-driven display engines.

### 2202.02527 — Complex-amplitude Fourier single-pixel imaging via coherent structured illumination

Complex-amplitude Fourier single-pixel imaging (CFSI) via coherent structured illumination, by Zhao et al. (Hebei University / University of Macau), is a letter demonstrating single-pixel acquisition of both amplitude and phase of a transmissive object. A 632.8 nm He-Ne beam illuminates a phase-only LCoS-SLM displaying Fourier-basis phase patterns; because LCoS modulation is not 100% efficient, the reflected light contains both the phase-modulated structured field and an unmodulated reference traveling the same path, producing common-path interference — a compact, stable single-beam interferometer. The zero-frequency component of the object's spectrum is collected by a 9-micron-core single-mode fiber and a PMT; a 4-step phase-shifting sequence (0, pi/2, pi, 3pi/2) recovers the complex Fourier spectrum F = alpha*{(D0-Dpi) + j*(D3pi/2-Dpi/2)}, and an inverse FFT reconstructs the complex transmittance. At 128x128 resolution (8-micron LCoS pitch, 1/3-magnification 4f system) the theoretical resolution/FOV are 5.33 microns / 682.67 microns; a USAF-1951 chart confirms 6.2 microns. Undersampling (17.2-62.2% sampling ratios) removes high-frequency noise and cuts acquisition from 195 minutes (full sampling) to ~34 minutes; low-frequency noise is suppressed using a 3x3 central neighborhood estimate. Demonstrated on etched glass phase objects (372-1051 nm etch depths), a damselfly wing, and the resolution chart. For the telepresence cube this is a capture-stage technique: phase-amplitude imaging with a single-pixel detector suits invisible wavelengths or photon-starved regimes, though minute-scale acquisition limits it to static scenes.

### 2202.08650 — Shaping entangled photons through emulated turbulent atmosphere

Shekel, Lib, Sardas and Bromberg (Hebrew University of Jerusalem) demonstrate compensation of turbulent-atmosphere scattering for spatially entangled photons using pump shaping — applying wavefront shaping to the bright classical pump rather than to the fragile photon pairs. Spatially entangled photons are produced by SPDC in a 2-mm type-0 PPKTP crystal pumped by a 14 mW, 404 nm CW laser (Schmidt number ~680); SLM1 shapes the pump beam, and SLM2 emulates turbulence with phase screens obeying Kolmogorov/von Karman statistics (PSD 0.49 r0^(-5/3) (k^2+k0^2)^(-11/6) exp(-k^2/km^2), Fried parameter r0; scaled parameters r0=0.14 mm, l0=10 mm, li=5 microns emulate a 1-km link with Cn^2=10^-15). A partitioning optimization over 30x30 SLM1 pixels maximizes pump power at a target area; because the pump's angular spectrum transfers to the two-photon correlations, the entangled-photon signal is simultaneously focused. For the static case they measure pump enhancement eta~31 (efficiency ~40%) and SPDC enhancement eta~15 (~15%, after accidental-count subtraction); for a dynamically drifting atmosphere (frozen-turbulence phase screen moving 1 pixel per 150 s) they reach eta~45 (pump) and eta~15 (SPDC). Higher-order spatial modes can be encoded on the compensating phase, e.g., a pi-step adding OAM. SLM response (~10 Hz) and few-counts-per-second SPDC signals currently limit tracking speed; fast modulators (deformable mirrors, DMDs, MEMS) could scale to real turbulence timescales. For the cube, this supports the free-space transport stage: classical-beam wavefront correction that also restores quantum and spatial-mode channels between nodes.

### 2202.10572 — Ghost projection. II. Beam shaping using realistic spatially-random masks

Ghost Projection II (Ceddia, Pelliccia, Kingston and Paganin, U. Melbourne/ANSTO) develops beam shaping via NNLS-optimized combinations of spatially random masks, using realistically imaged x-ray masks: 5-nm-foam Ni foam and 120-grit sandpaper. A desired 40x40 radiant-exposure image is decomposed into weights w_k over mask sub-fields-of-view; for the Ni foam (25-nm sub-FOVs stepped 1 pixel at a time, 30 microns/pixel) N'=672 of the candidate masks are selected, reaching a noiseless NNLS SNR of 3.34x10^8; with Poisson photon noise (10^4 contrast photons), exposure noise (shutter tolerance sigma_w=1/100 s) and translational noise (sigma_ij=1/10 pixel) the SNR drops to 4.70+-0.14 with a 10^5-photon pedestal. A TSP-optimized scan path (3678.3 px = 110.35 mm) plus exposure gives ~130 s total per pattern at 1 mm/s stage speed and 10^4 photons/px/s flux. Extensive guidelines cover mask transmission, speckle contrast, delroughness, and shutter-free continuous trajectories; an improved design (consecutive masks, 50-nm FOVs, pedestal 10) yields final SNR 7.17+-0.26 — 2.7x better than the baseline (2.67+-0.11) — with resolution on the order of micrometers. Applications envisioned: shaping hard x-rays, gamma rays and matter waves, universal-mask lithography beyond EUV, and volumetric additive manufacturing by reverse tomography. For the telepresence cube this is a display-stage option: writing arbitrary light patterns without configurable optics using cheap random masks, enabling robust free-space pattern projection at the cost of a uniform exposure pedestal and minute-scale throughput.

### 2202.11138 — Parametric Mie resonances and directional amplification in time-modulated scatterers

Asadchy, Lamprianidis et al. (Stanford, Aalto, KIT, IIT Kanpur) present a theory of parametric Mie resonances in a spherical particle whose permittivity is modulated in time at twice the incident-light frequency — a sub-wavelength photonic time crystal hosting optical parametric amplification. Using Floquet-Mie theory and temporal coupled-mode theory, they show the sphere supports parametric resonances whose threshold modulation strength scales as M_thr proportional to gamma_tot + Delta-omega^2/(2 gamma_tot); for a Lorentz-Drude material (omega_p=3.5 omega_r, omega_m=0.5 omega_r) the momentum bandgap width Delta-kappa = M*(omega_m/4c)*omega_p^2/(sqrt(omega_r^2-omega_m^2/4)*sqrt(omega_r^2-omega_m^2/4+omega_p^2)). Higher-order multipoles oscillate at very low modulation (magnetic nu=5 at M=2.27x10^-4). Crucially, parametric-oscillation curves for different multipoles intersect continuously, so two modes can be amplified coherently at one frequency with controllable relative phase and amplitude — impossible with gain media, where lasing occurs only at discrete points. Two designed spheres: (A) M=0.68, R=1.048(2c/omega_m), electric-plus-magnetic dipole resonance crossing (omega_inc=0.498 omega_m) gives unidirectional scattering with zero backward scattering (first Kerker condition), C_sca/C_geom=2629.2 and C_abs/C_geom=-2627.5 (stationary sphere: 5.5); (B) M=0.093, R=1.481(2c/omega_m), electric-quadrupole plus magnetic-octupole crossing (omega_inc=0.4995 omega_m) gives dips in both forward and backward scattering (first and second Kerker conditions), C_sca/C_geom=858.3 and C_abs=-857.5 (stationary: 2.53). Applications include shadow-free detectors of weak light (scattering sideways), nanoscale directional amplifiers, and parametric metasurfaces. For the cube, time-modulated meta-atoms offer display and perception elements: active, amplifying directional scattering and extremely sensitive detection for the free-space optical link.

### 2202.13668 — Reflection-mode optical diffraction tomography for label-free imaging of thick biological specimens

Reflection-mode optical diffraction tomography (rODT), by Kang, Zhou, Brelen, Mak, So and Yaqoob (MIT / Chinese University of Hong Kong), is an epi-mode, label-free volumetric imaging technique for thick scattering biological specimens, built on speckle-correlation reflection phase microscopy (SpeCRPM): a rotating diffuser creates broadband dynamic speckle illumination (lambda_c=800 nm, Delta-lambda=40 nm), a Linnik interferometer with two identical NA=1 water-immersion objectives provides single-shot wide-field off-axis holography, and sample depth z_S plus reference arrival time tau_R are scanned by motorized stages. Light propagation through the multilayer sample is modeled with a modified first-order Born approximation, giving an OTF that includes specimen-induced spherical aberration (phase shift Delta-phi = sum d_i (sqrt((n_i omega/c)^2-k_r^2) - sqrt((n_0 omega/c)^2-k_r^2))); a look-up table [n,d]=L(z_S,tau_R) retrieves refractive index and thickness per layer. Simulations predict 300-nm lateral / 1.2-micron axial PSF at the surface (400 nm / 2 microns at 400 microns depth). Through a tissue phantom (~2.8 scattering mean free paths, l_s~160 microns), live red blood cells at 700 microns depth are imaged with quantitative phase, and membrane fluctuations measured at 66 fps (RMS ~100 nm vs 40 nm background). Ex vivo rat cornea: epithelium (n=1.37, 32 microns), stroma (n=1.35, 138 microns), endothelium (n=1.39, 8 microns); Dua's and Descemet's membranes 4 microns apart are resolved with nanometer-scale surface profiling; 3D Richardson-Lucy deconvolution improves nerve-fiber FWHM from 751 to 584 nm (~30%). For the telepresence cube this is a capture-stage candidate: deep, label-free volumetric phase imaging of the user through scattering media, with sub-micron axial resolution.

### 2203.02202 — Carbon Footprint of Selecting and Training Deep Learning Models for Medical Image Analysis

This paper is an empirical measurement study of the energy consumption and carbon footprint of selecting and training deep learning models for medical image analysis, using nnU-Net for segmentation across several public datasets (BraTS brain, MONuSeg, Heart). It compares four estimation frameworks — MLEC, EIT, Carbontracker, and CodeCarbon — which yield widely different estimates, and it applies the THETA guideline. Headline numbers: MONuSeg training took 61 h 55 min ≈ 30.2 kWh mixed energy, Heart 116 h 40 min ≈ 48.9 kWh, and Brain 368 h 20 min ≈ 66.1 kWh; the authors also train with automatic mixed precision (AMP), cutting time by roughly 17–20%. The study emphasizes that reported emissions depend on grid energy mix, location, and PUE. Relevance to the telepresence cube: the cube's representation stage will likely rely on learned models (neural avatars, Gaussian-splatting priors, neural codecs), and this paper supplies the methodology to estimate and minimize the training carbon/energy budget and to choose energy-efficient training settings (AMP, model selection vs. full training). It does not constrain any optical architecture and has no compatibility issues; it is a meta-analysis rather than a system. Caution: it covers training-time energy only, not inference-time energy, so it informs development and cloud-training choices for the cube's neural components rather than the runtime power budget.
---

### 2203.05822 — aiWave: Volumetric Image Compression with 3-D Trained Affine Wavelet-like Transform

This paper presents aiWave, a learned 3-D wavelet-like transform for volumetric image compression that supports both lossy and lossless modes. Using a lifting scheme, 3-D CNN prediction and update filters compute h = A(x_e) ⊙ (x_o − P′(x_e)) and l = B(h) ⊙ (x_e + U′(h)), where the affine maps A, B (sigmoid-bounded and invertible) make the transform data-dependent while guaranteeing perfect reconstruction in lossless mode via rounding. Quantization is uniform with step QS; the rate is −log2 p_q(q) with either factorized (aiWave-light) or context-based (aiWave-heavy) entropy models, optimized with λ-weighted MSE over λ ∈ {1,4,16,64,128}; training used Adam at learning rate 1e-4 for about 6 days on a GTX 1080Ti. On six datasets (FAFB, FIB-25, CT-Spleen, MRI-heart, Chaos-CT, Attention), aiWave-heavy beats JP3D by up to 8.885 dB BD-PSNR (−67.8% BD-rate on Attention); aiWave-light encodes a 64³ volume in 0.281 s on GPU vs. 0.487 s for JP3D, while aiWave-heavy takes about 986 s due to context modeling. The lossless mode reaches 4.545 bpp on MRNet vs. 5.97 bpp for JP3D. For the telepresence cube this targets representation/transport of volumetric data (e.g., captured 3-D scene volumes or voxelized light-field data): the affine-transform trick is a principled way to get content-adaptive reversible transforms, and the lossless mode guarantees no information loss. Warnings: aiWave-heavy's context entropy coding is far too slow for real-time edge use and GPU decode is required; despite the track-1 assignment, this is compression, not optics.
---

### 2203.06784 — Expansion of image space in enhanced-NA Fresnel holographic display

This paper expands the image space of a Fresnel holographic display by enhancing the numerical aperture NA = NΔx/(2z), which sets the viewing angle Ω_V = 2 sin⁻¹(NA) and defines the critical distance z_c = NΔx²/λ beyond which diffraction-limited resolution is lost. Phase-only Fresnel holograms are computed with a modified Gerchberg–Saxton algorithm; because the SLM pixel pitch p is larger than the hologram sampling interval, high-order images appear at sin θ_m = mλ/p, and an angular low-pass filter in direction-cosine space removes them. Experiments use a Holoeye PLUTO phase SLM (1920×1080, 8 µm pitch) with a 473 nm laser: viewing angle is 4.2° at z = z_c and 7.7° at z = z_c/2, demonstrating that moving the image plane closer to the SLM expands the viewable image space. For the telepresence cube this directly informs the display stage for a holographic SLM-based optical architecture: it quantifies the fundamental viewing-angle vs. resolution vs. distance trade-off for a 10-cm-cube-sized holographic engine and gives a practical recipe (angular filtering, modified Gerchberg–Saxton) for phase hologram synthesis. Warnings: a phase-only SLM plus a coherent laser source are required, with associated speckle; the expanded angles (≤8°) remain narrow, so the hologram plane must be placed very close to the viewer; color multiplexing is not addressed.
---

### 2204.00884 — Optical modelling of accommodative light field display system and prediction of human eye responses

This paper develops a wave-optics simulation framework to model accommodative light-field displays together with a realistic human eye model, and predicts the viewer's accommodation response. Elemental PSFs are computed via Fourier transform of a generalized pupil function with subaperture shift Δs_n = z_eye(1/z_r − 1/z_CDP)[p_n, q_n], modeling how each display subaperture projects rays that must intersect at the rendered depth z_r within the eye's pupil; polychromatic PSFs are weighted by the luminosity function, and accommodation is predicted with the VSOTF metric. The eye model includes Zernike aberrations sampled from the Thibos population statistics (10 virtual eyes), chromatic aberration (A = 633.26, c = 214.10), and accommodation-dependent spherical aberration (slope −0.0435 µm/D). Simulated viewpoint densities are 2×2, 3×3, and 4×4 subapertures within a 3 mm pupil at a 2D central depth plane (CDP) for stimuli spanning 0–4 D. Headline finding: with 2×2 viewpoints the predicted accommodation error is small and nearly constant across vergence demands, contradicting earlier studies; higher viewpoint density mainly widens the depth of field. For the telepresence cube this informs the display stage for a light-field optical architecture: it quantifies the minimum viewpoint density (and hence renderer cost) needed to elicit correct accommodation and avoid vergence–accommodation conflict in the viewer, and it defines CDP placement. Warnings: it is a simulation study — real optics, eye tracking, and pupil-size variation still need validation.
---

### 2204.10587 — Adaptive hyperspectral imaging using structured illumination in a spatial light modulator-based interferometer

This paper builds an adaptive hyperspectral imaging instrument by replacing one mirror of a Michelson interferometer with a reflective liquid-crystal spatial light modulator (SLM, HOLOEYE LC-R 1080, 8.1 µm pixels, 1920×1200). A 3×3 checkerboard gray-level phase mask (400-pixel squares, gray values 0–255) is displayed on the SLM; because the SLM's liquid-crystal birefringence is electrically tunable, each of the nine beamlets (generated by a 3×3 aperture mask, 2 mm-radius holes) incident on a different checkerboard square receives a distinct phase delay δ_i = 2πΔn_i·d/λ (Δn = n_e − n_o), so nine near-contiguous spectral channels are captured in a single shot with no mechanical scanning. A supercontinuum source (LEUKOS SAMBA) plus electro-optic filter (LEUKOS BEBOP, 5–100 nm bandwidth) mimics a solar target at central wavelength 637.4 nm (Fe X line) with 30 nm bandwidth. Headline numbers: spectral resolution ≈3.8 nm (limited by SLM reflectivity ≈66%), interferometer FSR ≈11 nm (arm-difference 13 µm, ~17 nm at 10 µm), finesse F ≈14.4 (R₁≈0.66, R₂≈0.975), wavelength tunability ≈8 nm with spectral sampling ≈0.03 nm per gray unit, phase-mask update time ≈18 ms (SLM response; ~1 kHz SLMs exist), phase jitter 0.2–0.3 nm in the most linear gray range (80–180), max phase flicker 19% over 26 s (cut ~80% by cooling to −8°C), 24 V DC power, resonance condition 2µd = mλ. For our telepresence cube this paper contributes to the capture stage (space-resolved multi-spectral sensing of the scene) and, more importantly, provides engineering data for any holographic-SLM free-space display: phase-only SLM driving (polarization parallel to LC director via polarizer + half-wave plate), one-time phase calibration, and quantitative phase-stability/flicker characterization that bounds achievable hologram reconstruction fidelity. It constrains SLM-based optical engines to devices with calibrated look-up tables and favors dielectric-multilayer-coated SLMs (>97% reflectivity) and thermal stabilization. No latency/Mbps/voxel data are reported; it is an experimental-optics demonstration without a display output, so direct free-space-emission use is limited.
---

### 2205.05144 — Limited-memory BFGS Optimisation of Phase-Only Computer-Generated Hologram for Fraunhofer Diffraction

This short OSA paper optimizes phase-only computer-generated holograms (CGH) for the far-field (Fraunhofer) regime using the L-BFGS quasi-Newton optimizer with a cross-entropy (CE) loss, MSE(X,Y)=(1/n)Σ(X_i-Y_i)^2 vs CE(X,Y)=-(1/n)ΣX_i log(Y_i), both flattened to 1D arrays. The optimization argument is the phase-only hologram H; the objective is the loss between target image T and reconstructed image R computed by Fraunhofer diffraction. In simulation on the mandrill target, all combinations of Adam/L-BFGS x MSE/CE were run at learning rate 0.1 for 100 iterations from the same random phase initialization; Adam+CE failed to converge, while L-BFGS+CE converged fastest and to the lowest reconstruction MSE (~0.035 vs ~0.095 for the others). The best hologram was validated on a holographic projector with a binary-phase ferroelectric LC SLM (1440 Hz refresh, 1280x1024, 13.6 µm pixel pitch, 532 nm DPSS laser, 50 mW); binary-phase modulation imposes a 180-degree rotational symmetry in the replay field, so the target was made rotationally symmetric, and captured replay fields confirmed the CGH with residual noise from binary quantization. Relevance: display stage — L-BFGS+CE is a candidate high-quality hologram solver for a phase-SLM-based free-space optical engine, and the Fraunhofer far-field formulation suits projecting an aerial image onto a volume. Compatibility warnings: the method is an iterative per-hologram optimization (no timings reported; convergence requires ~100 iterations), so it is far from the cube's real-time budget unless unrolled/learned; binary-phase SLM symmetry halves useful area; no code released.

### 2205.05823 — Continuous wavelet transform of multiview images using wavelets based on voxel patterns

This paper (Saveljev, Konyang University) constructs a family of multiview wavelets — "pattern-based" (PB) wavelets — whose basis functions are derived from the voxel patterns (VPs) that tile the image plane of lenticular/barrier autostereoscopic multiview, integral, plenoptic and light-field displays. A VP of order n is a series of n unit-height rectangular pulses of width 1/n distributed over a display cell, aligned differently for positive (in front of screen, separated pulses) and negative (behind screen, close pulses) depth planes; Fourier transforms are derived analytically as sums of sinc terms (pairs of shifted pulses, plus a central pulse for odd n). Wavelets are built by biasing each VP (bias b1Dn=1/2 for n=1,2, b=1/n generally) to achieve zero mean and normalizing to unit square norm (c1Dn, c2Dn coefficients), generalizing the French-hat wavelet; 1D (horizontal-parallax-only) and 2D (full-parallax, separable products over n x n cells) versions, plus a scaling function and special functions (chirp, period-1 pulse trains) to cover spectral holes. Direct and inverse continuous wavelet transforms (CWT) are demonstrated on a binary multiview tetrahedron and a gray-scale light-field image of books, producing per-depth-plane 2D coefficient arrays; depth-selective filtering is shown (titles readable only in their own planes), and coefficient modifications (reversed depth; full-parallax→HPO conversion) followed by inverse transform give correct 3D results when printed and viewed through crossed lenticular plates (15 lpi, 1.69 mm cells). Relevance: representation and display stages — the wavelets are a native, sparse, depth-plane basis for lenticular/light-field content, offering a candidate compact transform representation for the directional light-field optical engine, and the depth-plane selectivity could enable depth analysis/compression of the captured human. Warning: requires rectified views, integer pixels per cell, and is designed only for multiview images (not general imagery).

### 2205.07030 — Realistic Defocus Blur for Multiplane Computer-Generated Holography

This paper (IEEE VIS/TVCG) presents a multiplane computer-generated holography (CGH) pipeline that reconstructs phase-only holograms with near-incoherent defocus blur and suppressed edge-fringe artifacts on LC-SLM holographic displays. Two contributions: (1) a targeting scheme — instead of painting out-of-focus regions black, the depth map V_depth is quantized (1-4 bits, up to 16 planes), each plane's out-of-focus regions are convolved with a Gaussian kernel whose size is inversely related to distance from the focus plane, and targets are formed as P_k = w2(w0*P_defocus_k + w1*P_focus_k); (2) a loss L_m = m0*L2(P_k,I_k) + m1*L2(M_k⊙P_k, M_k⊙I_k) that weights the in-focus region via binary mask M_k (surveyed values m0=1.0, m1=2.1) — the second term is a focus-weighted L2. The pipeline works with iterative (Gerchberg-Saxton, SGD) and non-iterative (Double Phase) CGH, and best results come from SGD optimization with a DP-inspired phase update rule: phase is decomposed into phi_low/phi_high around a mean, with offset as optimizable variable, applied on even/odd pixel checkerboards, in a forward model u(x,y)=(O_h*h_r)*h_-r that virtually propagates out to ~30 cm and back to enable SLM-plane optimization. Validation used a proof-of-concept three-laser (473/515/639 nm) display with Holoeye Pluto-VIS SLM, 4f system, camera on a 25-mm X-stage; target planes 1 mm apart. Numbers: full-color 8-plane hologram optimization takes 57 s / 4623 MB on an RTX 3070 laptop (vs 1.5 s for tensor holography); experiments show contrast-preserving, fringe-free results and realistic blur vs naive targeting and tensor holography. Relevance: display stage for an SLM-based optical engine — the loss/targeting scheme is the strongest candidate for volumetric multiplane reconstruction of the avatar with natural focus cues; warning: iterative optimization is far from real-time (57 s), and occlusion handling in multiplane CGH remains unsolved.

### 2205.15941 — Memory-efficient Segmentation of High-resolution Volumetric MicroCT Images

This MIDL paper tackles GPU-memory-limited 3D segmentation of ultra-high-resolution volumes (~250M voxels) with a two-stage cascaded network. Stage 1 is "meU-net," a memory-efficient 3D U-net that trains on two patches simultaneously: a standard patch (P x P x P, e.g., 160³) that supervises the entire network, and an expanded patch (kP x kP x kP, k=1.25-1.75, sampled concentrically) that feeds global context but disables gradient computation at the first resolution level (halving memory) and only supervises the second decoder level via an extra loss — this exploits the observation that the first resolution level dominates memory (memory ∝ P³C, doubling for gradient maps). Loss is weighted cross-entropy plus soft Dice L_dice = 1 - 2ΣLP/(ΣL+ΣP+ε) with per-class weights w_i = softmax(Σcount/count_i) for class imbalance. Stage 2 is a standard 3D U-net with "full-level post-concatenation": the stage-1 one-hot prediction (downsampled per level) is concatenated into the decoder at every level instead of being concatenated into the input image, preventing the encoder from over-relying on coarse predictions. Dataset: 30 fruitfly microCT volumes (1072x470x470 voxels, 2.95 µm³) with two imbalanced classes (mid-gut, small hind-gut). Results: meU-net halves memory vs U-net (13 GB vs est. 30 GB for 240³ patch, batch 4; 6 vs 10.5 GB for 160³), enabling larger patches that lift hind-gut Dice from 0.654 to 0.741; the cascade reaches 0.948/0.768 (mid/hind-gut) vs nnU-net's 0.931/0.659 at similar memory (15 GB, P100, training >8 days). Relevance: capture/representation stage — the two-patch gradient-disabled deep-supervision trick and decoder-side concatenation are directly transferable recipes for processing high-resolution volumetric human captures (voxel grids) on the cube's limited embedded GPU; domain (segmentation, not rendering) limits direct use, and results are offline training-time, not real-time inference.

### 2206.00299 — Restoring and tailoring  very high dimensional spatial entanglement of a biphoton state transmitted through a scattering medium

This quantum-optics paper (FEMTO-ST/Institut Langevin) restores and manipulates an ~8000-dimensional (Schmidt numbers K_x=86, K_y=92) momentum-entangled biphoton state after one photon of each pair traverses a thin ground-glass diffuser. Twin beams are generated by non-collinear type-II spontaneous parametric down-conversion in a 0.8 mm β-BBO crystal (355 nm pump, 330 ps pulses, 27 mW, 1 kHz) with signal/idler telescopes (magnification -1) imaging the near-field onto an SLM (Hamamatsu LCOS-SLM X10468-07) conjugated with the diffuser, then 2f far-field imaging onto two photon-counting EMCCDs (ANDOR iXon Ultra 897) with 710 nm/3 nm filters; ~0.15 photon/pixel, correlation peak σ ~0.8 mm^-1, ~23% pair-detection rate (>4000 pairs per acquisition). The transmission matrix (TM) of the medium is measured with a coherent alignment laser using phase-shifting interferometry (Popoff's method) between a 256x256-pixel SLM ROI (binned 4x4 into 64x64 macropixels) and a 100x100-pixel far-field ROI; phase-conjugation masks computed from the TM focus the laser through the diffuser to one or two target spots, and the same masks addressed on the SLM re-collapse the biphoton momentum correlation peak (from low-contrast two-photon speckle) at exactly the targeted locations — demonstrated for center, off-center (ν_x=+3 mm^-1) and dual-spot targets. Relevance: display stage, conceptually — this is the canonical wavefront-shaping result (SLM phase masks correcting scattering) that underlies real-time correction of a free-space optical engine's optics; but it is single-wavelength, requires a coherent source, a measured (time-stationary) transmission matrix, and kHz-scale SPDC rates, so for the telepresence cube it is background inspiration for SLM-based scattering compensation rather than a deployable technique.

### 2206.02221 — Ultrafast Transverse Modulation of Free Electrons by Interaction with Shaped Optical Fields

This paper (EPFL/CNR/Technion/ICFO/Milano-Bicocca, with HOLOEYE) demonstrates a Photonic free-ELectron Modulator (PELM) that imprints arbitrary transverse amplitude/phase patterns onto free-electron wavepackets by inelastic interaction with an SLM-shaped ultrafast light field — no electron-optics elements or nanostructures needed. A HOLOEYE PLUTO 2.1 reflective SLM (1920x1080, 8 µm pixels, ~800 nm-optimized) imprints phase patterns onto 600-fs, 1.57 eV IR pulses (1 MHz, peak field ~10^7 V/m) that are focused (25 cm lens) onto a tilted 30 nm Ag / 20 nm Si3N4 electron-transparent film inside a 200 keV ultrafast TEM (JEOL JEM-2100, LaB6 photoemission); electrons crossing the semi-infinite evanescent field (stimulated inverse transition radiation) are redistributed into energy sidebands ±ℓħω (measured by EELS, ~1.2 eV resolution, 15 eV gain-side window on a GIF+K2 detector). The coupling is β_PELM(x,y) = (e/ħω)∫dz′ E_z^SLM(x,y,z′)e^{-iωz′/v}, giving inelastic components ψ_ℓ(x,y) = ψ_0·J_ℓ(2|β|)·e^{iℓ·arg(-β)} (ℓ-th order Bessel functions). Phase patterns with a π shift in half the SLM produce HG10/HG01 Hermite-Gaussian light fields whose two-lobed distributions are directly imprinted on the electron pulse, matching theory (tilt of lobes from off-normal incidence, δ~4.5°); simulations (STEM-CELL) show such HG beams act as Hilbert-phase plates, enhancing zero-defocus contrast of a 50 nm Skyrmion array (1.13 µm field of view), with proposed extensions to electron single-pixel imaging and vortex (chiral dichroism) probes. Relevance: not directly applicable to an optical telepresence display — it modulates electrons, not photons — but it is the strongest demonstration that an external SLM + planar film can arbitrarily shape a wave function at femtosecond rates; for the cube it only informs (as analogy) SLM-driven structured wavefront generation, and the hardware (UTEM, vacuum, keV beams) is entirely out of scope.
---

### 2206.05433 — Gigahertz-rate random speckle projection for high-speed single-pixel image classification

Hanawa, Niiyama, Endo and Sunada (Kanazawa U.) experimentally demonstrate dynamic random speckle generation at multi-GHz rates for single-pixel (compressive) imaging/classification, replacing ~10 kHz SLM-based pattern projection. A narrow-linewidth 1550 nm tunable laser is phase-modulated by a 16 GHz-bandwidth LiNbO₃ modulator driven with a 25 GS/s pseudorandom sequence; the modulated light propagates through a 1–100 m step-index multimode fiber (200 µm core, NA 0.39, ~10⁴ guided modes at 1550 nm), where wavelength-sensitivity of modal dispersion converts fast phase modulation into rapidly decorrelating speckle patterns. Speckle contrast C = σ/Ī falls to 0.13 for 25 GS/s modulation at 100 m (≈ M ≈ 59 independent patterns), and scales with MMF length and modulation bandwidth. In the application, speckles illuminate a DMD-displayed 28×28 MNIST digit, and the single-pixel detector signal (6 ns acquisition, τ_s = 0.02 ns, N = 300 samples) is fed to a one-layer 100-unit ReLU network; experimental four-class accuracy is 92.6% vs 98.4% numerically with Gaussian random masks; the system runs at 1/T_N ≈ 167 Mfps, and with wavelength-division multiplexing (6+ wavelengths, 1547–1553 nm) classification accuracy reaches ~95–97% at up to ~1 Gfps acquisition rates. Dynamic scene switching is captured by correlation analysis. This is relevant to our capture stage: it is an all-optical, ultra-fast pattern-projection engine that could serve as a high-rate structured-light / compressive-capture front-end for the telepresence cube (the cube itself would need the MMF+modulator chain and a fast photodetector; environmental sensitivity of MMF speckles to thermal/vibration noise is a robustness caveat), and conceptually the "random speckle→single-pixel encoding" trick can also be inverted as a possible free-space emission/encoding mechanism. No licensing issues; hardware is commercial-off-the-shelf.

### 2206.07281 — Super-resolution image display using diffractive decoders

Işıl, Mengu, Zhao, Tabassum, Li, Luo, Jarrahi and Ozcan (UCLA) present a jointly-trained "hybrid autoencoder" display: a digital CNN encoder maps each high-resolution target image into a low-resolution (LR) phase-only pattern for a low-space-bandwidth-product SLM, and a passive all-optical diffractive decoder (L = 1, 3 or 5 transmissive layers of ~200×200 diffractive neurons, thickness h[m,n] = tanh(o[m,n])/2·(h_m−h_b)+h_b, transmittance t = exp(j2π(τ(λ)−n_a)h/λ), propagation modeled by FFT-based Rayleigh–Sommerfeld / angular-spectrum convolution) optically decodes that pattern into a super-resolved image at its output FOV — no electronics except the encoder, processing at the speed of light. Training loss is ℒ = (1/N)Σ|y_i − σŷ_i| + γe^(−η) (MAE plus an output-power-efficiency penalty; γ = 0.015/0.005 for the fabricated L = 3/1 THz decoders). Numerical results (EMNIST-letter display dataset, 60k train / 6k test, phase-only encoding, pixel 0.533λ): SR factor k = 4–8; the k = 8, L = 5 design resolves 2.132λ line pairs using a 4.264λ-pixel SLM, i.e. ~4× effective pixel super-resolution and ~16× SBP increase; deeper decoders always win; 8-bit and 6-bit phase quantization are robust (18.58/18.20 dB PSNR vs 18.61 dB at 16-bit), 2-bit fails. Experiments at THz (λ ≈ 0.75 mm, 3D-printed layers, vaccination-style random layer misalignment during training, diffraction efficiencies 2.39% and 3.29%): PSNR 13.134 dB (L = 3) and 12.151 dB (L = 1). This is directly useful for our display stage: it is the strongest candidate mechanism for the free-space optical engine — a tiny, low-power, passively super-resolving front-end that multiplies the SBP of whatever SLM the cube carries (holographic SLM architecture), and its LR-encoding property also lowers the data the SLM must receive (up to 16× fewer pixels), easing our sub-1Mbps transport. Caveats: proof-of-concept at THz, monochrome, single-wavelength, ~cm-scale layers, and training requires dataset-matched content; scaling to visible RGB needs nanofabrication (two-photon polymerization / lithography), so it is a long-horizon option, not today's component.

### 2206.07628 — Tunable wavefront control in the visible spectrum using low-loss chalcogenide phase change metasurfaces

Moitra, Wang, Liang, Lu, Poh, Mass, Simpson, Kuznetsov and Paniagua-Dominguez (A*STAR / SUTD) experimentally realize tunable all-dielectric Huygens' metasurfaces in antimony trisulfide (Sb₂S₃), a low-loss chalcogenide PCM with high refractive index (n_am ≈ 3–3.5 in the visible) and large amorphous↔crystalline index contrast Δn ≈ 0.5. Devices are 160 nm Sb₂S₃ hole-array nanoresonators (period 380 nm, hole diameter ~300 nm, gap 80 nm; PMMA/PDMS cladding n = 1.45) fabricated by e-beam lithography + Cl₂ dry etch; FDTD design puts the Huygens' condition (overlapping electric/magnetic dipoles, ~100% transmission, full 2π phase) at ~680–705 nm. Switching: furnace anneal to 320 °C (5 °C/min, Ar) crystallizes the film (resonance redshift ~60–100 nm, phase change > 1.5π at 680 nm); reversible laser re-amorphization (660 nm, 28.6 mW, 50 ns pulses) and re-crystallization (405 nm, 24.1 mW, 5 µs pulses) restore the spectrum; the bound-state-in-the-continuum resonance is reversibly tuned 710→780→690 nm. Demonstrations: a gradient supercell (8 holes, π/4 phase steps, 2π total) steers transmission into the +1 order at ~14° with ~17% measured efficiency in the amorphous state (≈10% after re-amorphization), vanishing upon crystallization; a phase-only 8-level CGH "hologram" (300×300 µm) reconstructs an image at 680 nm with efficiency dropping 2.5× when crystallized (partly amplitude effects). For our telepresence cube this is a candidate pixel/emitter technology for the display stage — non-volatile, zero-hold-power, visible-spectrum wavefront-shaping elements that could one day form a compact reconfigurable phase-only modulator — but current switching is far too slow for video-rate avatars (thermal/laser-pulse switching, large birefringent grains degrade response, true multi-level switching not yet shown), and nanofabrication is needed; treat as a forward-looking SLM-alternative datapoint, not an integration target. No licensing issues (academic paper).

### 2206.07859 — Phase-Only Holographic Assisted Planar Printing for Massively Multiplexed Optical Display and Encryption

Wei, Hu, Feng, Zhao and Yang (HUST/USC) introduce "Holo-Printing": a framework that fuses printings (surface intensity) and holograms (far-field diffraction) so that a single phase-only surface displays a different printed image along each observation angle, enabling massively multiplexed, dynamic planar displays and encryption. Theory: a periodic rectangular lattice of size Lx×Ly decomposes its wavefront into discrete plane-wave orders (m,n) with spatial frequencies f_m = m/Lx, f_n = n/Ly; by placing each desired printing in a distinct diffraction order and ensuring a minimum frequency gap of 2/Lx between non-zero orders (so |Ẽ₁|² main lobes do not overlap), every field is reconstructed with acceptable crosstalk, and channel count (M+1)×(N+1) grows with surface size. Each lattice is treated as an independent computer-generated hologram whose phase-only profile is computed with the Gerchberg–Saxton (GS) algorithm, plus an intensity-compensation step that deflects excess light into "unused" orders to preserve grayscale. Experiments with a phase-only SLM: 25 angle-multiplexed grayscale printings in a 5×5 order array (observed via a 4f system with circular aperture filtering), an 8-channel video mode (60 Holo-Printing frames played in ~6 s ≈ 10 fps per channel), an encryption demonstration (order/angle-based keys), and a 324-channel Holo-Printing on a bilayer gradient metasurface multiplexing 2 wavelengths (950/1550 nm) × 2 polarizations × 81 angles (648×1296 unit array, numerically decoded images). For our telepresence cube this informs the display stage: angle-multiplexed phase-only emission is a plausible way to serve multiple viewing directions from one SLM (directional light-field emission), and the GS-based phase computation is cheap; dynamic video-rate operation is demonstrated at ~10 fps with an SLM, and metasurface versions are static. Caveats: 4f filtering optics needed for clean observation, zeroth-order/direct-reflection contamination at (0,0), efficiency limited by phase-only modulation; no bandwidth/latency numbers reported.

### 2206.09155 — An iterative method based FLC-SLM system design for forming multiple complex structures simultaneously in 3D volume with tissue

Kumar, Thakur and Biswas (IISER Mohali) build a cost-effective wavefront-shaping system for forming multiple complex light structures simultaneously at different depths of a 3D volume through scattering media (ground-glass diffusers of 120/220/450/600 grit and fresh ex-vivo chicken tissue 323/588/852 µm thick), using a ferroelectric-liquid-crystal (FLC) binary-phase SLM (ForthDD SXGA-R5; 40 µs pixel switching, 4.5 kHz refresh, no phase calibration needed) with a He-Ne 633 nm source, 4F relay, and a dual-camera rig (one camera for feedback, one positioned at different depths for 3D verification). Algorithm: a weighted-mutation genetic algorithm (WMA-GA) — crossover with biased parent selection O = x·P_i + ¬x·P_j, Bernoulli-sampled weighted mutation O^w = B(O, W_m), XOR-flip mutation with exponentially decaying rate r_m (1%→0.5%, λ = 500), population 200, plus an R² fitness function R² = 1 − Σ(I_j−S_j)²/Σ(I_j−Ī)² that outperforms peak-to-background-ratio fitness for complex structures; simulation model: N = 40,000 input modes (200×200), M = 256 output modes, Gaussian transmission matrix, 30% added noise. Headline numbers: WMA-GA reaches a fitness of 1000 in ~75–600 iterations where standard GA reaches 542 after 700 (185% better enhancement experimentally, 746% in simulation); optimal weighting factor W ≈ 10.4% ± 5.8; with one optimized single phase mask, structures "A" and "O" are formed simultaneously 1.62 mm apart axially and 266 µm laterally through 565 µm chicken tissue; fluorescence (methylene blue, 680–710 nm) inside tissue is enhanced by shaping 633 nm excitation, with standard-GA comparisons showing tighter focus and background suppression. For our telepresence cube this is a display-stage datapoint: it demonstrates simultaneous multi-plane 3D structure formation from one phase-only mask — conceptually the same "one SLM pattern → several focal planes" trick an aerial volumetric/holographic engine needs — and validates cheap binary-phase SLMs plus iterative optimization as viable hardware; but it optimizes static masks over many iterations (not real-time video), targets tissue/scattering imaging, and reports no latency or bandwidth figures. Academic paper, no licensing concerns.

### 2206.10131 — An Integrated Representation & Compression Scheme Based on Convolutional Autoencoders with 4D DCT Perceptual Encoding for High Dynamic Range Light Fields

Khaidem and Sharma (IIT Madras) propose 4DDCT-UCS, an integrated representation-and-compression pipeline for high-dynamic-range light fields (HDR LF). Workflow: (1) Representation — a simulated coded-aperture camera (two optimized aperture transmittance masks r_n(s,t)) compresses the full LF into just N = 2 acquired images a_n(u,v) = Σ_{s,t} r_n(s,t)·l_{s,t}(u,v); a 2D-convolutional autoencoder (Chainer, trained on 51 LF datasets with square-loss objective argmin_{f,g} Σ|l_m(u,v) − l̂_m(u,v)|²) learns the mapping f: X→Y (25 sub-aperture views → 2 images) and the reconstruction g: Y→X̂. (2) Compression — the two acquired images are interpreted as a 4D volume, transformed with a separable 4D discrete cosine transform on 8×8×8×8 blocks (X_k = √(2/N)·c_k·Σ x_n cos((2n+1)kπ/2N)), uniformly quantized (X_q = round(X(k)/Q(k))), run-length encoded (AC) + DPCM (DC), Huffman entropy-coded, then mapped into the perceptually uniform IPT color-opponent space with a piecewise Perceptual Transfer Function (3 intensity ranges per REC 1886) on luma and a power-law scaling P_out = (P_in)^λ·(2^n−1) on chroma with λ optimized by an error-minimizing function argmin_λ (1/MN)Σ|⌊(P_in)^λ(2^n−1)⌋/(2^n−1) − P_in|; metadata LUTs are attached and the whole stream is re-encoded with HEVC Main10 (4:2:0, 10-bit) — supporting incremental streaming. Results on the super-dense HDR LF dataset "Super Dense Teddy" (50×50 angular, 5168×3448 spatial views; 64×64 blocks, 20 epochs): against JPEG-XL, BD-Rate savings of −86.2% to −96.9% and BD-PSNR gains up to 83.7 dB across distortion levels d = 1–5 (QP 2–42); total bytes are far lower because only 2 images traverse the codec; reconstruction from 2 acquired images is visually near-lossless. No fps/latency/Mbps figures are reported. For our cube: relevant to capture and transport — coded-aperture acquisition (2 images instead of 25 views) could shrink the sensor chain and input bitrate of a light-field-style avatar capture, and the 4D-DCT+perceptual entropy stage is a lightweight, hardware-friendly codec candidate; note the LF domain (static HDR scenes), no real-time validation, and JPEG-XL/HEVC patents apply to deployed standard codecs.

### 2606.10550 — LentiAvatar: Pseudo-Multiview Reconstruction and Subpixel Prism Rendering for Real-Time Stereoscopic Communication

LentiAvatar couples a monocular Gaussian head-avatar pipeline to a glasses-free lenticular display for real-time autostereoscopic video chat, and is directly relevant to TAYF as an end-to-end demonstration of driving a physical light-field panel from a live avatar. The avatar is a standard 3DGS head (covariance $\Sigma_j=R_jS_jS_j^\top R_j^\top$) deformed per-frame by tracked expression/jaw/eye/head-pose codes; the paper's actual contribution is "pseudo-multiview" (PMV) supervision that turns natural head turns in monocular video into side-view training signal, using yaw-binning ($\mathcal{B}=\{15°,20°,25°\}$, frames accepted only within $2.5°$ of a bin and $14°$-$27°$ magnitude), a frame-quality ranking score, camera/target alignment gated by IoU$\ge0.32$, and a "strict head-and-hair" matte that excludes unreliable neck/collar regions from the loss — this suppresses the floating-alpha, ghosting, and color-fringe artifacts that would otherwise appear when the same monocular avatar is rendered from many lateral angles simultaneously (outside-support opacity $O_\alpha$ drops from 0.0625 to 0.0607 vs. no-PMV baseline). At runtime it renders $N=32$ virtual views over $\pm25°$ at $960\times540$ and composes them into a $3840\times2160$ lenticular raster via a calibrated per-subpixel routing mask $E(p)=\sum_i M_i(p)V_i(u_p)$; on an RTX 4090 the 32-view render + subpixel compose costs only ~18ms total, but the live metrical face tracker dominates at 109ms/frame (10.65 FPS end-to-end) until distilled into a subject-specific MobileNetV3 driver that cuts tracking to 4.7ms and raises throughput to 38.49 FPS. For TAYF this is a capture/representation-to-display bridge rather than a free-space optics result — the display itself is a conventional lenticular panel, not a true aerial volume — but the pattern (drive a Gaussian avatar → render N angular views → route to physical viewing zones via a calibrated per-subpixel/per-channel mask) is exactly the computational structure TAYF would need for a directional light-field cube face, and the finding that avatar-quality-under-oblique-viewing (not raster throughput) is the bottleneck for lateral zones is a direct warning for any multi-view free-space engine.

### 2407.14053 — DirectL: Efficient Radiance Fields Rendering for 3D Light Field Displays

DirectL attacks the rendering-cost bottleneck of autostereoscopic light-field displays (LFDs) that require dozens of offset views per frame, and is a strong candidate for TAYF's display-driving pipeline if the optical engine ends up being a lenticular/parallax-barrier panel rather than true holography. It formalizes the interlacing math: for a lenticular grating with tilt $\alpha$, line count $L_x$, and offset $K_\text{offset}$, subpixel $(x,y,k)$ maps to a viewpoint index $v=\lfloor N_v\cdot(x_\text{offset}/L_x)\rfloor$ where $x_\text{offset}=(3y+3x\tan\alpha+k-K_\text{offset})\bmod L_x$; standard practice renders $N_v{=}45$-$96$ full-resolution views (e.g. their 65-inch 8K panel needs 96 views over an 80° FOV) and interlaces, wasting >90% of rendered pixels. DirectL instead performs "ray-order rendering": it precomputes, per display, exactly which world-space ray each output subpixel needs, casts NeRF/3DGS rays directly in that order (view-independent color/opacity per Eq. 4-10, standard $\alpha_i=1-e^{-\sigma_i\delta_i}$ compositing), and only re-orders the G/B channels post-hoc — combined with a "subpixel-repurposing" scheme that reuses otherwise-discarded idle subpixels within a local search window (Eq. 14) to cut required rendered pixels by ~55%. Net result: up to 40× speedup over the standard multi-view-then-interlace paradigm, reaching ≥25 FPS real-time on a 48-viewpoint 2K LFD (previously requiring full-frame multi-GPU rendering). This is a pure rendering-efficiency result with no new optics — the display panel itself (LCD + lenticular sheet) is off-the-shelf and not remotely 10cm-cube-compatible in this bulky consumer form — but the ray-order/subpixel-repurposing trick is directly transferable to any TAYF light-field tile that must synthesize its own multi-view raster from a Gaussian-splat avatar in real time on limited compute.

### 2512.20464 — Snapshot 3D image projection using a diffractive decoder

Işıl et al. (UCLA, Ozcan lab) build a genuinely free-space, screen-less volumetric display: a phase-only SLM encoder plus a cascade of passive, fixed diffractive phase layers (a "diffractive deep neural network" fabricated once and never reconfigured) that jointly route a single encoded wavefront to reconstruct different 2D images at multiple discrete depths in a single coherent-light shot — precisely TAYF's target output modality (an image forming in literal free space with no surface). The forward model is standard scalar diffraction: propagation between planes uses the angular-spectrum transfer function $H(f_x,f_y;z_d)=\exp[jkz_d\sqrt{1-(\lambda f_x)^2-(\lambda f_y)^2}]$ for $f_x^2+f_y^2<1/\lambda^2}$, and each diffractive layer is a thin phase mask $t(x,y)=e^{j\phi(x,y,z_k)}$ whose phase values are the trainable parameters, end-to-end optimized (JAX/Adam, lr $10^{-3}$–$10^{-4}$, 2000 epochs) jointly with a Fourier-feature digital encoder network that maps an $M$-slice volumetric target into one SLM phase pattern. Numerically, a 3-layer decoder resolves 4 axial planes spaced $\Delta z=3\lambda$ at 22.5dB PSNR / 0.92 PCC (vs. 19.8dB/0.79 for free-space propagation with no decoder), scales to 28 axial slices at $\Delta z=1\lambda$ separation, and exposes a clean brightness/crosstalk trade-off (diffraction efficiency 0.0005%–35%, with speckle and inter-plane leakage rising sharply above ~10%). They built a real two-SLM optical prototype (HOLOEYE PLUTO-2.1 encoder, HOLOEYE LUNA decoder, 650nm laser, 10cm decoder-to-encoder spacing) that reproduced 2-plane MNIST-digit projections matching simulation. For TAYF, the diffractive-decoder concept is highly relevant to a compact optical engine: it shows that a single coherent illumination + one thin phase-encoded plane + a handful of static diffractive layers can perform depth-selective free-space image routing entirely optically after training, potentially replacing the heavy iterative CGH optimization of conventional holography with pre-baked passive optics; the hard limits are that resolution/FOV are set by the tiny 32×32-pixel demonstrated content, everything is coherent-monochromatic (color needs separate wavelength-multiplexed optimization), speckle rises fast with brightness, and it is still a 2D-image-per-plane (not full angular light-field) architecture, so it addresses depth cues but not multi-directional viewing without further extension.

### 2211.02784 — Waveguide Holography: Towards True 3D Holographic Glasses

Jang et al. (Meta Reality Labs) combine a pupil-replicating waveguide combiner (used in AR glasses for étendue expansion) with a phase-only SLM to make a true 3D holographic near-eye display without any projection lens, by modeling and inverting the complicated coherent multi-path interference the waveguide normally treats as an artifact to avoid. Because the waveguide's in/out-coupler gratings create pupil-replicated copies of the wavefront that interfere and scramble phase/amplitude, the authors model the whole waveguide as an approximately linear-spatially-invariant (LSI) system and represent its propagation as a multi-channel complex-convolution: several kernels $h$ plus complex apertures $Q$ (in-coupler selector) and $R$ (out-coupler calibration), summed linearly across channels to also capture the system's spatial-variance. This forward model is calibrated end-to-end using a custom "wavefront camera" (Mach-Zehnder phase-shifting interferometer) that captures ~1000 ground-truth complex output wavefronts for a random-phase SLM input, minimizing $\mathcal{L}=\|u'(x,y)-u(x,y)\|^2$; once calibrated, CGH generation is standard gradient-descent on an L2 image loss with the trained model appended. Measured results: a 1.15mm-thick glass waveguide (50° center TIR angle, 532nm) delivering full per-pixel depth control from near to optical infinity, a 7×5° FOV on the benchtop rig (7.64° in one build) limited by SLM pixel pitch, a 7mm-square computationally steerable 3D eyebox (>40dB PSNR uniformity), and — notably — sub-arc-minute resolution with >3× Strehl-ratio improvement by using the model to cancel grating-boundary phase discontinuities that otherwise clip the numerical aperture. For TAYF this is squarely a "shrink the optical path" result: it demonstrates that a millimeter-thick passive waveguide plus SLM can replace bulky free-space holographic projection optics while still producing full-parallax, multi-depth 3D imagery, which is the kind of étendue-expansion/miniaturization trick a 10cm cube's optical engine would need — but it is a near-eye (through-waveguide, into-a-pupil) system, not a genuine free-space-emission display, so it is directly useful only if TAYF pivots toward a wearable/near-eye variant rather than literal open-air holography, and its FOV/eyebox are still SLM-pixel-pitch-limited (sub-micron pitch panels remain the "long-term solution" the authors call out as unsolved).

### 2303.11287 — Simultaneous Color Computer Generated Holography

This paper solves single-shot full-color holography from one SLM pattern (rather than sequential R/G/B frames), directly relevant to any TAYF holographic engine's temporal-bandwidth budget. The forward model is $I_{z,\lambda}=|f_\text{prop}(e^{i\phi_\lambda(s)},z,\lambda)|^2$ optimized by gradient descent over a combined RGB loss (Eq. loss_func_rgb). The key physics is a "color-depth ambiguity": under Fresnel propagation the kernel $H(z,\lambda)=\exp(i\pi\lambda z(f_x^2+f_y^2))$ makes wavelength and distance interchangeable, so a hologram formed in red at $z_0$ automatically reappears (as an unwanted "depth replica") in green at $z_0\lambda_g/\lambda_r$ and in blue at $z_0\lambda_b/\lambda_r$ when the same SLM pattern is illuminated by all three wavelengths simultaneously — this is exploited constructively for the target plane but corrupts other 3D content. They mitigate it by using an SLM with wavelength-dependent phase response (extended phase range, since LCoS phase delay $\phi_\lambda=2\pi d\,n(s,\lambda)/\lambda$ depends on wavelength via dispersion): a Holoeye Pluto-2.1 with $2.4\pi$(R)/$5.9\pi$(G)/$7.4\pi$(B) phase ranges substantially suppresses depth replicas versus a naive $2\pi$-only SLM, and they argue $\sim\!8\pi$ range at 6+ bits/$2\pi$ is the practical sweet spot. Because RGB simultaneous holography is over-determined (3× more target pixels than SLM DOF), they add a perceptual opponent-color loss (luminance $O_1$, chrominance $O_2,O_3$ per Eq.) with different Fourier-domain low-pass cutoffs for luminance (75% width, ~30 cyc/deg) vs. chrominance (45% width, ~18 cyc/deg) matched to human contrast sensitivity, yielding +6.4dB PSNR and +0.266 SSIM over a plain RGB loss on a 294-image test set; they validate with real captured 2D and 4-plane 3D focal-stack holograms (90–120mm range) using an SLM. For TAYF, this is a color/bandwidth optimization directly applicable to any coherent-illumination holographic engine: it shows a path to full-color 3D content from a single SLM frame (3× fewer frames than sequential color), which matters for a battery-constrained device needing high effective frame rate, though it requires an SLM with strong per-channel phase-range dispersion (a specific hardware spec) and still leaves the fundamental holographic FOV/étendue and speckle problems unsolved.

### 2305.05196 — Viewing-angle expansion of holographic image using enhanced-NA Fresnel hologram

Chae (ETRI) presents a purely computational technique for expanding the viewing angle of an SLM-based holographic display beyond the "native" diffraction angle set by pixel pitch — a core lever for TAYF since $\Omega=2\sin^{-1}(hNA)$ ties viewing angle directly to hologram numerical aperture, which for a fixed SLM is normally capped at $\theta=2\sin^{-1}(\lambda/2\Delta x)$ (e.g. 3.8° for 512×512 pixels at 8µm pitch). The method exploits deliberate undersampling: propagating the hologram field $g(x,y)$ at a distance shorter than the critical distance $z_c=N\Delta x^2/\lambda$ produces "enhanced-NA" aliased replica fringes that still encode a genuinely higher spatial-frequency content than the nominal SLM bandwidth would allow, because the sampled impulse-response and transfer-function kernels ($h(x,y)$, $H(f_x,f_y)$) replicate periodically in Fourier space (Eq. 10-11) while remaining unitary/invertible under the shearing action of Fresnel propagation on the Wigner distribution. By deliberately using the double-Fourier-transform algorithm and preserving (rather than cropping) these aliased high-order replicas — verified with a 3-fold angular-spectrum expansion at $z=z_c/3$ for a 532nm, 512×512, 8µm-pitch system with $z_c=61.6$mm — the achievable viewing angle scales up with the reconstruction distance $z$ (Eq. 5) rather than staying pinned to the SLM's native diffraction angle, at the cost of a shorter allowable image-object distance. For TAYF this is a computational (not hardware) lever for the display track: it means SLM angular field-of-view is not a hard physical ceiling but a distance/algorithm trade-off, which could let a compact 10cm optical path trade working distance for wider viewing angle without new optics — but it only reported numerical/optical verification at millimeter propagation distances and small (few-degree) baseline angles, so scaling this trade to a human-sized viewing cone at practical standoff distances remains undemonstrated and the paper does not address speckle, color, or dynamic (moving-object) content.

### 2309.10816 — Multisource Holography

Kuo et al. propose "multisource holography," a hardware-level speckle-suppression technique for holographic near-eye displays using two spatially-separated coherent illumination sources through two SLMs — directly relevant to TAYF's optical engine because speckle and eyebox non-uniformity are named failure modes for any coherent free-space display. With a single SLM, illuminating from $M$ incoherent off-axis point sources at phase slopes $\vec m_i$ (Eq. 4) does reduce speckle by incoherent intensity summation, but because an ideal SLM has "infinite memory effect" ($g_{z,m_i}(\vec x)=g_{z,0}(\vec x+\tfrac{\lambda z}{2\pi}\vec m_i)e^{j\vec x\cdot\vec m_i}$, Eq. memory_effect), each source's output is just a shifted copy of the on-axis field, making arbitrary-content generation an ill-posed non-negative deconvolution — i.e. a single SLM with multiple sources cannot independently control what each source displays. Their fix places a second SLM a distance $\Delta z$ downstream; once source separation exceeds $\Delta m\ge 2\pi p/(\lambda\Delta z)$ (Eq. memory_effect_condition, with $p$ the pixel pitch), the second modulator decorrelates each source's contribution, letting joint ADAM optimization of both SLM patterns (one phase-only, one amplitude-only, matching the DOF of a single complex SLM) produce genuinely independent, speckle-averaged copies. Simulated results (8µm-pitch SLMs, $\Delta z=2$mm, RGB at 640/520/450nm, 15-plane focal stacks): a $6\times6=36$-source grid gives 29.4dB PSNR, >10dB over a single-source baseline, while beyond ~36 sources contrast at 1 arcmin starts falling as the two SLMs run out of DOF to keep sources decorrelated (100-source case shows visible haze); it also beats 6-frame temporal multiplexing in PSNR/SSIM using only a single frame per color, and produces a near-uniform eyebox energy distribution versus the ~5-orders-of-magnitude central peak of smooth-phase single-source holograms (which causes floater artifacts and poor accommodation response per cited user studies). For TAYF, the two-SLM multisource architecture is a directly transferable despeckling/eyebox-uniformity technique for any SLM-based free-space or near-eye engine, at the cost of doubling optical path complexity (two synchronized SLMs plus a precisely-spaced multi-source illuminator) — a nontrivial addition to a 10cm form factor but far more compact than temporal multiplexing at equivalent quality, which instead needs high-speed (>kHz) SLMs and burns frame-rate budget the cube may need for motion tracking.

### 2310.04409 — Metal-Optic Nanophotonic Modulators in Standard CMOS Technology

This paper demonstrates active nanophotonic liquid-crystal modulators fabricated with zero design-rule changes inside a standard 65nm bulk-CMOS foundry process, by repurposing the back-end-of-line (BEOL) copper/aluminum interconnect metal layers as plasmonic nanorod resonators. Nanorod arrays (pitch $\Lambda=330$nm, width $w=230$nm, tuned via simulation to resonate at 700nm) are built by connecting metal layers M5-M7 through vias; the same metal wires simultaneously serve as mechanical LC alignment layers, the optical resonator, and interdigitated electrodes, letting drop-cast liquid crystal reorient under an applied field and shift the local refractive index sensed by the plasmon resonance. Because response speed for standard LC modulators is set by $\tau=(\gamma/\kappa)\cdot d^2/((\Delta U/U_{th})^2-1)$ (viscosity $\gamma$, elastic constant $\kappa$, cell thickness $d$) and commercial devices use micron-scale cells giving $\tau>1$ms, shrinking the effective cell thickness to the nanoscale via CMOS-resolution metal patterning is the core trick: measured rise time at $\Delta U=5$V is $\tau=27.5\pm3.3\mu$s (36±4 kHz modulation speed) and decay time $30.4\pm2.1\mu$s, roughly 100× faster than sub-kHz commercial high-speed LC modulators, with a fitted effective cell thickness $d_\text{eff}\approx55$nm. For TAYF this is a fabrication-pathway result rather than a display-physics result: it shows that fast (tens-of-kHz), electrically-addressed nanophotonic light modulators — the building block underlying SLMs, beam-steerers, and reconfigurable metasurfaces — could in principle be manufactured on cheap, mass-produced bulk-CMOS dies rather than exotic photonic foundries, which matters directly for the cost/integration/power constraints of a consumer 10cm cube; the caveats are that this is a single-pixel proof-of-concept device (not an SLM array), operates at one design wavelength (700nm) with narrow-band plasmonic resonance, and the ~36kHz speed, while fast for LC, is still far slower than the µs-to-ns switching a genuinely high-frame-rate volumetric/holographic display would ultimately want.

### 2403.15265 — Practical considerations for high-fidelity wavefront shaping experiments

This is a methods/troubleshooting paper (with accompanying OpenWFS Python toolkit) for wavefront-shaping — using an SLM to pre-distort an incident wavefront so that scattering/aberration inside a turbid medium is cancelled and light refocuses to a diffraction-limited spot — and it is relevant to TAYF less as a display technique and more as a rigorous error-budget framework transferable to any SLM-based CGH pipeline. The core theory: the "enhancement" $\eta=I_b/I_0$ (focus intensity relative to unshaped background) has closed-form ideal values of $\langle\eta\rangle=N$ for ideal complex modulation with $N$ independently-controlled SLM segments (Eq. enhancement), reducing to $\langle\eta\rangle=\frac{\pi}{4}(N-1)+1$ for realistic phase-only modulation (Eq. enhancement-phase) — i.e. phase-only SLMs intrinsically lose a factor of $\pi/4\approx0.79$ relative to full complex control. The paper decomposes real-world shortfalls into independently-measurable "fidelity" factors: finite-mode truncation, SLM phase-calibration error, SLM phase jitter (diagnosed by holding both interferometer arms fixed and watching residual noise at elevated frame rate), and signal-to-noise-driven fidelity $F_N=\text{SNR}/(1+\text{SNR})=P_\text{sig}/(P_\text{sig}+P_\text{noise})$ (Eq. fidelity_SNR), plus a fluorescence-specific photobleaching artifact that mimics a fidelity loss but is actually sample damage from repeated measurement. A representative demonstrated result achieved enhancement of ~16 against a theoretical ceiling of 100 ($N$=100 modes), with the individual fidelity terms (amplitude mismatch, noise, phase-calibration) diagnosing exactly where the gap came from. For TAYF, this paper's direct display relevance is limited (it targets scattering-media focusing, e.g. deep-tissue microscopy, not free-space image projection), but the enhancement/fidelity decomposition and the phase-only vs. complex-modulation $\pi/4$ penalty are exactly the kind of quantitative error budget any CGH-based TAYF optical engine would need to characterize SLM-limited image quality (phase calibration drift, finite pixel count, SNR) and to distinguish hardware miscalibration from fundamental physical limits — essentially a rigorous QA methodology to borrow rather than a display architecture to adopt.

### 2404.11846 — Directional intense terahertz radiation driven by abruptly autofocusing lasers in air

Working in a completely different spectral regime (THz generation, not visible display), this paper is included for its femtosecond-laser-plasma-in-air physics, which is the same underlying mechanism candidate laser-excited aerial volumetric voxel displays rely on. Two-color (fundamental + second-harmonic via a 100µm type-I BBO crystal) femtosecond pulses focused into air ionize a plasma filament that emits broadband THz via a photocurrent mechanism, but the emission normally has an unwanted conical angular profile with an on-axis intensity dip, caused by a relative phase "dephasing" between the two colors as they propagate through the filament; the authors show the true dephasing length is only ~0.6mm (not the ~20mm previously assumed) once the Gouy phase of a focused Gaussian beam is correctly included, which explains why the dip persists even for few-mm filaments. Their fix reshapes the input beam into an abruptly-autofocusing (AAF) profile using an SLM-imprinted cubic transverse phase $\psi_\text{SLM}(R)=-CR^3/w_\text{SLM}^3$ (modulation depth $C=2\pi$ to $16\pi$ in experiments); the AAF wavefront acts as a "multi-focal-length lens" that extends the effective two-color dephasing length ~5× to ~3mm, suppressing the conical dip and yielding coherent on-axis superposition of THz source elements — measured as elimination of the angular dip plus a 17-fold enhancement in on-axis THz directionality/intensity versus an ordinary Gaussian beam, generalizing across the 1200-1600nm OPA tuning range tested. For TAYF, the direct relevance is to Track A's laser-plasma-voxel branch: this demonstrates that SLM-shaped autofocusing beams give fine control over where and how strongly a femtosecond-laser-induced air-plasma filament couples energy on-axis, which is the same knob (beam-shaping to control filament location/intensity/directionality) that a compact fs-laser aerial-voxel display would need to draw controlled, spatially localized emission points in free air; the caveats are that this result is specifically about THz (not visible) emission directionality and does not address visible-light voxel brightness, safety, scan speed, or repetition rate — it is evidence for the *controllability* of air-plasma physics via wavefront shaping rather than a display result itself.

### 2301.00245 — Reprogrammable holograms from maskless surface photo-morphing

Reda, Salvatore, Astarita, Borbone & Oscurato (University of Naples Federico II / Politecnico di Milano) demonstrate an all-optical, single-step maskless lithography scheme that writes and erases diffractive kinoforms directly into the surface topography of an azobenzene-containing polymer film (azo-resist), with no photoresist development or etching step. A phase-only LCoS-SLM (Holoeye Pluto) projects a computer-generated grayscale intensity pattern I_W(x,y) through a 4f system and 50x/NA=0.55 objective onto the azo-resist; light-driven mass migration reshapes the surface relief as h(x,y,t) = ∇²[I_W(x,y)]·h0(t), with the modulation depth growing linearly, h0(t)=c·t, at an empirically measured inscription efficiency c = 10.5 ± 0.5 nm/s. The target kinoform phase φ(x,y) is computed by a Gerchberg-Saxton/mixed-region-amplitude-freedom (MRAF) algorithm from the desired far-field image via I_out = |FT[U_in·e^{iφ}]|², and the ideal 2π modulation depth h0 = λ/(n-1) is reached after an optimized exposure time (t_opt = 103±1 s for a single-plane pattern, 120±1 s for a multiplexed one), giving a measured diffraction efficiency η_+1 = 0.60 ± 0.02 and transmissivity 0.96. Because the azopolymer surface can be optically flattened and rewritten (illumination in the chromophore absorption band restores the pristine state), the authors exploit multiple write/erase/average cycles (N=10) to reduce speckle noise by 1/√N, and demonstrate ternary-encoded optical data storage/encryption (3,125 bytes per single exposure, splitting a secret message into "HELLO"/"WORLD" halves across successive reprogramming steps). For TAYF this is a display-stage curiosity rather than a real-time candidate: it is a genuinely rewritable, lossless (near-unity transmittance), sub-wavelength-depth flat hologram — attractive if the cube ever needed a physically reconfigurable diffractive element fabricated in-field — but the ~100-120 second write/erase cycle per frame is roughly 9-10 orders of magnitude too slow for a 30-60 Hz human telepresence stream; it is only relevant as a possible enrollment-time or firmware-calibration optical element, not as the dynamic hologram engine itself.

### 2411.19445 — Achromatic single-layer hologram

Li, Zhou, Yuan, Cai, Teng, Song & Duan (Hunan University / Sun Yat-sen / Westlake / Shanghai Jiao Tong) tackle the standard CGH limitation that a phase-only diffractive optical element (DOE) is normally monochromatic, by designing a single-layer, single-wavelength-fabricated DOE that simultaneously encodes red/green/blue channel information with low crosstalk. Their phase-retrieval pipeline is built on the spatial-frequency-tunable method (SFTM), a decomposition of the Fresnel propagation ABCD matrix T = [[1,λz],[0,1]] into scaling, quadratic-phase (chirp) and Fourier-transform operators (Eq. 1) that lets the diffraction-image scale factor m and distance z be chosen independently per wavelength while auto-correcting chromatic magnification error — the key trick that removes the usual λ-dependent image-size mismatch across color channels. Crosstalk between the 3×3 red/green/blue superposition terms is suppressed by imposing a diffuse-scattering phase constraint (phase fluctuation range ±nπ, n≈0-0.2) on a signal window (SW) larger than the target image, plus a soft-quantization/energy-scaling constraint (relaxation parameter τ=0.6) iterated via a modified IFTA (Eqs. 3-6). Demonstrated results: independent color-channel separation of magnified letters at z=10/15/22 cm; a full-color 2.0x cube and 1.8x white cat holographically reconstructed from a single hologram; a 60 Hz full-color holographic video (3x the ~20 Hz effective frame rate of conventional time-multiplexed RGB SLM holography) — the authors state this is the first full-refresh-rate full-color holographic video from a single hologram layer; and a fabricated two-level (binary), twin-image-free, polarization-insensitive security DOE (5000×5000 pixels, 2 µm pitch, 10×10 mm, K9 glass/SPR955 photoresist) achieving ~30% diffraction efficiency versus a 40.5% theoretical binary maximum. For TAYF's optical engine this single-layer full-color, twin-image-suppressed CGH approach is directly relevant to the SLM-based holographic-display branch: it triples effective full-color refresh rate versus conventional RGB time-division holography (important since SLM refresh is a hard bottleneck for any tiny holographic engine) and shows binary/two-level fabrication is viable at low diffraction efficiency; its limits are that it is still a flat, single-depth-plane image at fixed magnification per design (not dynamically reconfigurable per frame beyond the phase pattern the SLM itself displays) and the ~30% efficiency binary version would need real SLM hardware (not a fixed DOE) to be useful for a live avatar.

### 2601.01221 — Metasurface-based Terahertz Three-dimensional Holography Enabled by Physics-Informed Neural Network

Shao, Tang, Xu, Zhao, Tian, Liu & Wu (Shanghai Jiao Tong) replace the classical Gerchberg-Saxton (GS) iterative hologram-design loop with a physics-informed neural network (LM-PINN) for designing THz metasurface holograms operating at λ=116 μm (f=2.58 THz) using a 64×64 array of silicon-pillar meta-atoms (period 58 μm, height fixed 60 μm, width/length independently tunable 12-50/14-50 μm). The core trick is a differentiable "local polynomial fitting" surrogate that maps meta-atom geometry (w,l) to its complex transmission/phase response via a 5th-order local polynomial (Eq. 1, NMSE 2.77e-6 for transmission, 2.17e-6 for phase vs. 1.53e-2/1.34e-2 for a naive global fit), replacing full-wave FDTD inside the training loop; this is combined with a differentiable angular-spectrum-method (ASM) propagator (Eqs. 8-9) so the whole target-to-geometry pipeline is trained self-supervised (no paired geometry labels) end-to-end with a Y-shaped CNN outputting the w/l maps directly, driven by a shape+light-efficiency loss (Eqs. 2-7). Against GS (3000 iterations) on MNIST-digit targets, LM-PINN wins decisively in FDTD-validated simulation (single-plane: NPCC 0.971/0.964 vs. 0.778/0.804, SSIM 0.53/0.63 vs. 0.28/0.32, PSNR 17.9/19.6 dB vs. 10.8/13.2 dB) and experimentally with a fabricated silicon metasurface under a THz quantum-cascade-laser (NPCC 0.94-0.95, PSNR 15-18 dB); a "distance-encoded" variant (Dist-LM-PINN) generalizes zero-shot to arbitrary target distances (3-20 mm), multi-focal metalenses, and full 3D holograms (a 15-plane aircraft model) without retraining, and inference is ~1 s on CPU / 0.5 s on GPU for a 512×512 metasurface versus 3.4-44 s for GS. For TAYF this is a compute-side enabling result for any metasurface/SLM-holography optical branch: it shows a trained network can replace iterative per-frame hologram optimization with a sub-second forward pass, which is the kind of speedup a battery-constrained cube would need to hit real-time hologram generation — but it operates in the THz band (116 μm wavelength, not visible light), on a static fabricated silicon metasurface rather than a dynamically reconfigurable device, so it is a design-methodology proof-of-concept for fast hologram computation rather than a deployable visible-light real-time display component.

### 2210.06941 — Generation of superhelical time-varying OAM beam with space-time-coding digital metasurface

Zhang, Li, Wong & Li (HKUST / CityU Hong Kong) experimentally generate orbital-angular-momentum (OAM) beams whose topological charge l(t) sweeps continuously in time using a space-time-coding digital reflective metasurface at 11 GHz (microwave, not optical). The metasurface's reflection coefficient is r(θ,t) = A·exp[i·N(θ+w·2π t/T)·t/T] (Eq. 1), which for a monochromatic excitation produces a reflected OAM beam with time-varying charge l(t)=Nt/T and an added nonlinear-in-time term the authors call "superhelicity" (winding number w) — a higher-order twist of the wavefront beyond simple linear OAM ramping. The physical device is a 20×20 = 400-element array of varactor-diode unit cells (10×10 mm, ~λ/3) driven by an FPGA through 8 azimuthal groups implementing 3-bit (8-state, 45° steps) discrete phase coding; a demonstrated configuration sweeps l from -4 to +4 with winding number w=-1 over a 1 ms period. Because standard single-probe near-field scanning cannot time-align measurements taken at different scan positions within one modulation cycle, the authors introduce a two-probe (signal + fixed reference) technique to reconstruct the dynamic amplitude/phase field over a 0.6×0.6 m, 45×45-pixel scan area, then compute the OAM spectrum A_l(t) via azimuthal Fourier decomposition (Eq. 5) to confirm mode purity and the predicted superhelical phase evolution (Eq. 6, fit constant γ≈0.8). This is only tangentially relevant to TAYF: it is a microwave-band, meter-scale beam-shaping and free-space-communication result (motivated by OAM multiplexing and particle manipulation, not image display), but the underlying space-time-coding digital-metasurface hardware — discrete varactor-controlled phase states driven by FPGA to synthesize a dynamically time-varying wavefront — is conceptually the same building block a reconfigurable beam-steering or holographic-projection element in a cube would need; the 1 kHz modulation rate achieved here is far too slow for real-time frame-rate holography, illustrating that even microwave time-modulated metasurfaces are still switching orders of magnitude slower than the tens-of-hertz a live human video stream requires, let alone visible-light equivalents.

### 2211.08296 — Deep-Learning-Empowered Inverse Design for Freeform Reconfigurable Metasurfaces
