Liu, Yang, Li & Xu (Tsinghua) present a CNN+genetic-algorithm inverse-design pipeline for freeform 1-bit reconfigurable coding metasurfaces in the microwave band (5.8 GHz), whose key contribution is a "decoupling" trick that halves the training-data burden for reconfigurable (not just static) meta-atoms: modeling a PIN-diode-loaded meta-atom as a two-port microwave network (Eq. 1-4) shows that under a lossless-structure assumption the free-space reflection coefficient Γ1 depends on the static structure only through its S22 scattering parameter, so a single full-wave simulation with a lumped port placed at the switch location yields both ON/OFF states' response, rather than requiring two separate simulations. A 73-layer ResNet (trained on 110,000 HFSS-simulated 16×16-pixel patterns, MAE loss, converging after 152 epochs/13 hours on a V100) predicts the S22 spectrum from pixel geometry in parallel (1000 patterns in 0.25 s), and a genetic algorithm (population 1000, fitness=1/MAE, 300 generations, ~70 s) searches pixel patterns to hit a pre-computed target S22^r derived analytically from the PIN-diode's RLC-equivalent switch parameters (Eq. 5). Demonstrated on-demand designs include wideband (5.35-6.41 GHz at 180°±45° phase difference), single-frequency, and multi-frequency 1-bit coding metasurfaces; a fabricated 16×16-element (412.8×412.8 mm) wideband prototype achieved a measured 20.8% fractional bandwidth (5.4-6.65 GHz) for beam steering up to 60°. For TAYF, this is a design-automation tool for beam-steering reconfigurable-metasurface hardware, not itself an optical-band or display-ready device — it operates at microwave frequencies with centimeter-scale unit cells (25.8 mm pitch) and second-scale (not sub-millisecond) inverse-design turnaround, so its direct applicability is limited to informing how a future optical-band reconfigurable holographic metasurface's meta-atom library might be inverse-designed rather than to any near-term cube-scale prototype; the relevance to TAYF's hardest open question (free-space optical emission) is indirect, via methodology transfer rather than device transfer.

### 2512.12625 — Deep-learning-enabled inverse design of large-scale metasurfaces with full-wave accuracy

Xu, Shao, Zhao, Xu, Tian, Chen, Sun, Lin, Bao, Mai & Wu (Shanghai Jiao Tong / RMIT / Shanghai Institute of Microsystem) address the scale bottleneck in metasurface inverse design: full-wave FDTD of a real, aperiodic (non-locally-periodic) metasurface is intractable beyond micrometer scale (a 50 μm² patch at 5 nm mesh needs ~100 hours/100 GB), so essentially all prior deep-learning inverse-design work has been capped at ~100×100 meta-atoms. The authors first quantify mutual-coupling range for TiO2 nanofin (Pancharatnam-Berry phase, 532 nm, LCP) and nanopillar (propagation phase, 500 nm) meta-atom libraries: local-response mean-squared error between an N-layer and (N-1)-layer neighborhood model drops to <5% of its peak by N=5 (Eq. 1-2, Fig. 2c-d), fixing a 5-layer (11×11) local neighborhood as the accuracy/cost sweet spot. A lightweight 2-layer MLP (256/512 neurons) then predicts the real/imaginary near-field response of a target meta-atom from its own and its 5-layer neighbors' geometry (a flattened 121×1 input vector), achieving full-wave-level accuracy (MSE ~3×10⁻² test loss) at a 5,000x FDTD speedup and only 8 GB memory; because response is purely local, an arbitrary n×n metasurface decomposes into n² overlapping sub-models, letting the pretrained MLP predict full-metasurface near-fields up to 25,000×25,000 elements (>20,000λ, centimeter scale) in 8-10 hours, with <3% EN-MSE versus full-wave truth. A U-Net inverse-design module (trained purely on synthetic (R,S) pairs generated by the frozen forward MLP, bypassing FDTD entirely for training-data generation) then maps target near-field responses directly to metasurface geometry in one forward pass (43-45 s for a 1000×1000 array, no iteration), fabricated and validated as a 12-focus multi-foci metalens and "SJ"/"TU" holographic-projection metasurfaces (measured PSNR 24-28 dB, SSIM 0.81-0.82 against target). This is the most directly enabling OPTICS paper in this batch for TAYF's hardest constraint: it demonstrates that centimeter-scale (i.e., roughly cube-face-sized), full-wave-accurate, aperiodic metasurface holograms can be inverse-designed and computed in seconds-to-hours rather than the days-to-weeks FDTD would require, which is the computational prerequisite for ever treating a metasurface as a per-frame-programmable holographic element instead of a fixed fabricated pattern — but the demonstrated devices remain single, statically fabricated holograms (not dynamically switchable per video frame), so bridging this inverse-design speed to actual real-time reconfigurability still requires marrying it with a fast electro-optic or phase-change tuning mechanism (see 2510.00950 in this batch) rather than lithographic re-fabrication.

### 2510.00950 — Analysis and Design of a Reconfigurable Metasurface based on Chalcogenide Phase-Change Material for Operation in the Near and Mid Infrared

Pitilakis, Katsios & Boulogeorgos (University of Western Macedonia / Aristotle University of Thessaloniki) design a reflective, thermally-reconfigurable metasurface unit cell using Ge2Sb2Te5 (GST) phase-change material sandwiched between two SiO2 layers under a square metallic patch on a ground plane (total multilayer thickness 90 nm: 30 nm oxide/30 nm GST/30 nm oxide, cell width w_c=200 nm, patch width 0.9w_c). GST toggles between an amorphous (quasi-transparent) and crystalline (high-index, lossier) state under thermal activation, giving a near-unity refractive-index contrast; the paper computes the full complex dispersion ε_r(ω)=ε1-jε2 across visible-to-MIR using a combined Drude/Tauc-Lorentz model fitted to published GST ellipsometry data, Kramers-Kronig-derived ε1, and a Lorentz-Lorenz-formula interpolation across eleven intermediate (partial-crystallization) states between the two extremes — meaning the device is not just binary but continuously/multi-bit tunable in principle. Reflection spectra are computed analytically via transmission-line/ABCD-matrix equivalent-circuit modeling (validated against CST full-wave simulation) and optimized to hit >180° reflection-phase coverage with reflection magnitude better than -6 dB at λ0=1550 nm for normal incidence; oblique-incidence performance degrades but a 180° phase span at -6 dB is still achievable at 45° TE incidence for a 100%/60% crystallization-ratio pair. A back-of-envelope thermal-crosstalk estimate (lumped thermal circuit) suggests <10% parasitic temperature rise in unheated neighboring cells given the sub-micron cell footprint and the ground-plane heat sink, though the authors flag that full multiphysics simulation is still needed. For TAYF this is a genuinely relevant near-infrared (1550 nm — adaptable in principle toward visible) reconfigurable phase-shifting metasurface unit, directly usable as a wavefront-shaping/beam-steering pixel for a holographic optical engine at sub-micron pitch; its critical caveat for a live human telepresence use case is speed — GST phase transitions are thermally driven (heating/cooling, not electronic), with the paper only speculating about "future work on thermoelectric reconfiguration speed" rather than reporting measured switching times, and PCM devices in the broader literature typically switch on microsecond-to-millisecond timescales at best (often much slower with thermal crosstalk/fatigue concerns over repeated cycling), which would need to be several orders of magnitude faster and validated for millions of reconfiguration cycles before it could drive a 30-60 Hz per-frame holographic avatar rather than a slowly-reprogrammed static or quasi-static beam pattern.

### 2301.00593 — Reconfigurable Metasurface: A Systematic Categorization and Recent Advances

Liu, Yang, Xu & Li (Tsinghua) present a review/taxonomy of reconfigurable metasurfaces (R-MTS) built around a single unifying "information allocation strategy": since a plane EM wave E(t,r) = E0·exp[j(ωt - k·r + φ)]|p⟩ has exactly five independently modulatable dimensions — phase φ, amplitude E0, polarization |p⟩, direction k, and frequency ω — and the element-level modulation matrix M_i(t,r,R_i) = A_i·F_i·exp[j(Δω_i t+Δφ_i)]·J_i (Eq. 5) covers all five, any reconfigurable element can be classified by which dimension(s) its digital control bits are allocated to. The paper systematically catalogs 1-bit elements (single switch, two states, one of five dimensions: phase/amplitude/polarization/direction/frequency-only, with worked Jones-matrix derivations for polarization switching combinations, e.g. 1-bit polarization gives LP(x)↔LP(y), LP↔CP, or RHCP↔LHCP depending on the fixed/switched phase pair) and 2-bit elements, splitting the latter into "2-bit-manipulating" (both bits control one element actively across up to two dimensions, C(5,2)+5=15 combinatorial types) and "multiplexed-manipulating" (one bit actively controls one dimension while the other bit distinguishes response to two different incident waves, 5×5=25 theoretical combinations) categories, reporting phase-quantization losses of 3-3.9 dB for 1-bit and 0.6-0.9 dB for 2-bit phase coding. It also reviews time-modulated/space-time-coding R-MTS via Fourier-series harmonic generation (Eq. "M(t)=Σa_h·exp(j2πht/T)", noting the fundamental speed bottleneck: harmonic shift is capped by switch/FPGA switching speed, 1/T ≪ f_c) and surveys the state of THz/optical-band switches (Schottky diode, HEMT, graphene, CMOS, VO2, ITO, GST, liquid crystal, PEDOT:PSS), explicitly noting that in these bands "no lumped switch is available" and the meta-atom itself must double as switch, with GST femtosecond-pulse amorphous-crystalline transitions cited as one such optical-band mechanism. For TAYF this survey is a useful conceptual map rather than a device: its core lesson is that a reconfigurable free-space optical engine is fundamentally a bit-budget allocation problem across five wave dimensions, and its explicit statement that THz/optical R-MTS still faces "improving efficiency, increasing switching speed, eliminating grating lobes and addressing independently" as open challenges (echoing the switching-speed caveat found for GST in 2510.00950 above) is the clearest one-sentence confirmation in this batch that no current reconfigurable-metasurface technology yet reaches the switching speed needed for real-time free-space video-rate holographic human reconstruction — the gap is squarely in the "optical/THz reconfigurable element" row of their own taxonomy table, which the field has not yet filled at millisecond-or-faster speeds.

### 2601.00630 — Video-rate holographic telepresence via single-shot, reference-free wavefront measurement

KAIST/Tomocube group demonstrate a working measured (not rendered) holographic telepresence link: a coherent 532nm source illuminates an object, a pre-characterized geometric-phase diffuser turns the incident wavefront into an intensity-only speckle pattern (no interferometric reference beam needed), and the complex field is recovered via a speckle-correlation scattering-matrix (SSM) power-iteration algorithm (correlation matrix Z_pq built from intensity fluctuations, Eq. 4-9) followed by Smoothed Amplitude Flow + Nesterov-accelerated-gradient refinement (Eq. 11, Algorithm 2) for temporal stability. The recovered phase is projected on a phase-only SLM (Holoeye LC2012, 36um pitch) with a rotating diffuser for speckle suppression, producing volumetric refocusing of real objects (three dice at z=0mm and z=250mm) that numerically and optically match reference photos. Critically they report a full closed-loop system: 28 fps sustained throughput, 1.24s end-to-end latency (compute-bound, not network-bound — reconstruction runs on 4x RTX A6000 GPUs over a 1Gbps LAN, ~4MB/frame, 896Mbps), and they explicitly show that naive Wirtinger-Flow reconstruction flickers frame-to-frame under motion while their SAF+NAG solver stays stable — i.e., temporal consistency, not per-frame fidelity, is identified as the actual bottleneck for holographic telepresence. Relevance to TAYF: this is the strongest "real-time CGH" data point found — actual video-rate (28fps) wavefront-based free-space SLM replay of a real, moving object, which is faster than the 57s/frame and ~1.5s/frame numbers already cited in `hardware/optical-engine.md`. But it does not close the gap: the scene is three dice over a small depth range with a full optical bench (multiple relay lenses f=200-400mm, external diffuser, external camera on a translation stage) — nothing about this is 10cm-cube-compatical; monochromatic only; the authors themselves flag narrow numerical aperture constraining resolution/viewing angle and residual speckle; and reconstruction depends on 4 datacenter GPUs, the opposite of "compact." It demonstrates that video-rate SLM holographic replay of real (not purely synthetic) 3D scenes is achievable today, which is a genuine data point worth citing, but it says nothing about scaling to face-level complexity, color, wide FOV, or the compute/power budget a 10cm sealed cube could sustain.

### 2306.12031 — Large-volume focus control at 10 MHz refresh rate via fast line-scanning amplitude-encoded scattering-assisted holography

Hokkaido/Okayama/KAIST collaboration proposes "FLASH focusing": a resonant scanner sweeps a line beam across the columns of a 2D DMD, converting spatial degrees of freedom (DOFspatial = N x Mcol pixels) into temporal degrees of freedom via the tradeoff DOFspatial x kappa = N x M (Eq. 1-2), so a slow (~24kHz) DMD is turned into an effectively ultrafast 1D wavefront modulator. Combined with a scattering medium (opal diffuser) as a fixed holographic focusing element and a pre-measured transmission matrix (binarized amplitude phase-conjugation, Fig. 2g), they achieve random-access holographic focus control of micron-sized foci at up to 31 MHz refresh rate (measured on/off switching of 32.5ns, Fig. 3e-f) over a large addressable 3D volume (5mm x 5mm x 5.5mm, tuning power ~100,000 m^-1, NA 0.21-0.8 across z=0.01-10mm). They demonstrate genuinely simultaneous multi-spot addressing (17 spots captured across two axially separated planes within a 1us camera exposure; two spots frequency-multiplexed at 4 and 8MHz through separate fibers) and extrapolate that a faster polygon-scanner variant could reach ~120MHz modulation speed. Relevance to TAYF: this is a real, measured "how to scale point-generation rate" trick — trading spatial for temporal DOF gives 1-2 orders of magnitude higher point-addressing rate than existing SLM/varifocal approaches, directly on-topic for Branch A's repetition-rate research variable. However it does not close the gap: it addresses single or a handful of diffraction-limited foci behind a scattering medium via holographic phase conjugation (a focusing method, not an image/voxel-array generator per se), energy efficiency is only ~10^-5 (they note this needs ~1-2 orders of magnitude improvement plus gain-medium amplification), the addressable volume (mm-scale) is far smaller than a human feature, and — critically — it is not a free-space-emission mechanism at all: it requires a fixed scattering medium in the beam path to do the holographic focusing, so it is not directly a laser-plasma voxel generator despite superficial resemblance ("fast 3D point addressing"). At best this is a candidate scanning/addressing sub-technique that a future laser-plasma engine could borrow to hit MHz-class point-addressing rates instead of relying on mechanical galvo scanning, but the paper itself does not touch plasma ionization, free space, or eye safety, and the demonstrated volume/energy numbers are far from a photoreal face.

### 2501.10198 — Time-Resolved Measurements of Cumulative Effects in Gas Dynamics Induced by High-Repetition-Rate Femtosecond Laser Filamentation

Bochum group measures, via pump-probe interferometry (folded-wavefront interferometer, Fourier-transform fringe analysis) and a capacitive plasma probe for filament length, how femtosecond laser filamentation in air behaves at repetition rates from 1kHz to 100kHz (1.03um, 273uJ pulses, 50fs, 3.8GW peak power). Key finding: below ~10kHz, the heat deposited by each filament fully diffuses away before the next pulse arrives (air density recovers to 99.9% within 1us of the next pulse at 1kHz), so each pulse ionizes fresh ambient air; above ~10kHz, diffusion cannot keep up and a permanent, stationary density-depletion "well" forms (at 100kHz, density stays depleted to ~92% between pulses rather than recovering), meaning every subsequent pulse now ionizes air that is already thermally and density-perturbed by prior pulses, changing the local nonlinear refractive index and thus the filamentation/ionization dynamics itself. Relevance to TAYF: this is a genuine, measured physical constraint directly on Branch A's "how does voxel generation rate scale with repetition rate" question. JSID 2025's baseline (~10k voxels/s) sits right at this crossover; the finding implies that naively cranking repetition rate to push voxel/s counts into the 10^5-10^6 range the north-star track wants will run into cumulative gas-dynamics effects that alter the propagation medium pulse-to-pulse — a real physics reason (not just laser-power/eye-safety) why simple rate-scaling of laser-plasma voxel generation may not be linear, and a concrete new research variable ("does cumulative air-density depletion destabilize voxel position/brightness at high rep rate") worth adding to `experiments/voxel-display/README.md`'s question list. It does not offer a fix or a positive scaling result — it's a caution, not a narrowing of the gap, and the paper doesn't discuss displays, voxels, or imaging at all (it's a pure plasma-physics/THz-source characterization paper); the connection to TAYF's voxel-rate scaling question is inferential, not stated by the authors.

(Note: several other plasma/filamentation-tagged candidates were checked and found off-topic for TAYF despite surface keyword overlap — 2603.17164 "Experimental Scaling of Diffraction Efficiency in Laser-Induced Plasma Gratings" (Princeton/Stanford/LLNL) is about plasma diffraction gratings for controlling multi-petawatt laser beams in particle-accelerator/fusion contexts, not display; 2603.23285 "Optical modelling of shaped laser pulses in plasma" is a laser-wakefield-acceleration simulation toolkit paper; 2408.09052 "Ambient air plasma acceleration in tightly-focused ultrashort infrared laser beams" is MeV electron acceleration via ponderomotive force; 2301.04458 is THz generation from plasma inside dielectrics. None of these concern free-space voxel emission, aerial imaging, or displays — they were excluded from the writeup after reading abstracts/intros because the "plasma" keyword match was coincidental to a different application domain (particle physics / THz sources), not because they were skipped.)

### 2501.06102 — Gigahertz directional light modulation with electro-optic metasurfaces

Texas A&M group builds a plasmonic-organic hybrid (POH) metasurface: a corrugated metallic slot-waveguide array engineered into a plasmonic quasi-bound-state-in-the-continuum (quasi-BIC) resonance, infiltrated with the organic electro-optic chromophore JRD1. Applying a field shifts the chromophore's refractive index and thus the resonance, giving a measured tunability of 0.4nm/V and, critically, a genuinely fast electro-optic response: 3.6GHz 3dB bandwidth (vs. 3.9GHz pure electrical S-parameter bandwidth), with clean eye diagrams at 3.5 and 4Gb/s digital modulation (Fig. 5). They demonstrate real 3-way beam steering (0th/+1st/-1st diffraction order) by driving a phased array of these resonators with independently optimized per-electrode voltages (3:1 side-lobe suppression, matching a coupled-mode-theory model). Relevance to TAYF: this is the fastest genuinely electrically-tunable metasurface found in this pass, directly answering priority #3's ask for something beyond thermal/laser-pulse switching — GHz-class, not the ms-to-second switching of phase-change or liquid-crystal metasurfaces already catalogued as too slow. However it does not solve video-rate wide-FOV image projection: it steers a beam among only 3 discrete diffraction orders (not a full 2D addressable image), the demonstrated device is 50um and NIR (1510nm test wavelength), and the authors' own claim about scaling to visible-light displays is explicitly stated as future work contingent on separate advances in visible-wavelength EO polymers (ref. 49) — not demonstrated here. Real, useful data point for "electrically fast metasurfaces exist" but not itself a display engine.

### 2303.14066 — Electrically Tunable Reflective Metasurfaces with Continuous and Full Phase Modulation for High-efficiency Wavefront Control at Visible Frequencies

A*STAR (Singapore) group builds a genuinely different kind of tunable metasurface SLM: an all-dielectric TiO2 disk-resonator metasurface over a metal reflector, with an ultra-thin (500-830nm) liquid-crystal layer as the tuning medium and 96 individually electrically-addressable 1um-pitch Al pixel electrodes. Because the LC layer is so thin, phase modulation comes from LC-induced shifting of the metasurface's own resonance (not propagation-phase accumulation in the LC itself), giving a full, continuous ~1.72pi phase sweep at visible wavelength (650nm) with under 3V and >50% reflectance (Fig. 3). They demonstrate real, working dynamic beam steering by programming supercells of 3-12 pixels with optimized voltage profiles (DIRECT-L + COBYLA optimization), achieving up to ~22 degree FOV and up to ~50% diffraction efficiency into the desired order (Fig. 4), reconfigurable in either direction. Relevance to TAYF: this is a strong candidate for the "electrically tunable, visible-wavelength, full 2pi phase, small-pixel SLM" gap — it is a real, fabricated, individually-addressable device, not a simulation, and reports pixel-addressing tested at 10kHz square-wave drive, comfortably above video-rate (24-60Hz) requirements even though the paper does not report a full LC-switching-speed benchmark (LC response is typically ms-scale, so this should be read as "fast enough for video rate," not GHz-fast). It falls short of closing the gap because it is a lab-bench 96-pixel linear array (not a 2D megapixel array), a discrete beam-steering demonstration rather than an image/hologram projector, and there is no discussion of how this scales to millions of pixels, color, or a 10cm free-space form factor — but among the metasurface candidates surveyed, it is the one that most directly satisfies "electrically tunable and fast enough for video rate" rather than either too-slow (thermal/phase-change) or too-narrow (GHz beam steering, few channels).

### 2511.03860 — Ultrafast, reconfigurable all-optical beam steering and spatial light modulation

Caltech (Atwater group) demonstrates the fastest wavefront reconfiguration speed found in this entire search: a high-Q (Q=370) amorphous-silicon Mie-resonant metasurface modulated all-optically via the instantaneous optical Kerr effect (n2=0.0002 cm^2/GW) plus a slower two-photon-absorption free-carrier background, achieving pulse-limited beam steering up to +-13 degrees with a measured 74-femtosecond response time (near-IR, 1304.5nm resonance) and claiming (per abstract) arbitrary 2D all-optical spatial light modulation. Relevance to TAYF: 74fs is roughly 13 orders of magnitude faster than video rate, so raw switching speed is emphatically not the bottleneck here — but this is not an electrically-addressable display device at all. The "content" written onto the metasurface is itself an optical pump pattern (a spatially structured pump beam controls the probe's wavefront via the pump-probe scheme, Fig. 1a) — meaning to display arbitrary imagery you would first need another device to pattern the pump beam, which just relocates rather than solves the "how do you address a 2D SLM with content" problem. It is best read as fundamental nonlinear-optics physics establishing an upper speed bound for metasurface reconfiguration, not as a candidate video-rate display driver; useful context for `hardware/optical-engine.md`'s framing that switching speed per se is not the reason existing tunable metasurfaces are too slow (addressing architecture is).

### 2511.22639 — A 160°×160° Dynamic Holographic Meta-Projector

Jinan University / USTC group solves the narrow-viewing-angle problem cited in `hardware/optical-engine.md` (the ~few-degree FOV limitation attributed to arXiv 2203.06784), via a genuinely clever hybrid architecture rather than a faster-switching metasurface: a conventional 2000x2000-pixel LCoS SLM (Holoeye GAEA, native 60Hz) is optically pixel-compressed and projected onto a *static, passively fabricated* TiO2 metasurface (6000x6000 unit cells, 249.3nm pitch) that performs pixel-interpolation — each large SLM pixel maps onto a cluster of subwavelength metasurface phase elements, so the composite phase pattern has SLM-level dynamic programmability but metasurface-level (subwavelength) spatial frequency content, enabling much larger diffraction angles than the SLM's native pixel pitch would allow. They derive an explicit k-space distortion-correction transform for extreme angles (Eq. 1-2, Jacobian gamma^-4 brightness correction) and a modified Gerchberg-Saxton retrieval algorithm (Fig. 4a) that up/down-samples between the SLM's M x M grid and the metasurface's N x N grid. Measured results are real and substantial: 159.4 deg x 159.2 deg FOV (vs. ~70x70 deg previous best cited in the literature, a ~7x7 area improvement over pixel-compression alone), 45.1% total diffraction efficiency, arbitrary (not pre-set) image content via per-target-image SLM phase optimization, and a demonstrated dynamic "dolphin swimming" hologram video confirmed at 60Hz refresh via photodetector FFT (Fig. 5d-e) — exceeding the ~24Hz human flicker-fusion threshold. Relevance to TAYF: this is the single most direct hit against `hardware/optical-engine.md`'s stated CGH fidelity ceiling ("coherent speckle, narrow angle, not real-time yet") — it demonstrates wide-angle (approaching the 180 deg theoretical subwavelength limit), video-rate (60Hz), arbitrary-content holographic projection using hardware that exists today (a commercial SLM plus one static, cheaply-replicable metasurface). It should change the optical-engine.md ranked-mechanism table's "narrow angle" caveat for holographic SLM/CGH — angle is no longer the blocking constraint this citation shows it to be. It does not close the whole gap, though: the video was precomputed frame-by-frame and played back sequentially (the paper explicitly defers "increasing frame rate" and real-time hologram computation to future GPU/high-speed-interface work, so the underlying hologram-generation-speed problem — the 57s/frame or ~1.5s/frame numbers already in the doc — is untouched by this result); the demonstration is monochromatic (full color is explicitly future work); the projection distance/image scale (z=8.39mm, ~95mm image) is a small benchtop setup, not evaluated at 10cm-cube integration; and — like essentially every CGH paper in this corpus — there is no treatment of a moving human face, only geometric test patterns (dragon, dolphin). Net: a real, measured, meaningful narrowing of the "narrow angle" sub-problem specifically, not of the "compute a hologram of a live human face fast enough" sub-problem, which remains as unsolved as before.

### 2401.12537 — Motion Hologram: Jointly optimized hologram generation and motion planning for photorealistic and speckle-free 3D displays via reinforcement learning

Shanghai Jiao Tong group addresses CGH's coherent-speckle problem (the other fidelity-ceiling issue `hardware/optical-engine.md` cites, alongside narrow angle) with a mechanical/computational hybrid trick: rather than time-multiplexing multiple *distinct* holograms on a fast SLM (which needs SLM refresh rates most commercial devices don't have) or tiling multiple SLMs, they physically shift a single phase-only hologram along a small trajectory (a few pixels) while the SLM displays it, producing spatially-shifted replicas with correlated "motion speckle" that average out when temporally integrated by the eye/camera — and they jointly learn both the hologram and the optimal motion trajectory with a reinforcement-learning agent (actor-critic, PPO, reward = focal-stack PSNR gain including an in-focus-region term, Eq. 9) rather than hand-designing the motion path. Measured results: simulation PSNR improves from 23.22dB (traditional) to 33.22dB (motion hologram), a 10dB gain, SSIM up by 0.47, beating 2-frame time-multiplexed holography and matching 4-frame time-multiplexed holography quality using only a single SLM and single laser; real benchtop optical experiments (not just simulation) on full-color multi-plane scenes (Bamboo, Bunny, cave) confirm 3.9-4.8dB PSNR gains over traditional holography with correct 3D defocus behavior (avoiding vergence-accommodation conflict artifacts that plain holography exhibits). Relevance to TAYF: this is a genuine, measured way to reduce speckle — one of the two fidelity problems the optical-engine.md ranking table cites against CGH — using only commodity hardware (one SLM, one laser, plus a mechanism to physically translate the SLM/hologram along a trajectory). It does not touch the other cited problem (narrow FOV — see 2511.22639 above) or the real-time-generation-speed problem: the motion trajectory and hologram are jointly optimized offline per scene via RL/SGD, and the paper reports no video-rate live-content generation numbers, so this should be read as a photorealism/speckle fix layered on top of an unsolved generation-speed problem, not a full solution. It also adds a new engineering requirement (precise, fast, repeatable mechanical or electro-mechanical motion of the SLM or hologram synchronized to display timing) that is itself unevaluated for size/power/reliability at 10cm-cube scale.

### 2409.11049 — HoloTile RGB: Ultra-fast single-shot, discretized, full-color computer-generated phase-only holography

University of Southern Denmark (Glückstad group) extends their "HoloTile" CGH modality to full color and demonstrates genuine real-time video-rate holographic playback, which is a direct, strong hit against priority #2's core question. The trick: rather than optimizing one large iterative hologram over the whole SLM (the source of the ~57s/frame-class slowness cited in `hardware/optical-engine.md`), HoloTile tiles many small, independently and rapidly computable *subholograms* across the SLM (Eq. 1's convolution of a PSF-shaping term with a tiled subhologram array), which both drastically cuts computation (each subhologram only needs to represent a small local patch) and — because the PSF is explicitly shaped into a square, non-overlapping output pixel via an analytically computed phase profile (Eq. governed by dimensionless beta, no iterative optimization needed for that part) — eliminates the coherent-speckle problem *without* needing multi-frame temporal averaging, unlike most competing speckle-reduction schemes. For RGB, they align the three wavelength-dependent output grids to a common pixel pitch (Eq. matching lambda*f/l_s across channels) and drive a Holoeye GAEA 2.1 SLM in Color-Field-Sequential mode, achieving full 8-bit phase modulation at 60Hz per color channel (180Hz color-cycling). Critically, they demonstrate actual real-time video playback, not just static images: four camera-captured video sequences ("Butterfly," "Fireworks," "Citrus," "Nemo") computed and displayed in sync with playback, citing a >100x hologram-generation speed improvement over conventional CGH from their own prior HoloTile work, with the PSF-shaping hologram computed once and reused across an entire video (only the tiled object hologram is recomputed per frame). Relevance to TAYF: this is one of the strongest concrete "faster real-time CGH" data points found in this pass — genuine video-rate (60Hz+), full-color, speckle-free holographic video demonstrated on commodity SLM hardware, directly better than the 57s/frame or ~1.5s-per-frame numbers already cited. It does not close the gap, however: the reconstructed image is a "pseudo-digital" discretized output-pixel grid (the tiling/PSF-shaping trick trades continuous-wavefront fidelity for speed and speckle-freedom — effectively rendering a point/pixel array rather than a naturally continuous holographic reconstruction, which may cost resolution or subtlety on complex, non-flat content like skin/hair), the demonstrated content is generic 2D video clips (fish, fireworks, fruit) not 3D volumetric human faces, there is no discussion of FOV (the companion 160x160 meta-projector paper above is a separate, unrelated fix for that problem) or of a 10cm-cube-compatible optical path, and no mention of eye safety or power budget. Still, combined with 2511.22639's wide-FOV result, this is the strongest evidence in this corpus that both of `optical-engine.md`'s two named CGH fidelity-ceiling problems (speckle, narrow angle) have independent, real, measured partial solutions in the recent literature — while the deeper "render a photoreal moving human face in real time" problem remains untouched by either.

### 2506.08253 — High-density three-dimensional holography using rapid modulation of light

UNAM/Louisiana State group tackles a different CGH fidelity problem — inter-plane crosstalk in multiplane 3D holographic reconstruction, caused by coherent light from different depth planes interfering with each other — using a fast DMD (~10kHz refresh) rather than extra optics (photon sieves, scattering media) that prior methods needed. Their method: discretize a 3D object into point-cloud contours across multiple depth planes, and for each point independently compute a closed-form (non-iterative) kinoform hologram via an analytic Gaussian-beam-to-Fourier-space expression (Eq. 2: psi-tilde_zi in closed form) back-propagated to the DMD plane via the angular spectrum method (Eq. 3-4) — because each point's hologram is a simple closed-form Fresnel/Fourier calculation rather than an iterative optimization, and because the DMD refreshes each point's hologram sequentially at ~10kHz, each depth plane effectively gets its own independently time-multiplexed illumination, eliminating coherence-induced crosstalk without needing photon sieves or scattering elements. They demonstrate real reconstructed 3D solids (pyramid, cone, double cone) with transverse point separations down to 100um and confirmed Gaussian beam fidelity across depths. Relevance to TAYF: this is a genuine, real (not merely proposed) technique for a specific CGH quality problem (multiplane crosstalk) using only a fast DMD and closed-form (hence fast-to-compute) per-point holograms — relevant to both the "real-time CGH" and, more directly, to Branch A's "voxel"/point-cloud framing since the output is literally a sparse 3D point cloud rather than a continuous surface. It is a small-scale, monochromatic (637nm CW laser), geometric-solids-only demonstration (not faces, not color, not evaluated at the point counts a recognizable face needs), and the paper does not report an overall achieved points/second throughput figure comparable to JSID 2025's ~10k voxels/s baseline — so while conceptually complementary to Branch A's laser-plasma point-generation-rate question, it is a different (SLM/DMD holographic, not plasma-ionization) physical mechanism and cannot be directly compared on the same scaling axis without that missing number.

### 2605.04509 — CoherentRaster: Efficient 3D Gaussian Splatting for Light Field Displays

A real-time rendering-pipeline paper directly targeting Branch B's compute bottleneck: light-field displays (LFDs) need dozens-to-hundreds of view-dependent renders combined into one interlaced image, and naively running 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) per view is far too slow (their baseline: 5.8fps at 2K, 2.1-4.1fps at 4K). CoherentRaster instead performs subpixel-level rasterization directly, using "view reuse" to eliminate redundant computation across neighboring viewpoints and "view remapping" to recover GPU warp-level memory efficiency lost to the LFD's spatially-interlaced subpixel layout — a genuine systems/rasterization-architecture contribution, not just a smaller model. Measured results (commented-out table in the source but populated with real numbers): grouping views into batches of |Vk|=2/4/8/16 trades quality for speed, e.g. at 2K, |Vk|=8 reaches 87.7fps (vs. 5.8fps for per-view 3DGS, a ~15x speedup) with PSNR 51.94dB/SSIM 0.9989 against a per-view 3DGS pseudo-ground-truth; at 4K with |Vk|=8, 55.6fps at PSNR 52.19dB. Relevance to TAYF: this is a real, measured "how do you actually drive a many-view light-field panel in real time" answer, directly on-topic for `experiments/light-field/README.md` and `pipeline/view_synthesis/README.md`'s stated open questions — it demonstrates that consumer GPUs (their comparisons are against standard hardware, not datacenter clusters) can render tens of light-field views at well above video rate from a Gaussian-splat scene representation, which is exactly the representation `pipeline/avatar/README.md` already uses (GETA-3DGS). It does not touch optics or free-space emission at all — it is purely a software/rendering acceleration for panel-bound light-field displays (the "hackathon track," not the north-star free-space track), and the reported scenes are static architectural/object scenes (sponza, cars), not a moving human face, so its applicability to TAYF's actual avatar content is inferred, not demonstrated.

### 2601.19901 — Light Field Display Point Rendering

A second, independently strong real-time light-field-display rendering result, notable because it is validated on an actual physical light-field-display prototype (custom-built, tilted-lens design per Yu et al. 2014, 3840x2160 native resolution, 345x194mm screen, 0.09mm pixel pitch, ~479 lenses across the screen width — the same general device class as the Looking Glass-style panel `hardware/optical-engine.md` names as the hackathon track), not just simulation. LFDPR (Light Field Display Point Rendering) improves on prior eye-based point rendering with texture-based splatting (avoiding oversampling of triangles mapped to only a few texels) and "LFD-biased sampling" that matches triangle sampling density to the display's actual native sampling grid, plus multiview mipmapping and angular/spatial supersampling+reconstruction to fight aliasing and view crosstalk. Measured on an RTX 3070 across four real scenes (sponza, gallery, coconut, car) rendering 48-96 views at 480x360 each: LFDPR is up to 8x faster than standard multiview rendering (MVR) with comparable or better quality (measured via RMSE, HDR-VDP3 P-det/Q/Q-JOD, and SSIM against a 96-view/32x-supersampled gold-standard reference), and up to 3-5x faster under 2x spatial/angular supersampling; per-view-buffer memory drops from 2.63MB (MVR) to 1.32MB (LFDPR). Relevance to TAYF: like CoherentRaster above, this is a genuine, hardware-validated answer to "can consumer-grade hardware drive a real many-view light-field panel at practical framerates" — directly useful evidence for the hackathon-track light-field/AIP panel choice in `hardware/optical-engine.md`, and for `experiments/light-field/README.md`'s open question about achievable view count and render performance. It is again a rendering-pipeline result, not new optics, tested on static/rotating synthetic scenes rather than a captured human avatar, and doesn't address the free-space (north-star) track at all.

### 2508.18540 — Real-time 3D Visualization of Radiance Fields on Light Field Displays

A third, independent, and even stronger real-time light-field-rendering result than CoherentRaster/LFDPR above, run on an actual commercial light-field display (the paper cites Leia, Sony, and Looking Glass as the target device class). The core method is a single-pass plane-sweep over multiplane images (MPI) computed from an input radiance field (supporting both 3D Gaussian Splatting and Sparse Voxels representations, unified in one framework, "G2LF" and "V2LF" variants), which lets many nearby quilt views share cached intermediate plane renders instead of independently re-rendering each of the panel's 45+ viewpoints from scratch — trading some view-dependent specular fidelity for large speed gains. Measured results: on standard benchmarks, up to 22x speedup versus independently rendering each view; their interactive OpenGL demo on a real commercial light-field display achieves 228 FPS for 45-view, 512x910 quilt images (bicycle scene) on a single RTX 5090, and more broadly >60 FPS for 90+ views — comfortably real-time, with a further 3.6x speedup of their OpenGL implementation over a Python/CUDA baseline. Relevance to TAYF: this is the strongest of the three independent light-field-rendering-speed papers found in this pass (alongside CoherentRaster and LFDPR) and, combined with them, represents a fairly convincing, three-way-corroborated answer to Branch B's rendering-side open question — real-time (well above video rate) many-view light-field rendering from Gaussian/voxel radiance-field content is achievable today on a single consumer/prosumer GPU and demonstrated on actual commercial panel hardware, not just simulated. This directly de-risks the `hardware/optical-engine.md` hackathon track's software feasibility. It says nothing about the free-space (north-star) track, nothing about how few native views are actually perceptually necessary (the panel's native 45+ views are simply rendered faster, not reduced — `experiments/angular-resolution/README.md`'s minimum-channel-count question remains open), and is demonstrated on static/rotating object scenes (bicycle, standard NeRF benchmarks), not a live human avatar feed.

### 2506.08064 — A Real-time 3D Desktop Display

ICTP Trieste group extends their open-source altiro3D C++ library to a genuinely live, working single-camera-to-light-field-display pipeline — and it is worth flagging that they explicitly name video conferencing as a target use case, making this the closest thing in this whole search to an actual working prototype of TAYF's hackathon track. Pipeline: a USB webcam (or a captured screen region) feeds frames into the MiDaS monocular-depth-estimation CNN (run via OpenCV's DNN module, ONNX format), which produces a per-frame depth map; a view-synthesis step (choice of "FAST" or "REAL"/geometric algorithm) then generates N viewpoints via simple OpenCV/Telea inpainting to fill disocclusion holes; the views are assembled into a "Quilt" and remapped to the display's native "Native" format via a precomputed pixel-and-device-specific lookup table (avoiding a slow per-frame remapping computation); the output feeds directly into a Looking Glass Portrait — the exact commercial light-field-panel product class already named as the hackathon-track candidate in `hardware/optical-engine.md`. Measured performance: on a laptop (Intel i9-10885H, GTX 1650 Ti Mobile), the system runs at 10Hz (~100ms/frame) using CPU-only MiDaS-small inference — notably, they found the naive CUDA backend was *slower* than CPU (~160ms/frame vs ~100ms/frame) and only CUDA-FP16 matched CPU speed (Table 2), a real, measured (if minor) finding about GPU-acceleration pitfalls for this kind of pipeline. Profiling (Table 3, gprofng) shows MiDaS inference dominates runtime (>50% inclusive), identifying the CNN depth-estimation step, not the view-synthesis or LUT-remapping steps, as the actual bottleneck. Relevance to TAYF: this is a real, complete, open-source, already-working system spanning capture -> depth -> multiview synthesis -> commercial light-field-panel display, essentially validating the entire hackathon-track pipeline end-to-end at a hobbyist/lab scale, and is worth a direct pointer from `hardware/bom.md`'s panel-sourcing task and `pipeline/view_synthesis/README.md`. It is not photoreal or high-quality: monocular-depth-derived multiview synthesis produces well-known artifacts (disocclusion inpainting errors, limited true parallax since only one physical camera view exists), 10Hz is below conventional smooth video rate (their own stated minimum threshold), and there's no quantitative image-quality or user-study evaluation — only a systems/performance report. Still, as a concrete existence proof that "capture a person, synthesize multiple views in real time, drive a commercial light-field panel" is buildable today with open-source code, this is one of the most directly actionable findings in this entire pass for the project's near-term hackathon deliverable, as distinct from the free-space north-star gap.

### 2604.16237 — Ellipsography: Single-Shot Speckle-Free Holography via Vectorial Interference Shaping

Probably the strongest pure speckle-suppression result found in this pass, and a third independent attack on `hardware/optical-engine.md`'s "coherent speckle" fidelity-ceiling problem (after Motion Hologram and HoloTile above), via a genuinely different mechanism: rather than scalar phase-only holography, Ellipsography jointly modulates phase *and polarization* at each pixel (a vectorial wave model) to structurally reshape optical interference and suppress speckle at its source, rather than averaging it away over time or space. Claimed and measured results are large: in simulation, single-frame image fidelity "equivalent to averaging a million conventional scalar holograms" (speckle contrast beats the 1/sqrt(N) temporal-multiplexing curve at N=10^6), and on a real benchtop prototype display (two cascaded 4f systems with polarization optics, not a stock SLM), they report ~30dB PSNR — a 10dB improvement over the best existing real-display speckle-suppression technique, with SSIM/LPIPS also favoring ellipsography, and pupil-invariant image quality maintained across eyebox positions (unlike smooth-phase holograms, which degrade sharply off-center). Relevance to TAYF: this is a real, measured, hardware-validated (not just simulated) leap in holographic image quality specifically targeting the speckle half of the CGH fidelity-ceiling problem, and — notably for a project whose optical engine must eventually target a moving human face — they explicitly test against 3D light-field content and multi-depth holograms, not just flat 2D images. It does not address real-time generation speed at all, and in fact is *slower* than conventional iterative CGH in its current form (~2.2s per full-color frame via gradient descent, explicitly not real-time; the authors state neural-network-based synthesis is future work needed to reach interactive frame rates) — so this paper trades the speckle problem for no improvement (and likely a net cost) on the speed problem. It also requires non-standard optical hardware (joint phase+polarization SLM control, not a commodity phase-only SLM), which the authors themselves flag as needing new SLM hardware support and further miniaturization before any near-eye or (a fortiori) 10cm-cube integration is plausible.

### 2404.10777 — Divide-Conquer-and-Merge: Memory- and Time-Efficient Holographic Displays

A learned-CGH speed/resolution-scaling paper directly relevant to priority #2's "anything faster than tensor holography" question, and notably the strongest resolution number found in this pass. Most deep-learning CGH networks (HoloNet, CCNN-CGH, and the "tensor holography" ~1.5s/frame method already cited in `hardware/optical-engine.md`) top out around 1080p-4K because GPU memory scales with hologram resolution during training/inference. This paper's "divide-conquer-and-merge" strategy splits a high-resolution target into sub-hologram patches (via a pixel-unshuffle layer), predicts lower-definition sub-holograms per patch, then merges them while modeling inter-pixel information between neighboring sub-holograms, implemented as a wrapper strategy applicable to existing SOTA networks (tested with HoloNet and CCNN-CGH as backbones). Measured results: 66fps for 1080p holograms (faster than the naive HoloNet baseline), and — the headline number — successful training and inference of full 8K-definition holograms on a single consumer RTX 3090 GPU, which the memory constraints of prior networks made impractical, with real optical experiments (not just simulation) verifying image quality. Relevance to TAYF: this pushes real-time-class neural CGH toward genuinely high resolution (8K) rather than just fast-but-limited-resolution, which matters if a free-space or wide-FOV holographic engine needs high pixel/point density to render a recognizable face rather than a coarse test pattern — a resolution axis that's arguably as important as the raw fps number `hardware/optical-engine.md` currently cites. It remains a 2D/near-eye-display-oriented result (no free-space or 10cm-cube evaluation), and 66fps is reported only at 1080p — the 8K case's actual frame rate is not given as a headline number in the excerpts reviewed, so the resolution and speed achievements should not be assumed to compound (i.e., this is not "8K at 66fps").

### 2601.08906 — A 10 Megahertz Spatial Light Modulator

Stanford group (Simon lab) demonstrates a genuinely novel spatial-light-modulator architecture that is, by a wide margin, the fastest arbitrary-2D-pattern beam-addressing device found anywhere in this search — directly relevant to Branch A's point/voxel generation-rate question, though the paper itself never discusses displays, holography, or voxels. The "Re-Imaging Phased Array" (RIPA) SLM encodes each desired spot's position as a distinct RF-modulated optical frequency tone via a ~10GHz electro-optic modulator, then decodes frequency-to-position using two cascaded RIPA "spectrometers" — each a folded delay-line interferometer (round-trip path lengths 9.4cm and 231cm) stabilized by piezo-controlled mirrors and re-imaged by microlens arrays to prevent beam divergence over many round trips — so that a focusing lens Fourier-transforms the resulting frequency-dependent phased array into a diffraction-limited spot whose (x,y) location is set purely by optical frequency, rastering row-by-row like a CRT. Measured results: a 44(1)ns rise time for site-resolved optical pulsing (>10 million effective frames/second), independent asynchronous multi-spot motion, splitting and recombination of beams, 2ns temporal / 5um spatial resolution characterization via a scanning photodetector, and — importantly — this is demonstrated as genuinely reconfigurable/arbitrary addressing (not a fixed pattern), verified optically, not just simulated. Relevance to TAYF: this is a real, physically demonstrated "how do you address points 2-3 orders of magnitude faster than existing SLM/AOD/DMD technology" result, conceptually on-topic for Branch A's repetition-rate and scan-speed research variables (JSID 2025's 10k voxels/s baseline vs. this device's >10^7 site-addressing events/s theoretical ceiling) and for the general "narrow the CGH speed gap" question. It must be read with real caution, however: the authors' own stated target application is neutral-atom quantum computing and MHz-rate microscopy, not display — holography/CGH is never mentioned as an application; the actually-demonstrated spot count is small (order ~9-11 independently resolved spots in the reported characterization), with the paper's own stated near-term scaling target being an "80x80 array," and true megapixel-scale resolution is explicitly flagged as a *future* combination of multiple wavelength channels, not something demonstrated here; it is fundamentally a 2D (x,y) beam-steering/addressing mechanism, not itself a volumetric (3D) point generator or a free-space-emission mechanism — turning this into a TAYF-relevant voxel engine would require pairing it with a separate depth/z mechanism (e.g., nonlinear plasma ionization or an SLM-holography pipeline) and would need real evaluation of whether the technique preserves any usefulness once pixel count scales toward the millions a face needs; and the current implementation is a multi-meter folded optical path with a high-power optical amplifier and active piezo stabilization — not remotely compact. This is best read as a strong, real, but early-stage physics result establishing that MHz-to-10MHz-class arbitrary 2D beam addressing is achievable, worth flagging in `experiments/voxel-display/README.md`'s research-variable list as a candidate scanning mechanism to watch, not as a ready-made solution.

### 2205.02367 — Time-multiplexed Neural Holography: A Flexible Framework for Holographic Near-eye Displays with Fast Heavily-quantized Spatial Light Modulators

Stanford/NVIDIA/CMU group directly tackles a mismatch relevant to priority #3: existing AI-driven CGH algorithms are built for slow, high-precision liquid-crystal SLMs, but a class of MEMS-based phase SLMs already exists that is dramatically faster — the paper cites a commercial Texas Instruments DLP phase SLM offering 1440Hz frame rates, at the cost of only 4-bit (16-level) phase precision — and standard CGH algorithms degrade badly on such coarse quantization because it is non-differentiable and breaks the usual gradient-based optimization. The paper's contribution is an algorithmic framework (a learned, camera-calibrated wave-propagation model plus quantization-aware optimization) that robustly generates heavily-quantized holograms exploiting the SLM's speed via time-multiplexing (e.g., 8 sub-frames per displayed frame, still leaving ~180Hz effective update rate after multiplexing at 1440Hz native rate — comfortably above video rate), and is flexible enough to be supervised by 2D, 2.5D RGBD, 3D focal-stack, or full 4D light-field target content, beating both the prior neural-3D-holography baseline (Choi et al. 2021, single-frame on the same fast hardware) and the OLAS light-field method on image quality and space-bandwidth utilization (Fig. 1). Relevance to TAYF: this is a genuine, existing example of what the project's search brief called for — a real, currently-available fast SLM technology (MEMS phase SLM, 1440Hz, TI DLP-class) that is not the ms-to-second-switching liquid-crystal or phase-change device class TAYF has already flagged as too slow — paired with an algorithm specifically designed to make its speed usable despite coarse phase quantization. This is complementary to, not competing with, the metasurface-based fast-switching candidates found elsewhere in this pass (2501.06102's GHz electro-optic metasurface, 2303.14066's LC metasurface SLM): it shows that "fast enough for video rate" phase-only spatial light modulation is achievable today with commercial MEMS hardware, if paired with the right algorithm, rather than requiring a still-experimental metasurface. It remains a near-eye (headset-bound) display result, not evaluated for free-space or a 10cm cube, and — like virtually every CGH paper surveyed — is demonstrated on generic 2D/3D test content, not a captured human face.

### Papers checked and excluded (keyword-matched but off-topic, noted for completeness rather than silently dropped)

Beyond the plasma-physics false positives already listed above (2603.17164, 2603.23285, 2408.09052, 2301.04458 — all real papers, all about particle-accelerator/petawatt-laser plasma optics or MeV electron acceleration, none about display or free-space emission), the following were read at abstract/intro level and found not to advance the free-space-display gap: 2409.03143 (Large Étendue 3D Holographic Display — real multi-source étendue-expansion result, but for *near-eye* eyebox/pupil-tracking VR, not free-space, and its own measured frame rate is only ~13.3Hz, explicitly stated by the authors as not real-time); 2405.09844 (Electrically switchable 2^N-channel cascaded metasurfaces — real 8-channel switching demo, but switching is via slow liquid-crystal retarders between bulky cascaded 4f relay systems, not a speed advance); 2410.15783, 2411.04937, 2511.03583 (phase-change and lithium-niobate tunable metasurfaces — real fabricated devices but either no reported switching-speed data, simulation-only efficiency claims, or (for the mid-IR PCM SLM) thermal phase-change switching consistent with the ms-to-second speeds `hardware/optical-engine.md` already flags as too slow, and wrong wavelength regime for a visible-light display in any case). None of these change the picture established by the papers written up above.

### 2408.02772 — Tomographic diffractometry of laser-induced plasma formations
(Read in full text, `research/papers/tmp/txt/2408.02772.txt`.) Tel Aviv University group develops a transverse optical-diffractometry technique that extracts plasma density/width/shape (Super-Gaussian model fit) with ~20µm spatial resolution along a femtosecond-filament's propagation axis, sensitive down to ~50µJ pulse energies. This is a *diagnostic/metrology* paper, not a display paper: the setup runs at ~1kHz (pump) / 500Hz (probe chop) — nowhere near JSID 2025's ~10kHz-crossover regime — and reports zero information about voxel rate, brightness, or scan-pattern throughput. It's genuinely about characterizing single filaments in isolation, useful background physics for understanding filament formation dynamics but contributes nothing to the voxels/s scaling question. Not a false-positive off-topic particle-physics paper (it is squarely air-plasma-in-the-JSID-sense), but it's orthogonal to the scaling question, not a data point for or against it.

### 2310.13374 — Single-Shot Local Measurement of Terahertz Correlated Second Harmonic Generation in Laser Air Plasma Filaments
(Read in full text, `research/papers/tmp/txt/2310.13374.txt`.) University of Rochester group builds a single-shot THz-field diagnostic (TFISH) using a two-color air-plasma filament as the THz source, at a 1kHz/100fs Ti:Sapphire system. This is THz-generation/detection physics, not display-relevant — no voxel, no scan pattern, no rep-rate scaling result. Excluded as off-topic for the same reason the prior pass excluded the THz papers (2301.04458, 2403.18499, 2404.14847): the "laser air plasma" match is about THz emission physics, not aerial-voxel display engineering.

### 2512.09401 — Photophoretic Trapping: Fundamentals, Advances and Future Directions
(Read in full text, `research/papers/tmp/txt/2512.09401.txt`.) This is a broad review (not new experimental work) that includes a "3D Volumetric display" subsection discussing the *existing* (2018, Smalley et al., Nature) single-particle photophoretic-trap volumetric display — a mechanically-scanned single cellulose particle rapidly repositioned and RGB-illuminated to draw sub-10µm voxels with near-360° viewing. **This is a genuinely different mechanism from laser-plasma** (405nm CW trapping laser moving one physical particle, not femtosecond air ionization) and is not currently listed in `hardware/optical-engine.md`'s ranked-mechanisms table at all — worth flagging as an omission, even though it's strictly out of Sub-goal A's laser-plasma scope. The review's own "Looking forward" section proposes parallel multi-particle trapping via SLMs as a path to higher voxel counts, but cites only a 2016 SPIE paper (Porfirev & Skidanov, ref [79], not on arXiv, not a 2025-26 result) — i.e. this is aspirational, not a demonstrated scaling result. **No new voxel-rate or voxel-count number appears anywhere in this review beyond the already-known 2018 single-particle figures.** Does not narrow the plasma-track gap; does surface a candidate 6th display mechanism that isn't yet in the project's mechanism inventory.

### 2606.26159 — Microparticles manipulation with a three-dimensional closed optical trap
(Read in full text.) A biomedical/cell-manipulation optical-tweezer paper (Beijing Institute of Technology) — traps mouse hepatocytes and microparticles in a dark-field 3D potential well without an SLM. No display application, no voxel content, no plasma. Off-topic, excluded — a clean false positive from the keyword filter (matched only on generic "optical trap").

### 2512.05042 — Structured Light at the Extreme: Harnessing Spatiotemporal Control for High-Field Laser-Matter Interactions
(Read abstract + author list in full; large multi-institution review, UCLA/SLAC/Rochester/ICFO/etc.) A review of structured-light control for particle-accelerator physics: programmable electron beams, OAM γ-rays, compact THz accelerators. Mentions "plasma light modulators" as a beam-shaping tool for accelerator applications, not display. This is exactly the kind of high-field-physics "plasma" false positive the prior pass already learned to exclude (same pattern as 2603.17164/2603.23285/2408.09052/2301.04458) — confirmed off-topic, not a display paper.

### 2312.04651 — VOODOO 3D: Volumetric Portrait Disentanglement for One-Shot 3D Head Reenactment
(Read in full LaTeX, `research/papers/tmp/src/2312.04651/`.) Real-time one-shot head-reenactment using tri-plane volumetric rendering, explicitly built and demoed for "holographic displays" — but the supplementary section ("Implementation Details for Holographic Display System") confirms this literally means a **32" Looking Glass monitor**, rendering 24 views over a ±17.5° yaw range, running at 25 FPS only by parallelizing across **seven NVIDIA RTX 6000 ADA GPUs**. Genuinely a HUMAN×OPTICS paper by keyword, but the "optics" side is a commercial lenticular panel (the project's own already-chosen hackathon track), not free space, and the compute cost (7 datacenter-class GPUs for one head at low angular range) is a useful data point on how expensive even panel-scale multi-view human rendering currently is.

### 2502.08085 — Interactive Holographic Visualization for 3D Facial Avatar
**Not in local corpus (no manifest.csv entry) — read via WebFetch of the arXiv abstract page and an ar5iv HTML render, not the project's own LaTeX pipeline; treat with correspondingly less certainty than corpus-resident entries above.** Targets pain-assessment medical training with a real-time 3D-Gaussian-Splatting facial avatar, described as being projected "holographically." The ar5iv-rendered text confirms this again means a **Looking Glass Portrait (LGP)** — 48 discrete views across a 40° view cone, screen-bound, not free space — with the 3DGS avatar renderer alone (not full display pipeline) hitting ≥60fps on an RTX 4060. Same pattern as VOODOO 3D: real HUMAN×OPTICS combination, but panel-bound.

### 2607.19731 — Fast Wave-optics Rendering of Multiplane Images for 3D Holographic Displays
(Read in full LaTeX, `research/papers/tmp/src/2607.19731/`.) A genuine CGH (coherent wavefront) paper, not a panel — but explicitly and repeatedly scoped to **"holographic near-eye displays"** (small eyebox, front-facing-only multiplane-image content, 4cm SLM-to-image-plane benchtop propagation distance, HOLOEYE Pluto-2.1 SLM). No human/face/portrait content anywhere in the method or results (Blender-style scenes, focal-stack test targets). Relevant to the optical-engine.md's already-documented Branch D (holographic SLM) literature, not free-space, not human content — does not close the gap, and the "HUMAN" tag match on this entry was a false positive (matched only on generic "view synthesis").

### 2505.06582 — Gaussian Wave Splatting for Computer-Generated Holography (Stanford, Choi/Chao/Yang/Gopakumar/Wetzstein)
**Not in local corpus (`manifest.csv` shows repeated fetch errors) — read via WebFetch of the arXiv abstract and the authors' project page (bchao1.github.io/gaussian-wave-splatting/), not the project's LaTeX pipeline.** This is the most directly relevant *methodological* bridge found in this pass: it derives a closed-form 2D-Gaussian-to-hologram transform, i.e. takes exactly the Gaussian-splat representation this project's `pipeline/avatar/` already uses and converts it into a CGH wavefront with correct occlusion/alpha-blending — the missing computational link between a Gaussian avatar and a holographic engine. However: (1) demonstrated only on standard NeRF/3DGS benchmark scenes (Garden, Kitchen, Materials, Room, Stump, Lego, Chair, Ship) — confirmed via the project page — **no human/face/portrait content**; (2) display target (near-eye vs. free-space) is not stated in any source I could access, and given this is the same Stanford lab as the two entries below (both explicitly near-eye), near-eye is the more likely — but this is my inference, not a verified fact, and should be marked **unverified**.

### 2508.17480 — Random-phase Wave Splatting of Translucent Primitives for Computer-generated Holography (same Stanford group: Chao, Yang, Choi, Gopakumar, Koiso, Wetzstein)
(Read in full text, `research/papers/tmp/txt/2508.17480.txt`.) The direct successor to Gaussian Wave Splatting — improves eyebox size and defocus accuracy via time-multiplexed random-phase holograms. Explicitly and repeatedly scoped to **"holographic near-eye displays"** ("eyeglasses-like device form factor," VR/AR), tested on Blender scenes and translucent triangle/Gaussian primitives, no human content. This confirms the GWS→RPWS line is a near-eye AR/VR research program, not a free-space one — so 2505.06582 above is very likely near-eye too, though I did not find a sentence in 2505.06582 itself that says so explicitly (hence "unverified" above rather than "confirmed").

### 2511.15022 — Complex-Valued 2D Gaussian Representation for Computer-Generated Holography (Zhan/Gao/Quan/Akşit — UCL/HKUST "complight" lab, ECCV 2026)
**Not in local corpus — read via WebFetch of the arXiv abstract only; full text not obtained.** A different group's Gaussian-primitive CGH method: reports up to 3200× faster rendering and 13dB higher PSNR versus prior Gaussian CGH methods (i.e., likely including GWS above), 30% less VRAM. No human content confirmed absent (checked). Whether the display target is free-space or near-eye is **explicitly unverified** — the abstract's phrase "light propagation kernel in free space" most likely refers to the standard free-space diffraction/Fresnel propagation operator used in CGH math generally (a term used even for near-eye systems), not necessarily a free-space aerial-display product target, and I could not confirm which from the accessible text. Flagging as a real open question rather than guessing either way.

### 2503.17032 — TaoAvatar / 2606.29333 — HiReFF: **false positives, verified and excluded**
(Read in full LaTeX/text.) Both cite "holographic communication" only as a one-line motivating buzzword in their intro/related-work (alongside AR/VR, e-commerce livestreaming, sports broadcasting) — neither paper actually targets, builds, or evaluates against any holographic or free-space display. TaoAvatar's actual target is real-time rendering on mobile devices and **Apple Vision Pro** (a near-eye stereo headset, not holographic in any optical sense); HiReFF is a feed-forward 3DGS reconstruction method with no display component at all. These illustrate a second failure mode in this search: "holographic" as a keyword catches marketing language about telepresence generally, unrelated to actual optics. Correctly excluded from being counted as HUMAN×OPTICS matches despite tag co-occurrence.

### Branch C (aerial imaging) — corpus sweep result: zero genuine papers found, venue gap not a physics verdict

A dedicated 467-paper triage of every "aerial"-tagged corpus entry, plus a full-15,783-paper keyword sweep for retroreflective/catadioptric/Fresnel/AIRR/ASKA3D/corner-cube terminology, found **zero genuine aerial-imaging-display-optics papers on arXiv** — all 467 were drone/satellite/remote-sensing false positives (object detection, 3D reconstruction from drone photos, land-cover mapping, cross-view geo-localization), the same failure mode as "plasma" matching particle-accelerator physics. This is not evidence the mechanism doesn't work: external web search confirms Aerial Imaging by Retro-Reflection (AIRR — Yamamoto/Suyama et al., Utsunomiya University, commercialized as ASKA3D) is a real, decade-plus, active research program published in Optics Express, OSA Continuum, and Optical Review — venues arXiv doesn't mirror. Titles found include papers almost verbatim on this branch's two open questions ("Reducing thickness of long-distance aerial display system in AIRR using Fresnel lens," Optical Review 2023; "Improved resolution for aerial imaging by retro-reflection with two transparent spheres," Optical Review 2022), but full text could not be obtained (Optica pages are JS-gated, Springer redirected to a login wall, ResearchGate returned 403) — no quantitative figures are reported as verified. For TAYF: Branch C should be marked "unassessed — wrong venue searched," not "ruled out" or "no evidence exists." AIRR's known form factor (floating LED signage, retroreflector plates tens of centimeters across, arm's-length-plus viewing distances) is aimed at kiosk/signage scale, not a 10cm sealed enclosure with source and optics both inside — even favorable numbers from the primary literature would leave the "does this fold into 10cm" question open. Next step for anyone following up: get institutional/document-delivery access to Optics Express, OSA Continuum, and Optical Review specifically for the Yamamoto/Suyama AIRR line — another arXiv sweep will keep returning zero regardless of how the search terms are refined.

<!-- SECTION1-END -->

## Track 2 — Human Representation & Capture (avatars, gaussian splatting, view synthesis)

### 2512.06438 — AGORA: Adversarial Generation Of Real-time Animatable 3D Gaussian Head Avatars

AGORA (Ramazan et al.) is a StyleGAN2-based generative framework that produces animatable 3DGS head avatars conditioned on FLAME shape/expression/jaw parameters $(\beta,\psi,\theta)$, building on GGHEAD/EG3D-style UV-space synthesis. A mapping network turns a latent $z$ and camera $\pi$ into $w$; the main generator outputs UV feature maps $F_\text{uv}=\mathcal{G}(w,\beta)$ that are bilinearly sampled at fixed UV coordinates to give raw Gaussian attributes (offset from the FLAME mesh, scale, rotation, color, opacity); the final 3D position is "3D lifting" — interpolating the posed FLAME mesh at each UV coordinate and adding the predicted offset. A separate lightweight deformation branch, conditioned on $(\psi,\theta)$ via style modulation on low-res ($64{\times}64$) features, predicts residual position/scale/rotation that are composed (summation/quaternion multiplication) onto the identity branch's canonical attributes — this two-branch split is what prevents identity/expression entanglement (verified in an ablation: single-branch conditioning collapses ID from 0.75→0.56). Expression fidelity is driven by "dual discrimination": the discriminator is conditioned on a synthetic FLAME rendering colored by expression-isolated vertex displacement $\Delta V=V_\text{posed}-V_\text{neutral}$ rather than plain UV coloring, which cuts AED (average expression distance) from 0.832→0.588 versus no dual-discrimination. For mobile/real-time deployment, AGORA-M extracts $N{=}10{,}000$ sampled posed-minus-neutral Gaussian-attribute residuals, computes their SVD, keeps the top $K{=}64$ singular vectors as shared "Gaussian blendshapes," and trains a tiny two-layer MLP to regress blendshape coefficients from $(w,\psi,\theta)$ — so per-frame animation is just one neutral-avatar Gaussian set plus a linear combination of 64 bases. Trained on FFHQ (70K images, mirrored to 140K) at $256^2$ (65K Gaussians) then $512^2$ (262K Gaussians), 4 days on 4×RTX A6000; AGORA achieves FID 3.17 (best among GGHEAD/EG3D/Next3D/GAIA), and AGORA-M sustains near-identical quality (FID 3.36) while running at 560 FPS on an RTX A6000 desktop GPU and 60 FPS on a mobile phone via a WebGL 3DGS renderer. For TAYF this is a human-representation-stage technique directly aimed at exactly the constraint the cube has: the blendshape distillation shows that a full generative avatar's per-frame animation cost can be collapsed to a linear low-rank update executable on a phone-class chip, which is the right computational envelope for an embedded cube; the caveat is it's head/face-only (FLAME-driven), single-identity per generator sample rather than true photoreal personalization from few-shot capture, and doesn't address body/hands.

### 2504.01559 — RealityAvatar: Towards Realistic Loose Clothing Modeling in Animatable 3D Gaussian Avatars

RealityAvatar (Li et al., BUPT) targets the specific failure mode of standard LBS-driven 3DGS avatars: loose clothing (dresses, jackets) whose deformation depends not just on current pose but on recent motion history (e.g., a person abruptly stopping after a spin still has swinging fabric). The pipeline initializes 35k canonical 3D Gaussians on the SMPL surface and decomposes canonical→observation deformation into two stages. First, a "latentbone encoder" splits SMPL pose parameters into four regional groups (left arm, right arm, legs, torso), concatenates each with a learned "clothes latent code" $\mathcal{Z}_c$ that captures garment-specific fluttering behavior, and encodes the whole pose sequence (not just the current frame) through per-part single-layer FC networks feeding a shallow MLP. Second, a "motion trend module" feeds this sequence of encoded pose features through an LSTM (chosen over attention after comparative testing, since the dataset is small and clothing dynamics are strongly local in time) — the final LSTM hidden state plus the canonical Gaussian position predicts offsets $\Delta x,\Delta s,\Delta q$ via a 3-layer MLP decoder, giving $x_e=x_c+\Delta x$, $R_e=\Delta R\cdot R_c$, $s_e=s_c\cdot\exp(\Delta s)$. These motion-trend-deformed Gaussians are then passed through a standard skinning-MLP LBS transform (à la 3DGS-Avatar) into observation space, with a skinning-weight regularization loss against SMPL's barycentric-interpolated weights, and rendered with SH color plus a 16-D motion-trend feature and a per-frame lighting latent through a 64-neuron color MLP. On the I3D-Human loose-clothing benchmark (dresses, spins, abrupt stops) the method reaches PSNR 31.87/SSIM 0.9752 novel-view and 30.10/0.9689 novel-pose in ~0.6h training, beating 3DGS-Avatar (30.62/29.21, 0.5h) and matching/slightly trailing the specialized Dyco baseline (31.22/30.12, 12h) at ~20× less training time; an ablation shows removing the LSTM causes the largest quality drop (PSNR 31.87→30.88), confirming the temporal module — not the part-segmentation — is the dominant contributor. For TAYF this is squarely a human-representation-stage method: it demonstrates that plausible non-rigid clothing dynamics can be captured with a lightweight LSTM+MLP head bolted onto standard LBS-Gaussian avatars, at sub-hour personalization time — relevant if the cube's enrollment step needs to bake in clothing behavior quickly; the caveat is it's trained per-subject on multi-view video (not from the sparse/monocular capture a 10cm cube would realistically have) and the LSTM's sequential dependency adds a small but nonzero latency/state-tracking cost to the real-time animation loop.

### 2510.10492 — Towards Efficient 3D Gaussian Human Avatar Compression: A Prior-Guided Framework

This paper (Yin et al., CityU HK / Alibaba DAMO) is a direct, quantitatively strong template for TAYF's transport stage: it separates a 3DGS human avatar into a canonical appearance model (compressed once) and per-frame SMPL pose parameters (compressed every frame at near-zero cost). At the encoder, a "network-free" canonical 3DGS avatar is trained in a star pose using GauHuman-style human-prior-based Gaussian initialization (avoiding SfM), then compressed with an off-the-shelf 3DGS codec (GeS-TM, an MPEG JEE 6.2 anchor). Per-frame temporal state is just SMPL's 72 pose + 10 shape + 3×3 global rotation + 1×3 translation = 94 scalars, arithmetic-coded (CABAC) — this is the entire bit cost of animating the avatar for a new frame. At decode time, canonical-to-target deformation uses standard LBS math: the per-joint blended transform $\mathbf{A}(\hat J_t,\hat\theta_t)=\sum_k \omega_k \mathbf{A}_k$ and translation $\mathbf{b}$ move each Gaussian's position $\hat p_t = A p_c + b$, while covariance transforms as $\Sigma_t = A\,\Sigma_c\,A^\top$ (i.e., rotating the Gaussian's shape along with its LBS-driven joint rotation) — this covariance-rotation step is what lets clothing-shaped Gaussians rotate correctly rather than just translate. Trained/evaluated on ZJU-MoCap and MonoCap (10 sequences, 100 sampled frames each), the method achieves rate-distortion superior to conventional G-PCC/GeS-TM/HEVC/VVC point-cloud/video anchors and to a learned dynamic-3DGS baseline (CompactSTG), landing at under 0.2 Mbps on ZJU-MoCap and under 0.26 Mbps on MonoCap at 25 FPS, versus over 1 Mbps for the comparison methods. For TAYF this is close to a reference architecture for the transport/representation split the notes.md document explicitly calls for ("do not transmit the entire 3D human every frame") — 94 floats/frame is trivially within any real-time link budget and the LBS covariance-transform math is exactly the operation the receiving cube would run to reconstruct Gaussian geometry from a compact parameter stream; the caveat is the canonical avatar itself (containing all appearance/clothing detail) must be pre-built from multi-view video and transmitted once per enrollment, so this doesn't solve monocular/on-cube capture, and there's no facial-expression or hand-pose channel in the 94-parameter stream.

### 2511.14927 — CPSL: Representing Volumetric Video via Content-Promoted Scene Layers

CPSL (Hu et al., McGill/USC/CUHK-SZ) proposes a 2.5D layered representation as a lightweight alternative to full 3D/4D reconstruction for volumetric video, decomposing each monocular frame into $K$ depth-ordered RGBA layers $L_k=(C_k,\alpha_k,z_k)$, warped for novel views via standard plane-induced homography $p_s\sim K_s(R-\frac{tn^\top}{d})K_t^{-1}p_t$ and composited front-to-back with premultiplied alpha, $I=\sum_k C_k\alpha_k\prod_{j<k}(1-\alpha_j)$. The key technical contributions beyond classic MPI/LDI are: (1) layer assignment as content-aware energy minimization over depth reliability, semantic saliency, and instance coherence with a pairwise smoothness term weighted by image gradient and semantic-edge strength, so foreground instances (people, hands) get "promoted" to dedicated layers while flat background is merged down to a target layer budget; (2) a "Dynamic Pixel Strip" (DPS) — a thin, view-adaptive interpolation band synthesized after warping between foreground/background layers along detected silhouette boundaries (blending depth and color with weight $\gamma(x)$) specifically to eliminate the cracks/double-edges that plague independently-warped depth layers at large viewpoint offsets; and (3) an "Edge-Depth Cache" storing only sparse quantized depth offsets along boundaries rather than dense depth maps, to keep the format 2D-codec-compatible (H.265/AV1) with per-layer bitrate allocated by minimizing saliency-weighted LPIPS under a rate budget. On DyCheck, CPSL beats MPI by +3.1dB PSNR (29.60 vs 26.50) and halves the "Crack" boundary-artifact metric (0.05 vs 0.11), while on the FSVVD full-scene multi-camera dataset it matches point-cloud-streaming perceptual quality (31.2dB PSNR) at over 7× lower bitrate (2.3 vs 18.2 Mbps) and sustains >60 FPS rendering with $O(KHW)$ per-layer 2D warps.
