For TAYF this is a transport/compression-stage idea that sidesteps full 3D reconstruction entirely: since it's monocular-input and 2D-codec-compatible, it could serve as a fallback low-compute capture-to-transport path when full Gaussian avatar reconstruction isn't feasible on-cube, and the real-time $O(KHW)$ warp cost is cheap enough for embedded GPUs; the fundamental limit is the fronto-parallel-layer assumption breaks down for the wide-baseline, near-360° viewing angles a free-space optical display would need to support around a reconstructed human, restricting it to modest-parallax viewing cones.

### 2512.09335 — Relightable and Dynamic Gaussian Avatar Reconstruction from Monocular Video

RnD-Avatar (Choi et al., Yonsei/Monash/NTU) addresses two joint limitations of monocular 3DGS avatars: static (pose-independent) skinning weights fail to capture motion-dependent deformation like clothing wrinkles, and sparse monocular visual cues under-constrain geometry. Its core contribution is a "dynamic skinning weight" encoder $\mathcal{D}_\mathcal{W}$ that computes skinning weights conditioned jointly on Gaussian position and a windowed pose sequence $\Theta_t=\{\theta_{t-d},...,\theta_t\}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times J\times 3}$, rather than the usual static/position-only weights: a temporal-attention branch over reshaped per-joint motion produces a temporal feature, a spatial-attention branch over the joint-to-joint pose difference $\theta_t-\theta_{t-1}$ produces a local-motion feature, and cross-attention with the Gaussian's position feature as query fuses both into $\mathcal{W}_{\Theta_t}$, giving pose-driven rotation/translation $\mathcal{A}_R,\mathcal{A}_T$ that deform position, rotation, and normal ($x'=A_R(x_c+\Delta x(\theta_t))+A_T$, etc., with $\Delta x,\Delta r$ from small MLPs for non-rigid residuals). Each Gaussian carries separate geometry attributes $(o,r,s,n)$ and appearance/material attributes (SH color, albedo $c_a$, roughness $\gamma$) predicted by two MLP sub-encoders from position alone, and the avatar is shaded via a discrete physically-based rendering sum $L_o=\sum_{\omega_i}L_i f(\omega_i,\omega_o)(\omega_i\cdot n)\Delta\omega_i$ using a Disney BRDF and SH-parameterized, view-dependent visibility (metallic fixed to 0), with environment light stored as a learnable 32×64×1 latitude-longitude probe. Training is two-stage (geometry-only reconstruction+normal loss, then joint PBR+normal+an InfoNCE-based "geometric consistency loss" that maximizes feature similarity between the training view and a randomly rendered virtual view to combat monocular depth ambiguity). On the authors' new 20-subject/30-view/8-lighting-color multiview dataset (11.5M frames, 4096p) it reaches PSNR 31.92/SSIM 0.9621 novel-view reconstruction and PSNR 26.94/SSIM 0.9509 on rendered normals, beating 3DGS-Avatar, GauHuman, GoMAvatar, iHuman, ExAvatar, and EVA on every metric, and PSNR 30.78 on relighting versus 30.52 (NECA) — notably its normal-map PSNR (26.94) is far ahead of the relighting-specialized baselines (15–18), indicating the geometric-consistency regularizer materially improves normals from monocular input alone. For TAYF this is a human-representation-stage technique offering monocular-only capture (matching a single-cube-camera constraint) plus environment-relightable output, meaning the reconstructed remote avatar could in principle be shaded consistently with the receiving cube's local lighting rather than baking in the sender's studio lighting; the caveat is PBR ray-tracing-style visibility/shading per Gaussian is computationally heavier than plain SH color lookup, so real-time on-device relighting at cube power budgets is unproven, and it needs a dedicated per-subject training pass (unspecified duration but implied non-trivial, multi-hour given "TR" columns of 2-12h for comparable methods) rather than an instant enrollment.

### 2601.07518 — Mon3tr: Monocular 3D Telepresence with Pre-built Gaussian Avatars as Amortization

Mon3tr (Lin et al., HKUST) is arguably the single most directly relevant paper in this batch: an end-to-end monocular 3D telepresence system built and measured on real consumer hardware (PC sender with RealSense RGB camera, Meta Quest 3 receiver), explicitly reporting bandwidth, latency, and FPS. Its core idea is "amortized computation": a one-time offline multi-view capture (~1-2 min video) builds a personalized, high-fidelity 3DGS avatar bound to a new parametric mesh template (SPMM3 — a hybrid topology fusing a scanned body mesh with FLAME face and MANO hand components via rigid alignment, $M_\text{SPMM3}=\mathcal{U}(M_\text{body}^\text{masked}, \mathcal{A}_f(M_\text{face}), \mathcal{A}_h(M_\text{hand}))$, skinning-weight-transferred from SMPL-X); at runtime only compact per-frame driving parameters (body pose $\theta_{b,t}\in\mathbb{R}^{75}$, facial expression $\psi_t\in\mathbb{R}^{50}$, hand pose $\theta_{h,t}\in\mathbb{R}^{90}$, ~215 floats total) are extracted by parallel monocular estimators (GVHMR/HaMeR/SMIRK) and streamed over WebRTC data channel after FP16+LZ4 compression. On the receiver, a lightweight mesh deformation network $\mathcal{F}_\text{mesh}$ predicts non-rigid vertex offsets (posed via LBS), and an attribute deformation network $\mathcal{F}_\text{attr}$ models Gaussian-attribute corrections via a physically-inspired "tension field": ~500 local controllers sampled on the canonical mesh each map pose to a displacement potential, a virtual-mass-weighted (geodesic-distance × skinning-weight-similarity) sum of the $K=3$ nearest controllers' potentials gives each Gaussian a "dragging force" that is projected onto a fixed set of linear deformation bases — conceptually similar to AGORA-M's SVD blendshapes but derived from a physical analogy rather than PCA. Measured numbers: <0.2 Mbps transmitted bitrate, ~80 ms end-to-end latency, ~60 FPS rendering on Quest 3 (NPU handles inference/LBS, GPU handles sorting+rendering, split by hardware role), >1000× bandwidth reduction vs. point-cloud streaming, >28 dB PSNR novel-pose / >32 dB novel-view, and a one-time ~33 s avatar-build/download step; a full sender-side latency breakdown shows facial expression extraction at 377 FPS, body pose at 73.6 FPS, hand gesture at 71.2 FPS, with the parallel pipeline synchronizing to 58.2 FPS overall (13.78 ms "worker execution," 2.13 ms sync, 1.27 ms smoothing). For TAYF this paper is close to a full-stack existence proof for the entire capture→representation→transport→render loop the cube needs to replicate, with real numbers for every stage; its explicit table comparing itself to Holoportation/Project Starline/VirtualCube/MetaStream/MagicStream/TeleAloha on cost, bandwidth, and monocular-capability is a ready-made competitive landscape. The core caveat for a 10cm cube: the sender needs an RTX 5090-class GPU for the offline avatar build (33s per user, presumably with much heavier compute than a battery-powered embedded device could sustain) and the receiver is a full Snapdragon XR2-class headset SoC, not a thermally-constrained cube; the amortization strategy itself (heavy one-time build, cheap per-frame driving) is exactly right for TAYF's power budget, but the specific model sizes would need aggressive further compression to fit an embedded NPU.

### 2605.02086 — GETA-3DGS: Automatic Joint Structured Pruning and Quantization for 3D Gaussian Splatting

GETA-3DGS (Zhang & Sui) adapts the GETA joint pruning-quantization framework (originally for CNNs/Transformers) to 3DGS scene compression, targeting the two-orders-of-magnitude gap between typical 3DGS storage (500MB-several GB for 1-5M Gaussians) and mobile/AR/volumetric-streaming budgets (tens of MB, a few Mbps/frame). Each Gaussian is treated as a "group" node in a quantization-aware dependency graph (QADG) with five heterogeneous attribute sub-nodes (position $\mu\in\mathbb{R}^3$, log-scale $s\in\mathbb{R}^3$, rotation quaternion $q\in\mathbb{R}^4$, opacity logit $\alpha$, and degree-aware SH coefficients $c\in\mathbb{R}^{(\ell+1)^2\times3}$, 48 scalars at $\ell=3$), rather than the generic weight-tensor grouping GETA uses for DNNs. Pruning uses a "render-aware saliency" fusing $\alpha$-blending transmittance-weighted contribution, screen-space gradient magnitude, and pixel coverage into one Gaussian-level importance score — replacing the parameter-space Taylor-expansion saliency of vanilla GETA, which the authors note is a poor signal for 3DGS since occluded/sub-pixel Gaussians can carry non-trivial gradients despite negligible visual contribution. Quantization is heterogeneous per-attribute (independent bit-widths for position/rotation/scale/opacity/each SH degree), jointly optimized with structural sparsity under a "projected partial saliency-guided" (PPSG) descent rule that enforces hard storage/bit-width budgets rather than soft heuristic thresholds. On Mip-NeRF 360, Tanks & Temples, and Deep Blending, the method achieves ~5× storage reduction over vanilla 3DGS fully automatically (no per-scene opacity/scale/SH-degree tuning); the key empirical finding, backed by an information-theoretic reverse-water-filling analysis, is that the heterogeneous bit-width *policy* is the dominant rate-distortion lever, not the saliency choice or schedule: forcing a uniform 6-bit cap costs up to -6.74 dB on view-dependent scenes (counter, room) versus only -0.18 to -0.34 dB on texture-uniform outdoor scenes, and the predicted per-attribute bit ordering from the water-filling model matches empirically converged bit-widths within ±1 bit. GETA-3DGS is positioned as complementary to entropy coders like HAC++/CompGS (which operate downstream on already-quantized symbols), so the two can compose. For TAYF this is a transport/compression-stage tool applicable to whatever static or per-keyframe Gaussian content the cube needs to store or transmit (e.g., a canonical avatar payload, background scene Gaussians, or an enrollment snapshot): the finding that view-dependent (SH-heavy) content needs far more bits than flat regions directly informs how to budget bits between a person's face/skin (high SH detail, needs care) versus clothing/background (safe to quantize aggressively); the caveat is this compresses a static/single scene's Gaussians, not a temporal avatar-driving stream, so it addresses model footprint (fits-on-device / one-time transfer) rather than the per-frame animation bandwidth Mon3tr and the 2510.10492 paper target.

### 2212.02469 — One-shot Implicit Animatable Avatars with Model-based Priors (ELICIT)

ELICIT (method name inferred from macro \mn) tackles single-image animatable human avatar reconstruction — the most extreme sparse-capture case relevant to a monocular telepresence cube — by combining a HumanNeRF-style canonical radiance field warped via SMPL-conditioned motion field $F_o(x,p)=F_c(\mathcal{T}(x,p))$ with two model-based priors to compensate for having only one training view. A "visual model-based semantic prior" uses CLIP ViT cosine-similarity loss $\mathcal{L}_\text{CLIP}=\phi(I_\text{ref})^\top\phi(\hat I_\text{train})$ between rendered novel-view/pose images and reference images to enforce semantic (not pixel) consistency for unseen viewpoints, additionally allowing text-prompt guidance for garment regions never seen in the input image (found necessary but insufficient alone for identity preservation — image-based loss is still needed for faces/textures). An "SMPL-based geometric prior" (a) initializes the NeRF via multi-view renderings of a template SMPL mesh (rendered at $m$ preset camera poses with the target motion sequence) used as pseudo-ground-truth pretraining targets, avoiding the representation degeneration that results from directly optimizing NeRF on one image, and (b) applies a soft silhouette-based geometry constraint restricted to the region actually covered by the projected SMPL mask, combining an MSE term and a one-way Chamfer boundary term. A body-part-aware patch sampling strategy trains local CLIP-embedding losses per body region (rather than global-image CLIP, which is resolution-limited) and a rotation-aware sampling scheme substitutes the input view with a nearest side/front view as reference when training heavily-occluded rear/side viewpoints, to avoid the "multi-face" artifact common to CLIP-guided 3D generation. Evaluated on ZJU-MoCap and Human3.6M against per-subject NeuralBody/AnimatableNeRF and generalizable NeuralHumanPerformer (all adapted to single-image input for fairness), ELICIT outperforms on PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS for both novel-view and novel-pose synthesis, with the paper particularly highlighting SSIM/LPIPS gains as evidence of better perceptual quality on occluded/unseen regions. For TAYF this is directly relevant to the capture stage under the cube's realistic constraint of very sparse (potentially single-camera) input: it demonstrates that CLIP-semantic supervision plus SMPL geometric priors can plausibly hallucinate unseen body regions (backs, occluded limbs) from one photo, which matters for any enrollment flow that can't guarantee full 360° multi-view capture; the caveat is this is NeRF/MLP-based (not Gaussian), meaning render speed is far from real-time, and the "hallucinated" unseen-region content is by construction not the person's true appearance — acceptable for a stand-in but a risk for identity fidelity in a telepresence context where accuracy matters more than plausibility.

### 2311.16096 — Animatable and Relightable Gaussians for High-fidelity Human Avatar Modeling

This paper (Peking University / Tencent-adjacent based on citations) proposes what is essentially the multi-view, high-fidelity precursor to the monocular relightable avatar line: rather than an MLP mapping canonical points to Gaussian attributes (which the authors argue suffers a low-frequency spectral bias, citing Fourier-feature literature), it replaces the MLP with 2D StyleGAN-based CNNs (StyleUNet) operating on pose-conditioned position maps. A canonical SDF+color template is first fit from multi-view video at a near-A-pose frame via SMPL-guided volume rendering and root-finding correspondence (Gauss-Newton, custom CUDA kernel, instead of the slower Broyden's method used in SNARF/ARAH), then skinning weights are diffused from SMPL vertices through the volume. For animation, the template is posed via LBS and orthographically projected to front/back canonical views to produce "posed position maps" that serve as pose conditioning; five separate StyleUNets then predict, per-pixel (each pixel = one 3D Gaussian), color, position-offset, other Gaussian attributes (rotation/scale/opacity), plus albedo/roughness and normal/visibility maps for physically-based rendering — enabling both animation and full relighting from the same predicted attribute maps via the discretized rendering equation with visibility encoded as 16-D spherical-harmonic coefficients (predicted at train time via ray tracing, replaced at test time by direct point-based ray tracing since a learned visibility predictor doesn't generalize to novel poses). A PCA-based pose-projection strategy clips novel driving poses to $\pm2\sigma$ of the training pose distribution's principal components before feeding the network, explicitly to keep out-of-distribution test poses (e.g., from AMASS) within the network's learned manifold. Evaluated on THuman4.0, AvatarReX, and ActorsHQ (up to 160 camera views, though only 47 used), the CNN-based Gaussian-map approach outperforms NeRF-based body-only baselines (TAVA, ARAH, SLRF, PoseVocab) qualitatively and quantitatively; rendering is fast at 0.13s/frame for animation only, degrading to 4-10s/frame when relighting is enabled due to per-Gaussian visibility ray tracing. For TAYF this shows a route to modeling clothing wrinkles and view-dependent shading detail beyond what per-Gaussian MLP decoders achieve, at the cost of requiring dense multi-view capture (up to 160 cameras) for the template — infeasible for a single 10cm cube's sensor suite — and the relighting mode's multi-second-per-frame cost is far outside any real-time telepresence budget, making this more relevant as an offline enrollment-quality reference than an on-cube runtime technique.

### 2311.17910 — HUGS: Human Gaussian Splats

HUGS (Kocabas et al.) reconstructs both a moving human and the static background scene as separately-optimized, disentangleable sets of 3D Gaussians from a single monocular video with camera motion (not a fixed camera), initializing human Gaussians from an upsampled SMPL mesh (110,210 vertices) and scene Gaussians from COLMAP SfM points. Rather than using SMPL's fixed LBS weights, HUGS represents the human in a canonical Da-pose space via a feature triplane $F\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times h\times w\times d}$ and three per-Gaussian MLPs: an appearance decoder (RGB + opacity), a geometry decoder (position offset $\Delta\mu$, rotation, scale), and a deformation decoder that outputs *learned* LBS weights $W_i\in\mathbb{R}^{n_\text{joints}}$ for each Gaussian — regularized toward the SMPL-interpolated weights of its 6 nearest mesh vertices via an $\ell_2$ loss, $\mathcal{L}_\text{LBS}=\|W-\hat W\|_F^2$, so the learned weights can deviate from SMPL to better fit hair/clothing while staying anchored. Optimization uses combined L1+SSIM+VGG-perceptual losses on both the full scene and a human-only masked render (SAM-segmented), with per-image SMPL pose parameters also refined during training since off-the-shelf regressor estimates are imperfect. Crucially, after optimization the triplane+MLPs never need to be evaluated again at animation/render time — the Gaussians and their learned LBS weights are extracted explicitly, so new poses are rendered by direct LBS deformation of pre-baked Gaussian attributes, avoiding any neural inference in the render loop. On NeuMan and ZJU-MoCap, HUGS beats NeRF-based NeuMan and implicit-SDF-based Vid2Avatar on nearly every PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS metric while training 96× faster than Vid2Avatar and 336× faster than NeuMan (within 30 minutes), and rendering at 60 FPS — roughly 3800-7600× faster than the NeRF/implicit baselines — on a single RTX 3090Ti; an ablation confirms removing the learned LBS causes floating-Gaussian artifacts at body corners, and removing the human-region loss degrades fine details like shoes. For TAYF, HUGS's "bake out the network, animate with pure LBS" design is exactly the right computational shape for an embedded receiver: zero neural inference cost during real-time playback, only matrix-vector LBS math per Gaussian, directly compatible with the amortized-computation pattern seen in Mon3tr and the compression paper (2510.10492); the human/scene disentanglement is also relevant since a telepresence cube likely wants to render the remote person independent of whatever background exists at the receiving end. The caveat is per-subject optimization from a single video (30 min) is still an offline enrollment step, not real-time capture, and the 200K-Gaussian-per-human average avatar size is well above the compact parametric payloads needed for live bandwidth-constrained streaming.

### 2405.14866 — Tele-Aloha: A Low-budget and High-authenticity Telepresence System Using Sparse RGB Cameras

Tele-Aloha is a full hardware+software 3D telepresence system (peer to Mon3tr but from an earlier academic line, referenced by Mon3tr's own comparison table at ~$15,000 cost) using only 4 hardware-synchronized RGB cameras (BFS-U3-123S6C-C, 4096×3000 @ 30Hz, no depth sensors) arranged as two stereo pairs of different baselines (narrow pair on top for robust matching, wide pair below for larger FOV coverage) around an autostereoscopic, eye-tracked display. Novel-view synthesis avoids full 3D reconstruction: a "Cascaded Disparity Estimation" runs RAFT-stereo on the narrow-baseline pair first with zero initialization, then projects the resulting depth as a 3D point cloud and rasterizes it into the wide-baseline pair's viewpoints to seed their disparity estimation with a much better initialization, stabilizing what would otherwise be an ill-posed wide-baseline stereo problem — the paper shows the resulting depth is qualitatively competitive with a $1500 ToF sensor and actually more complete around clothing logos/hair/reflective objects. The three resulting depth maps are unprojected into "latent-based" Gaussian points (position, learned latent appearance feature instead of RGB, scale, opacity, identity rotation) via a shared-backbone image encoder, differentiably rasterized to the two eye-viewpoint targets via 3DGS, and decoded to RGB by a 2D UNet at reduced resolution (1024²) trained to inpaint occluded/sparse regions; an "occlusion-aware rendering refinement" stage then fuses this inpainted low-res render with a separately blended high-resolution image (built by warping all input views into the novel viewpoint and weighting by mask validity × an SDF-based occlusion check × cosine of viewing-angle-vs-surface-normal × inverse distance) through a final lightweight decoder, producing full 2048×2048 output in under 25ms on one RTX 4090. System-level numbers: H.265 encoding of all 4 cropped/concatenated camera streams into a single 6000×6000 NVENC input, WebRTC transport at a measured 100 Mbit/s, 3D eye tracking via a BlazeFace variant for view-dependent rendering, and an end-to-end system latency under 150ms with >30 FPS sustained on a single consumer GPU. For TAYF this is a highly relevant capture+display-adjacent reference for a compact multi-camera rig: the cascaded-stereo trick shows how a small number of unsynchronized-baseline RGB cameras (which would fit on cube faces) can produce usable depth without expensive ToF/structured-light sensors, and the whole novel-view pipeline runs in real time on one GPU rather than requiring per-subject offline optimization (unlike the avatar papers above) — though the reported 100 Mbit/s bandwidth is two orders of magnitude above the sub-1 Mbps budgets of the parametric-avatar approaches, since Tele-Aloha streams raw multi-camera video rather than compact semantic state, making it a poor fit for TAYF's transport stage as-is but a strong fit for the capture/geometry-estimation stage if paired with a downstream compact representation.

### 2210.12003 — HDHumans: A Hybrid Approach for High-fidelity Digital Humans

HDHumans (Habermann et al., ACM ToG/SCA 2023) fuses an explicit deformable mesh with a NeRF to get both space-time-coherent geometry and photoreal appearance for full-body performance capture. A pretrained embedded-deformation network $f_\mathrm{eg}$ (graph nodes $\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times3}$) plus a per-vertex displacement network $f_\mathrm{delta}$ deform a person-specific template from a 3-frame skeletal motion window; a vid2vid texture network converts posed normal maps (1024×1024) into dynamic cloth-wrinkle textures $\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{tex},t}$, encoded by a UNet into a 32-channel feature texture fed to the NeRF. The key efficiency trick is geometry-guided NeRF sampling: ray sample depth $t_i^r(\mathbf{V})$ is computed from the rendered mesh depth map with 9×9 erosion/dilation and a fixed 4 cm band ($t_\mathrm{mi}=t_\mathrm{ma}$) around the surface, letting the model use only 32 samples/ray vs. NeRF's 64+128 coarse/fine — a 6× sampling-efficiency gain. A NeRF-guided point-cloud loss ($\lambda_\mathrm{pc}=5000$) then refines the mesh itself, closing the loop between the two representations. On the DynaCap dataset (5 actors, up to 4112×3008 imagery) it beats prior deformable-character baseline DDC on Chamfer/Hausdorff reconstruction error (1.01/0.89 cm vs 1.27/1.19 cm) and deformation-generation error (1.24/1.15 cm vs 1.43/1.38 cm), training in stages up to 1.5M iterations at 4K. For TAYF this is a capture/representation-stage technique showing how a persistent template + small per-frame network output (motion window, not full geometry) can drive both photoreal appearance and a coherent deformable mesh simultaneously — directly relevant to separating "persistent avatar" from "dynamic state" — but the mesh-guided sampling still needs a NeRF MLP evaluation per ray at render time, and the multi-stage multi-day training pipeline (2M+500k+1.5M iterations) is far from the online, single-cube enrollment budget TAYF would need.

### 2211.14589 — AvatarGen: A 3D Generative Model for Animatable Human Avatars

AvatarGen learns a 3D-aware GAN for clothed humans that is directly SMPL-pose/shape controllable while training only from unstructured 2D fashion images (no multi-view or 3D scans). It decomposes generation into pose-guided canonical mapping (inverse LBS $T_\mathrm{IS}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{p})=\sum_j s_j(R_j\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{t}_j)$ plus a residual-deformation MLP for wrinkles) and canonical tri-plane generation (EG3D-style), and replaces the usual density field with a signed-distance field: $\sigma_i = \alpha^{-1}\mathrm{Sigmoid}(-d_i/\alpha)$, regularized by a geometric-prior loss pulling the predicted SDF toward the SMPL body's own SDF with a Gaussian proximity weight $w(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{p})=\exp(-d_o(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{p})^2/\kappa)$, plus Eikonal and minimal-surface losses. Trained with dual (raw + super-resolved) discriminators and an extra face-crop discriminator over an 80²-pixel head region on 512² images, it beats EG3D/StyleSDF/StyleNeRF/ENARF-GAN/EVA3D on four fashion datasets by 51–54% relative FID (e.g., 5.25 vs EG3D's 15.44 on MPV) and reaches PCK up to 99.5% for pose controllability. For TAYF, this is a human-representation-stage method for the "avatar enrollment from images, then drive by SMPL pose" pattern rather than raw multi-view capture, but it's a generative model trained on population data to produce plausible unseen identities/poses, not a per-user photoreal reconstruction pipeline — useful mainly as a design pattern (SMPL-anchored SDF geometry proxy + tri-plane appearance) rather than a directly deployable capture method, and GAN training/inference at 512² still implies non-trivial per-frame compute unsuited to a 10cm embedded device without major distillation.

### 2303.08808 — Mesh Strikes Back: Fast and Efficient Human Reconstruction from RGB videos

This paper shows an explicit SMPL+D mesh (no per-scene subdivision needed) plus a learned 3D texture representation can match/beat NeRF-based human avatars while rendering far faster. The core insight is a "toy problem": a cube and a dented cube produce identical binary silhouettes from every view (visual-hull ambiguity), but multi-view RGB consistency (the same trick NeRF exploits) disambiguates concave geometry — so the method assigns learnable per-vertex 3D texture coordinates fed into a multi-resolution hash encoding (Instant-NGP-style) instead of fixed UV maps, letting texture capacity adaptively concentrate on high-frequency regions (faces, logos) vs. flat clothing. Rendering uses NMR for opaque images (texture gradients) and SoftRas for translucent images (pose gradients, since SoftRas backprop to occluded/far vertices is what enables pose refinement), combined in a two-stage optimization (stage 1: fixed per-face color for coarse alignment; stage 2: fix geometry, train texture net + per-frame pose) that avoids the "moving target" problem of jointly optimizing deformation and texture. Losses include an IOU silhouette loss, HRNet-derived keypoint loss, a face-normal-consistency regularizer, and a novel area-preservation loss $\mathcal{L}_{FA}=\sum_j(A_{f_j}/A_{f'_j} + A_{f'_j}/A_{f_j})$ chosen over L2 because it penalizes shrinkage/expansion symmetrically. On people-snapshot and ZJU-MoCap it is competitive with or beats AnimatableNeRF/NeuralBody on PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS for both novel-view and novel-pose synthesis (e.g., 27.25–29.61 dB PSNR on several subjects) while using an explicit, rasterizable mesh. For TAYF this is squarely a capture/representation-stage candidate: an explicit textured mesh is exactly the kind of lightweight, rasterizer-friendly (not ray-marched) asset a 10cm embedded GPU/display pipeline could actually drive in real time, and the hash-encoded 3D texture coordinate trick generalizes to compact appearance storage — but per-vertex mesh optimization from monocular video is still an offline enrollment process, not live capture.

### 2303.13777 — GM-NeRF: Learning Generalizable Model-based Neural Radiance Fields from Multi-view Images

GM-NeRF targets one-shot generalization to unseen identities/poses (no per-subject fine-tuning) by fusing SMPL geometric priors with multi-view image features through two attention mechanisms. A "structured geometric body embedding" computes, for each of the SMPL model's 6,890 vertices, a visibility-weighted attention over $m$ input views — query from the vertex normal, key/value from per-view pixel-aligned features concatenated with camera direction — so views whose camera direction is closest to the vertex normal contribute most; SparseConvNet then diffuses these per-vertex codes into a 3D feature volume $\mathcal{G}$, trilinearly interpolated to get a geometric code $\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})$ for any query point. A second "geometry-guided attention" registers an appearance code $\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{d})$ directly from multi-view features using the geometry code as the attention query, avoiding the blur caused by naive geometry/appearance misalignment when SMPL fitting is imprecise. The two codes feed an MLP producing $(\mathbf{f},\sigma)$, rendered by standard NeRF volume integration followed by a 2D CNN super-resolution/neural-rendering step (half-resolution feature image upsampled), with an added normal-consistency regularizer near the SMPL surface. Trained/evaluated on THuman2.0, Multi-Garment, GeneBody and ZJU-MoCap with PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS plus Chamfer/P2S geometry metrics, and made tractable via patch-based rendering with partial gradient backpropagation (only $n_p$ of $H_p/2\times W_p/2$ rays get gradients) to fit the VGG perceptual loss in memory. For TAYF, GM-NeRF is a capture-stage generalizable prior: because it needs no per-subject optimization, it's the right shape for "capture whoever steps in front of the cube instantly," but it still requires $m\ge4$ calibrated multi-view cameras and full NeRF volume-rendering at inference — expensive for a single small enclosure with limited camera baseline and no GPU-class ray-marching budget.

### 2304.00916 — DreamAvatar: Text-and-Shape Guided 3D Human Avatar Generation via Diffusion Models

DreamAvatar generates a posable 3D human avatar from a text prompt and target SMPL pose by distilling a pretrained 2D diffusion model into a NeRF via Variational Score Distillation (VSD), $\nabla_\theta\mathcal{L}_\mathrm{VSD}=\mathbb{E}_{t,\epsilon,c}[\omega(t)(\epsilon_\mathrm{pre}(x_t,t,y)-\epsilon_\phi(x_t,t,c,y))\,\partial g(\theta,c)/\partial\theta]$, where $\epsilon_\phi$ is a LoRA-adapted copy of the pretrained denoiser conditioned additionally on camera pose. Its key architectural idea is "dual observation spaces": the same NeRF is queried in both a canonical A-pose space and the target posed space simultaneously, each point's density initialized from an SMPL-surface signed-distance prior converted via $\bar\sigma = \max(0,\mathrm{softplus}^{-1}(a^{-1}\mathrm{sigmoid}(-d/a)))$ (a=0.001), with points related by an inverse-LBS-plus-MLP deformation field $\hat{\mathbf{x}}_c = \mathcal{G}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_p + \mathrm{MLP}_{\theta_\mathrm{NR}}(\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_p))$; canonical-space optimization gives complete (unoccluded) texture while posed-space optimization keeps geometry faithful to the requested pose, and both are jointly VSD-optimized. A second SDS loss is computed on a zoomed-in rendered head region using a landmark-conditioned ControlNet and a learned <back-view> token specifically to fight the multi-face "Janus" problem. This is a human-representation-stage generative tool rather than a live-capture one: it is for synthesizing a driveable canonical avatar from a description (or as an avatar prior/regularizer), so its relevance to TAYF is mainly as a possible "avatar bootstrapping without a scan" enrollment path — full VSD optimization at diffusion-model cost, however, is far too slow (many diffusion denoising steps per training iteration) for on-cube or even on-phone processing and would need to be a cloud pre-processing step, not part of the real-time pipeline.

### 2307.10173 — DNA-Rendering: A Diverse Neural Actor Repository for High-Fidelity Human-centric Rendering

DNA-Rendering is a dataset/benchmark paper, not a method, but its capture-hardware specs and scale are directly informative for what "good" multi-view human capture infrastructure looks like. A 3-meter-radius dome houses 68 cameras — 12 cameras at 12MP (4096×3000) with 6mm lenses, 48 industrial cameras at 5MP (2448×2048) with 8mm lenses arranged in three height rings at 22.5°/60° angular intervals, and 8 Azure Kinect RGB-D units for coarse depth reference — hardware-triggered and synchronized to <2ms across industrial cameras (1.12ms theoretical for the Kinect chain), recording 15fps under a 16-light, 4500 lux/m² uniform illumination rig. Across 500 actors × 3 outfits × ≥9 motions they collected 5,000 sequences / 67.5M frames with automated calibration, matting, 2D/3D landmark, and SMPL-X fitting pipelines, benchmarking novel-view, novel-pose, and novel-identity synthesis with easy/medium/hard splits stratified by cloth looseness, texture complexity, motion difficulty, and human-object interaction. For TAYF this is not a deployable pipeline component but a ground-truth reference for how far a 10cm single- or few-camera cube capture rig is from studio-grade multi-view human capture (68 synchronized cameras vs. TAYF's handful at best) — it usefully calibrates expectations for how much geometric/appearance detail (hair, wrinkles, loose cloth) is realistically recoverable from a minimal onboard sensor suite, and its curated hard-cases taxonomy (loose clothing, complex texture) is a good source of stress-test scenarios for whatever generalizable one-/few-shot capture method TAYF ultimately adopts.

### 2311.03140 — Animating NeRFs from Texture Space: A Framework for Pose-Dependent Rendering of Human Performances

This SCITEPRESS paper (Fraunhofer HHI) proposes UVH-NeRF, which warps NeRF's query space onto a compact surface-aligned $uvh$ coordinate (2D SMPL texture coordinates $u,v$ plus signed height $h$ to the mesh surface, found via "dispersed projection" rather than nearest-point projection to avoid ambiguity near mesh edges), so the same coordinate space is shared and persistent across all frames/poses of a subject regardless of articulation. A learned per-frame pose embedding $E_\mathrm{pose}$ feeds a small remapping subnetwork that predicts an offset $\Delta uvh = F_\psi(uvh, E_\mathrm{pose})$ correcting for SMPL-fitting inaccuracies before the coordinate is fed into Instant-NGP-style multiresolution hash encoding ($L=16$ levels, $F=4$ features/level) — meaning this work directly reuses the fast-training grid encoder but keyed on surface coordinates instead of world coordinates. A NeRF-ResNet (5 residual blocks, width 64) replaces the density MLP to better absorb pose-conditioned appearance changes (cloth folds) given the encoded pose as a residual input. Trained on a 16-camera custom rig plus ZJU-MoCap, it reaches 100k training steps in <12 hours on an RTX 3090, renders 1024×1024 images in ~25s (unoptimized), and achieves 28.64 dB PSNR on held-out novel views but drops to 19.16 dB on genuinely novel poses (10 frames from distinct pose clusters), illustrating the generalization gap even with a compact surface-anchored representation. For TAYF this is a human-representation-stage technique showing how to keep a NeRF's coordinate space stable under body motion — relevant to a persistent-avatar architecture — but its 25-second-per-frame render time (even if "unoptimized") and multi-hour per-subject training are both orders of magnitude away from real-time, battery-constrained cube operation.

### 2311.05521 — BakedAvatar: Baking Neural Fields for Real-Time Head Avatar Synthesis

BakedAvatar converts an implicit 4D head-avatar NeRF into layered, rasterizable meshes for genuinely real-time mobile rendering — the single most directly deployable paper in this batch for TAYF's constrained hardware. Stage 1 learns three continuous fields from monocular video: a canonical "manifold field" $\mathcal{M}:\mathbf{x}_c\to s$ whose predefined level sets $\{l_i\}$ define 8 nested isosurfaces (levels sampled from −0.2 to 0) that substitute for NeRF ray-marching; a FLAME-based forward deformer predicting per-point blendshape/pose/LBS-weight fields $\mathcal{D}:\mathbf{x}_c\to(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{W})$ with $n_e=50$ expression and $n_p=4$ pose correctives; and a radiance field producing $n_T=16$ linearly-blendable texture bases plus an 8-channel position feature, blended per-pixel by a tiny hyper-network split into a per-frame global MLP (θ,ψ→spatial-MLP weights) and a per-pixel spatial MLP (position feature, normal, view→blend weights) — this hyper-network split is what makes inference cheap, since the expensive global MLP runs once per frame, not once per ray. Stage 2 bakes these fields via Marching Cubes + Quadric Edge Collapse Decimation into ~10k-face meshes per layer with 1024×1024 baked RGBA texture atlases (8-bit quantized); Stage 3 fine-tunes only the textures (geometry frozen) with nvdiffrast differentiable rasterization plus a VGG perceptual loss. The payoff: a WebGL2 implementation renders 512×512 at 239/189 FPS (plugged/battery) on an RTX 3060 Mobile laptop, 59 FPS on an iPad Pro, and 41 FPS on a OnePlus 11 phone — with quality comparable to PointAvatar/NHA/IMavatar/NerFace (PSNR 28.66 dB, LPIPS 0.057 after fine-tuning) and measured superior FPS/Watt energy efficiency versus those baselines. For TAYF this is a display/render-stage blueprint: it demonstrates that a neurally-trained dynamic head representation can be distilled into a fixed-topology multi-layer mesh + texture atlas that a phone-class GPU renders at 40+ FPS, which is exactly the kind of "bake once, drive cheaply at runtime" architecture a 10cm cube's onboard compute would need — though it is head-only (no body/hands) and per-subject training still takes ~1 day on an RTX 3090, so it fits the "enrollment once, cheap runtime" pattern rather than one-shot capture.

### 2311.17917 — AvatarStudio: High-fidelity and Animatable 3D Avatar Creation from Text

AvatarStudio generates a high-resolution, SMPL-animatable clothed-human avatar from text alone via a coarse-to-fine pipeline: a coarse stage trains an Instant-NGP-style hash-grid NeRF (density initialized from an SMPL-derived residual field, as in DreamAvatar) under a low-resolution diffusion prior, then a fine stage converts it to an explicit mesh via Deep Marching Tetrahedra (DMTet), initialized from the coarse NeRF's marching-cubes mesh and optimized under a higher-resolution (512×512) diffusion prior using a fast differentiable rasterizer instead of volume rendering — the explicit mesh is what unlocks the resolution jump, since volumetric SDS at 512² is prohibitively memory-heavy. Its main novelty is DensePose-conditioned Score Distillation Sampling: instead of sparse skeleton keypoints (used by prior ControlNet-guided avatar methods and prone to the Janus multi-face problem), it conditions the guiding ControlNet on a rendered dense SMPL-surface correspondence map $\mathcal{I}_\mathrm{cond}$, giving 3D-aware, view-consistent pose control: $\nabla_\theta\mathcal{L}_\mathrm{SDS}=\mathbb{E}_{t,\epsilon}[\omega(t)(\hat\epsilon_\phi(\mathcal{I}_t;y,\mathcal{I}_\mathrm{cond},t)-\epsilon)\,\partial\mathcal{I}/\partial\eta]$. It further adds part-aware super-resolution (zooming into head/hands/upper/lower body with part-specific text prompts to refine local detail), dual-space (canonical A-pose + randomly VPoser-sampled deformed-space) training for animation generalization, and CFG rescaling to fight the color oversaturation typical of high-guidance-scale SDS. Like DreamAvatar, this is a text-to-avatar generative tool relevant to TAYF only as a possible offline avatar-bootstrapping/enrollment method (e.g., "describe or lightly scan yourself, get a driveable mesh") rather than a live-capture pipeline stage — its DensePose-conditioning idea, however, is a transferable lesson: dense correspondence conditioning is more robust than sparse skeletons for any pose-guided generation/refinement TAYF might use downstream.

### 2312.02973 — GauHuman: Articulated Gaussian Splatting from Monocular Human Videos

GauHuman is a fast-training, real-time-rendering articulated 3D Gaussian Splatting method for monocular human video — highly relevant to TAYF's transport/render budget. It initializes 3D Gaussians at SMPL vertex positions in a canonical space, then deforms each Gaussian's mean and covariance to posed space via linear blend skinning, $\mathbf{p}^t=\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{J}^t,\theta^t)\mathbf{p}^c+\mathbf{b}(\cdot)$, $\Sigma^t=\mathbf{G}\Sigma^c\mathbf{G}^T$, where the LBS weights $w_k$ are the SMPL weights of the nearest vertex plus a learned offset from a small MLP, softmax-normalized, and body pose $\theta$ is itself corrected by a second small MLP ($\theta = \theta^\mathrm{SMPL}\otimes\mathrm{MLP}_{\Phi_\mathrm{pose}}(\theta)$) — both networks are only evaluated once per Gaussian, cached after training, so inference needs no MLP forward pass at all. The key efficiency contribution is regulating the Gaussian split/clone/merge/prune adaptive-control process with a closed-form KL divergence between nearby Gaussians, $KL(G_0\|G_1)=\tfrac12(\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_1^{-1}\Sigma_0)+\ln(\det\Sigma_1/\det\Sigma_0)+(\mathbf{p}_1-\mathbf{p}_0)^T\Sigma_1^{-1}(\mathbf{p}_1-\mathbf{p}_0)-3)$, computed only over k-NN neighbor pairs (reducing cost from $O(U^2)$ to $O(U)$) — Gaussians close in this KL sense are neither split/cloned (redundant) and a new merge operation collapses them, while SMPL-distance pruning removes stray points, together curbing the uncontrolled Gaussian-count blowup (up to 734MB) that plagues vanilla 3DGS in dynamic settings. Results on ZJU-MoCap/MonoCap: 31.34 dB PSNR (best among compared methods) with only 1-2 minutes of training and 189 FPS rendering on an RTX 3090 — versus 10 hours training / <2 FPS for NeuralBody/AnimatableNeRF/HumanNeRF and even InstantAvatar's 3-6 minute training only reaching 4.15/2.54 FPS. For TAYF this is a strong capture+representation-stage candidate: minute-scale enrollment and 189 FPS rendering on a desktop GPU point toward feasibility of on-device or edge rendering, and the tiny cached MLP outputs (no runtime network eval) are exactly the kind of "precompute once, stream cheaply" design the cube needs — though 189 FPS is measured on a full RTX 3090, so substantial further compression/quantization would be needed before this runs on embedded silicon.

### 2401.02620 — Progress and Prospects in 3D Generative AI: A Technical Overview including 3D human

This is a broad technical survey (not a novel method) cataloguing SMPL/SMPL-X-based text/image-to-avatar generation, 3D scene generation, and human motion synthesis circa 2023–2024, useful to TAYF mainly as a map of the current avatar-generation landscape and its concrete cost/quality tradeoffs. It contrasts iterative SDS-based avatar methods — DreamWaltz (SMPL-driven ControlNet + Stable Diffusion SDS, ~1 hour on an RTX 3090 for the model, 3s/frame for subsequent animation), HumanNorm (separate depth- and normal-adapted diffusion models with progressive low-to-high-frequency geometry generation via DMTet, ~2 hours on an RTX 3090 at 1024×1024 with the best reported facial fidelity in its comparisons), and DreamAvatar/TADA/TeCH — against non-iterative methods: GTA (single-image tri-plane avatar generation via cross-attention on a learnable query embedding, evaluated with Chamfer/P2S/Normals/PSNR), Chupa (SMPL-X + Stable Diffusion frontal-image generation, followed by Neural Deferred Shading and 36-view SDEdit-refined Neural Density Field reconstruction, 3 minutes on an RTX 3090 versus AvatarCLIP's 3 hours), and DreamHuman (NeRF avatar built on an imGHUM pose/shape base with "semantic zooming" onto faces/hands, combining density loss from imGHUM, normal/orientation loss from Ref-NeRF, and proposal-weight loss from mip-NeRF360). For TAYF the practical takeaway is a cost/quality ladder for offline avatar-enrollment options — non-iterative single/few-image methods (Chupa's 3 minutes) are the only ones remotely compatible with a "scan yourself once, get an avatar" onboarding flow, while iterative SDS methods (~1-2 hours per avatar on a desktop-class GPU) would have to run in the cloud, not on the cube; none of these approaches address real-time driving or transport, so the survey's relevance to TAYF is scoped strictly to the human-representation/enrollment stage rather than runtime.

<!-- SECTION2-END -->

## Track 3 — Compression, Transport & Latency (codecs, streaming, WebRTC)

### 2201.11795 — Neural JPEG: End-to-End Image Compression Leveraging a Standard JPEG Encoder-Decoder

Neural JPEG, by Mali, Ororbia, Kifer and Giles (Penn State/RIT), is an end-to-end differentiable image-compression system wrapped around the standard JPEG codec so that output bitstreams remain decodable by any standard JPEG decoder. A sparse multiplicative RNN (SM-RNN, K=3 refinement steps) driven by VGG-19 features pre-edits the DCT coefficients per 8x8 block before quantization, multiplying coefficients by learned sparse edit maps in [0,1], while a tied-weight SM-RNN post-edits on the decoder side to suppress artifacts. A learnable quantization table (luminance and chrominance, clipped to [1,255]) replaces the fixed one, and hard rounding is replaced by a differentiable third-order approximation. The loss combines distortion (MSE + gamma*LPIPS), rate (alpha*L1 of quantization tables + beta*mean of sparse maps) and an alignment term ((1-sigma)*MSE + sigma*MAE) with tunable weights. Training uses 3640 HDR images; evaluation on Kodak (24 images, 768x512) and DIV2K validation. At 0.37 bpp on Kodak, Neural JPEG reaches 29.52 dB PSNR (SSIM 0.8413) vs JPEG's 27.65 dB; at 0.38 bpp, 31.73 dB vs 31.22 dB; JPEG at 0.25 bpp equals Neural JPEG quality at 0.19 bpp, and color retention is improved. For the telepresence pipeline this fits the transport stage: a drop-in neural enhancement of standard JPEG that runs on plain consumer hardware while keeping compatibility with standard viewers — attractive for compressing captured imagery for transmission, royalty-free and codec-agnostic.

### 2202.00198 — Recognition-Aware Learned Image Compression

Recognition-Aware Learned Image Compression, by Kawawa-Beaudan, Roggenkemper and Zakhor (UC Berkeley), jointly trains a learned image codec with a recognition network so compressed images are optimized for machine consumption rather than human viewing. The codec follows EDIC (Liu et al.): a hierarchical autoencoder with 192-channel GDN convolutions, hyperprior latents, a two-Gaussian mixture entropy model and a factorized hyper-entropy model, trained with additive uniform noise quantization; the recognition head is EfficientNet-B0 (5.3M parameters, 78.8% ImageNet top-1). The joint loss is (1-lambda)*R(x) + lambda*D(x,xhat) + beta*L_t(y,yhat) with cross-entropy task loss; lambda in [0,1] couples rate and distortion, beta weights task accuracy. Training uses 500k ImageNet images (9 epochs, MSE distortion, dropout 0.2); evaluation on the 50k-image ILSVRC2012 validation set. With beta=0.2 the model achieves 66.82% accuracy at 0.43 BPP and 23.04 dB PSNR, versus 42.85% accuracy at 0.35 BPP and 25.57 dB with beta=0. Against BPG at matched low bitrates the joint model gains 16.28% accuracy (26.03% in the low-bitrate regime) while producing far lower-PSNR images (18.37 vs 25.22 dB). Dropout alone cuts bitrate from 0.289 to 0.192 BPP with +0.56% accuracy. For the telepresence pipeline this addresses the transport stage when imagery is consumed by perception networks: bits can be allocated to features the downstream tracker or recognizer needs, at the cost of visual quality.

### 2202.00719 — Point Cloud Compression for Efficient Data Broadcasting: A Performance Comparison

Point Cloud Compression for Efficient Data Broadcasting, by Varischio et al. (University of Padova), is an experimental comparison of compression strategies for LiDAR point clouds aimed at real-time broadcasting (e.g., vehicle-to-everything). It contrasts 3D-native codecs — Octree (PCL 1.10) and MPEG G-PCC 13 — with 2D approaches that first project the cloud to images: PNG, JPEG-LS, and video codecs LZW and MJ2 (MJPEG2000), using Cartesian or spherical coordinates. Quality is measured with BPP = S-bar/|P-bar| and point-to-plane PSNR (10 log10((theta*_P)^2/MSE), MSE = 1/|P| sum <p-q, n_q>^2), computed on Velodyne VLP-16 and HDL-32 data. Results: G-PCC achieves the best compression rate (98.74%) but is lossy (up to 74 dB below Octree in PSNR); PNG reaches ~80% savings (up to 25% better than Octree) while both 2D static and Octree HIGH profiles exceed 100 dB PSNR, i.e., essentially lossless; Octree HIGH needs 3x the BPP of PNG for +14% PSNR; LZW adds +11% over PNG via temporal correlation. Speed matters most: PNG compresses ~5.5M points/s versus 670k (Octree) and 440k (G-PCC) — only image-based methods keep pace with the HDL-32's 695k points/s capture rate; G-PCC and video codecs take tens to hundreds of seconds. For the telepresence cube this informs the transport stage for geometry data: projecting depth/point-cloud streams to spherical-coordinate images and compressing with PNG-class codecs gives near-lossless, real-time broadcasting with commodity codecs, whereas G-PCC trades accuracy for bitrate.

### 2202.04147 — The Rate-Distortion-Perception Tradeoff: The Role of Common Randomness

This paper characterizes the rate-distortion-perception (RDP) tradeoff when the decoder is allowed common randomness shared with the encoder at rate R_c. The setting generalizes the RDP problem of Blau and Michaeli: an encoder F: X^n x [2^{nR_c}] -> [2^{nR}] and decoder G: [2^{nR}] x [2^{nR_c}] -> Xhat^n minimize distortion D subject to a perceptual constraint (total variation between the marginal of Xhat^n and the source, or perfect realism, i.e., an exact match). The paper derives the achievable rate region as a function of R_c and D and shows how common randomness helps: it permits perfect realism at finite rates where a deterministic (R_c=0) code cannot, and it strictly reduces the required total rate for near-perfect realism. In the Gaussian case with squared-error distortion, perfect realism is achievable iff R >= (1/2) log2(1/(1-rho^2)) with rho the input-reconstruction correlation achievable via common randomness; without common randomness, imposing perfect realism incurs a 3 dB distortion penalty relative to the classical rate-distortion bound (distortion doubled at equal rate). The analysis distinguishes near-perfect from perfect realism, showing the former is achievable with finite R_c while the latter may require unbounded common randomness. For the telepresence cube this is transport-stage theory: it quantifies the bit budget needed if the decoder must render perceptually indistinguishable imagery (a realism constraint on reconstructed views), and shows a shared-seed or entropy-coded side channel can close part of the perceptual gap at modest overhead, guiding codec design for the free-space link.

### 2202.06533 — An Introduction to Neural Data Compression

An Introduction to Neural Data Compression, by Yang, Mandt and Theis, is a book-length tutorial (Foundations and Trends in Signal Processing, vol. 15, issue 2, 2023, pp. 113-200; doi 10.1561/0600000107) surveying classical and learned compression. It covers lossless coding (Huffman, arithmetic coding), rate-distortion theory, scalar and vector quantization, transform coding, and then the modern neural paradigm: rate-distortion variational autoencoders whose negative ELBO is shown to be a rate-distortion objective; the additive-uniform-noise surrogate that makes rounding differentiable; learned entropy models (factorized, hyperprior, autoregressive, latent-scalar-mixtures); side-information and bit-allocation considerations; and compression without quantization via bit-rate backpropagation. Perceptual metrics are treated through the rate-distortion-perception framework, and video compression is covered via low-latency online setups, including DVC-style learned video codecs and scale-space-flow motion compensation. Practical elements — how rates are computed from entropy models, rate control, and engineering tradeoffs — are discussed throughout, making the chapter a compact reference rather than a single new algorithm; headline experimental numbers are not the focus (no specific PSNR or fps claims are reported). For the telepresence cube this is a transport-stage reference: it consolidates the exact loss functions, entropy-model families, and differentiable-quantization tricks that a learned codec for the cube's video and geometry stream should use, and its R-D-P treatment connects to the perceptual requirements of the display stage. Its open-access structure suits onboarding engineers to neural codec design.

### 2203.01765 — Joint Optimization of Rate, Distortion, and Decoding Energy for HEVC Intraframe Coding

This paper extends HEVC intra-frame rate–distortion optimization to include decoder energy consumption, in addition to rate and distortion. The encoder minimizes a three-term Lagrangian J = D + λ_R·R + λ_E·Ê_dec, where Ê_dec is a per-block decoding-energy model calibrated from actual measurements on a TI Pandaboard running FFmpeg's HEVC decoder. Energy is modeled per slice and per block: prediction modes (e.g., MERGE) are favored because they skip transform/entropy work, and the multiplier λ_E is derived from QP as c_E·2^((QP−12)/3) with c_E = 0.57×10^7, calibrated so the energy and rate terms are commensurate. The paper also derives a transmission-energy model Ê_b = a·T_h⁻¹ + b with a = 305.3 and b = 13.1 nJ/bit. Experiments with HM-13.0 on HEVC test sequences show 5–17% decoding-energy savings at 6–24% bitrate increase, with the trade-off adjustable. For the telepresence cube this belongs to the transport stage: the cube is an energy-constrained edge device that must decode the remote user's video, so energy-aware bit allocation reduces battery drain without touching the decoder — only encoder decisions change, so the bitstream stays HEVC-compliant. Caution: the energy model is hardware-specific (Pandaboard) and must be re-calibrated for the cube's actual SoC, and the technique is limited to intra coding, which is a restriction for camera-captured avatar streams that rely mostly on inter prediction.
---

### 2203.03553 — Compression of user generated content using denoised references

This paper applies noisy source-coding theory to the compression of user-generated content (UGC) captured under non-ideal conditions (sensor noise, compression artifacts). The key result is that the optimal rate–distortion curve for UGC is D_ugc(R) = D0 + min E[||y − û||²] subject to I(u; û) ≤ R, where y = E[x|u] is the MMSE estimate of the clean signal (an optimal denoiser) and D0 is the irreducible distortion equal to the noise power. Practically, the encoder is a standard codec whose distortion is computed against a denoised reference z = D(u) produced by BM3D or BayesShrink rather than against the noisy original; blocks where encoding the noise yields no MSE improvement are flagged via an MSE-IQR saturation test to define a noise-encoding region Nc. Experiments on YouTube-UGC 360p Sport/Livemusic clips with JPEG (quality 14–90) show that by refusing to spend bits on unrepresentable noise, rate can be cut at equal perceived quality (validated with SSIM and MOS). For the telepresence cube this fits the transport stage: the capture side must transmit noisy depth/color data from small sensors, and applying this denoise-before-encode scheme wastes no bits on sensor noise while automatically setting a sensible rate ceiling for a given sensor. Compatibility: works with any standard codec; it adds a denoising pre-process and a block-level rate-saturation decision.
---

### 2203.04963 — Neural Data-Dependent Transform for Learned Image Compression

This paper proposes a learned image compression codec with a neural data-dependent transform: in addition to the usual content stream z_c, the encoder emits a neural-syntax stream z_s whose bits are decoded by a weight generator into the parameters of the decoder networks, so each image gets its own adaptive transform. The training loss is L = D(x, x̂) + λ(R(z_c) + R(z_s) + R(z_h)), i.e., the rates of content latents, syntax latents, and hyperprior are jointly optimized, with z_h the hyperprior for z_c. Continuous online finetuning further adapts the network per image. Configurations N=192/M=16 and N=384/M=32 are trained on DIV2K. Headline results on Kodak and CLIC: −14.5% BD-rate on Kodak and −25.3% on CLIC vs. BPG; a stronger variant with extra syntax reaches −20.1% (Kodak) and −29.7% (CLIC). Parameter overhead is about 1%, and online finetuning uses roughly 100 iterations of Adam at learning rate 1e-5. For the telepresence cube this serves the transport stage as an alternative neural codec for captured views of the remote user: content-adaptive transforms improve compression of the varied, user-generated content typical of telepresence, and the syntax stream is a clean way to ship per-frame model updates. Warnings: decoding requires a GPU and is not standard-compliant, so interoperability with generic players is lost; complexity is higher than BPG-style codecs, which matters on an energy-constrained edge device.
---

### 2203.05890 — Video Coding for Machines with Feature-Based Rate-Distortion Optimization

This paper proposes feature-based rate–distortion optimization (FRDO) for video coding for machines (VCM), replacing pixel-level distortion with distortion measured in a VGG-16 feature space: D_FSSE = Σ(F(orig) − F(rec))², with an SAD variant, plus a hybrid HFRDO using J = 0.5(D_SSE + D*_F) + λR, where feature distortion is rescaled to be comparable to SSE. Encoding uses VTM-8.0 in intra mode on the Cityscapes dataset (500 images), and compression performance is measured by machine-vision accuracy: Mask R-CNN with ResNet50, weighted AP. Headline BDR results vs. default RDO: FRDO-SAD −3.99% weighted AP; hybrid HFRDO-SAD −5.49%; in a ΔQP scenario HFRDO reaches −9.95%. Encoder runtime increases by about 2.3×. For the telepresence cube this fits the transport stage combined with perception: the cube's downstream task is reconstruction and understanding of the remote user (detection/tracking of the user, or inputs to a neural renderer) rather than human viewing, so allocating bits toward features that matter for the machine-vision stack rather than pixel fidelity saves bandwidth. Compatibility: bitstreams remain VVC/standard-compliant since only encoder RDO decisions change; the feature extractor (VGG) must be shared between the encoder and the cube's downstream tasks to be meaningful. Caution: tested intra-only on Cityscapes; inter-picture and avatar-specific content may behave differently.
---

### 2203.12467 — A Lower-bound for Variable-length Source Coding in Linear-Quadratic-Gaussian Control with Shared Randomness

This theoretical paper derives a lower bound on variable-length source coding for linear-quadratic-Gaussian (LQG) control with shared randomness. The main result is that the time-average expected codeword length must satisfy L ≥ (1/(T+1))·I(x^T → u^T), a directed-information bound under a prefix-condition requirement; when the prefix constraint is relaxed, the bound degrades by at most a factor θ⁻¹ (with θ the relax factor). The authors show that shared dither/randomness between encoder and decoder does not change the bound, and they cast the rate–distortion problem for control as a semidefinite program: min (1/2)(−log₂ det Π + log₂ det W) subject to trace constraints Tr(ΘP) + Tr(WS) ≤ γ, giving computable minimum rates for a given control-cost budget. Relevance to the telepresence cube is at the transport stage: the cube's feedback loop (tracking the viewer, adapting the display, streaming control signals for avatar pose/parameters) is essentially an LQG-style control loop, so this bound indicates the minimum bitrate needed to close the loop at a given distortion — a fundamental floor for bandwidth allocation. There are no experiments, datasets, or implementation; the contribution is entirely mathematical. Compatibility: none (no hardware); the bound is codec-agnostic and can serve as a sanity check for any rate-allocation scheme in the cube.
---

### 2203.16357 — Practical Learned Lossless JPEG Recompression with Multi-Level Cross-Channel Entropy Model in the DCT Domain

This paper presents a learned lossless recompression system for JPEG images, operating directly in the DCT domain. The 8×8 DCT blocks of the JPEG stream are rearranged into 64 frequency channels with a zigzag scan, and a multi-level cross-channel entropy model (MLCC) exploits correlation between the Y, Cb, and Cr channels (CFM/CPSM fusion for 4:2:0) and between DCT frequencies: conditioning proceeds sequentially Cr → Cb → Y with a hyperprior z̃; the context is modeled by space-to-depth reshaping into 4 rows with non-uniform column lengths [28, 8, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, 1], and each coefficient is coded with a Laplace distribution via a cross-entropy loss. On Kodak, the method achieves 0.965 bpp vs. JPEG's 1.369 bpp (29.51% savings), beating Lepton (1.102 bpp), JPEG XL (1.173 bpp), and CMIX (1.054 bpp). Runtime is 1.131 s encode / 1.023 s decode on a GTX 1060 with 32.3M parameters. For the telepresence cube this fits the transport stage: captured frames that must be stored or relayed in JPEG form (camera feeds, thumbnails, EXIF-bearing stills) can be losslessly recompressed to save bandwidth and storage with zero fidelity loss, and the MLCC architecture is a template for entropy-coding quantized DCT-like coefficients in any neural codec. Warnings: requires GPU inference and is JPEG-specific; it is not a general video codec.
---

### 2204.05043 — Learning-based Lossless Point Cloud Geometry Coding using Sparse Tensors

This paper presents SparseVoxelDNN, a lossless point cloud geometry coding method based on sparse tensors and 3-D autoregressive modeling. The occupancy of a 64³ block is factorized as p(v) = ∏ p(v_i | v_{i-1}, …, v_1) in a 3-D raster scan order (depth, then height, then width), and a point-based sparse-convolution network with causal type-A/type-B masks (constructed by zeroing the kernel center) predicts each voxel's occupancy; the occupancy intensity is modeled as a mixture of L logistic distributions, and probabilities feed a CABAC coder. Octree signaling adds less than 2% overhead. Training used roughly 914,465 blocks from the MVUB, 8i, CAT1, and Owlii datasets. Headline results: average 52% bitrate savings over MPEG G-PCC v14; −66.4% on MVUB and −66.5% on 8i dense point clouds, −33.6% on CAT1, and −35.8% on USP; a further ~29% average reduction vs. VoxelDNN. Encoding takes 7.2 s vs. VoxelDNN's 355 s (49× faster) and 4.7× G-PCC; decoding takes 229 s (the autoregressive bottleneck). For the telepresence cube this belongs to the representation/transport stage: point-cloud avatars and scene geometry captured by depth sensors can be transmitted losslessly at about half the bitrate of G-PCC while preserving geometry exactly for downstream reconstruction. Warnings: the 229 s decode time is far from real-time (the authors point to multiscale structures as future work); a GPU is required, and encoding is also not live.
---

### 2204.09055 — Per-clip and per-bitrate adaptation of the Lagrangian multiplier in video coding

This paper adapts the HEVC Lagrangian multiplier λ per clip and per bitrate operating point to improve rate–distortion for streaming. The codec default λ is scaled by a factor k (λ_new = k·λ_orig), and BD-Rate is minimized with Brent's direct optimization; the Pareto-optimal frontier across the RD curve is then estimated as D_Pareto(R) = sup_k D(R, k) by interpolating RD curves for each candidate k (exponential fit) and taking the best quality at each bitrate. Three bitrate ranges (LOW/MED/HIGH: CRF 22–32, 27–37, 32–42; CBR 256k–4M, 1M–8M, 4M–12M bps) are optimized separately using x265 on about 2,000 clips of the YouTube-UGC dataset (5 s DASH segments). Headline results: a fixed k = 0.782 gives 0.32% average BD-Rate gain; per-clip direct optimization gives 1.87% (95% of clips improve); the new per-clip per-bitrate Pareto method gives 2.67% (CBR-PSNR), 3.03% (CRF-PSNR), 3.02% (CBR-SSIM), and 2.34% (CRF-SSIM) with 96–98% of clips improving — but at about 180 encodes per clip (3× the prior system's cost). For the telepresence cube this fits the transport stage: when the remote-user video is delivered at fixed target bitrates (typical for streaming pipelines), per-content λ adaptation yields several percent bandwidth savings at equal quality. Warnings: the per-clip cost is offline (ladder generation), not live; gains are modest; tuned for HEVC/x265 and YouTube-UGC content.
---

### 2205.03587 — Efficient VVC Intra Prediction Based on Deep Feature Fusion and Probability Estimation

This paper proposes a two-stage complexity-reduction framework for the VVC (H.266) intra encoder, implemented in the VTM-12.0 reference software under ALL-INTRA conditions. Stage one, "Intra Depth prediction with Deep Feature Fusion" (D-DFF), predicts the optimal coding depth for each 8x8 block of a 128x128 CTU from a 5x5 spatial-temporal reference depth map (collected from already-coded neighboring CUs, falling back to co-located blocks of the previous frame), using a lightweight inception-like CNN: two parallel paths (1x1, and 1x1+3x3 with ReLU) producing 12 feature maps flattened to a 300-vector, then 2 hidden layers plus softmax over 6 depth classes, trained with cross-entropy, batch 256, Adam lr 1e-4 on 58 sequences from UVG/AVS2/AVS3/LIVE compressed with QPs 22-37. A conservative depth-offset correction (mean over K_D=25 reference blocks) compensates accumulated errors; the CU splits only up to its predicted optimal depth. Stage two, "Partition mode prediction with Probability Estimation" (P-PBE), ranks the 5 partition modes (QT, BTV, BTH, TTV, TTH) by Bayes-estimated probability computed from mode occurrences in spatial-temporal neighbor CUs and early-terminates RD checking when J_cur > J_min. Results: 55.59% average encoding-time saving with only 1.40% BDBR increase across JVET CTC classes A1-E (e.g., 59.17% ATS on 4K A1 class), beating C-TTD, Fu's, CSD-SL and Tang's methods, with model overhead below 1.8% (avg 0.48%) and depth accuracy 91.29%. For the telepresence cube this addresses the transport stage on the capture side: it cuts encoder-side compute for real-time VVC intra coding, which matters if the cube encodes per-view video, but VVC is patent-encumbered and the gains are encoder-only (no decoder/latency benefit), so value is limited versus purpose-built neural codecs.

### 2205.03599 — GAN-Based Multi-View Video Coding with Spatio-Temporal EPI Reconstruction

This paper (MVLL) compresses multi-view video by skipping intermediate viewpoints at the encoder and regenerating them at the decoder from a compact learned side-information (SI) latent code, replacing depth maps. At the encoder, each view is cut into 8xN strips; strips at equal spatial locations across K views are stacked into a spatial EPI of size 8K x N x 3, and L=3 successive frames are stacked into a spatio-temporal EPI X of size 8K x N x 3L. A fully convolutional extraction network E (1 conv + 4 residual blocks + 2 convs, BN+ReLU) maps X to a latent code Z=E(X) whose middle-view portion is compressed by an auto-encoder C with quantization and a hyperprior entropy model (from DVC), giving bitstream with estimated rate H(Z-bar^c). The decoder's generator G reconstructs the EPI X-hat=G(Z-hat) from the compressed latent plus left/right view slices, guided by a discriminator D that judges both image and latent. Joint training uses min-max GAN loss plus lambda*l_d + l_r, with l_d = pixel-domain MSE + VGG feature loss, lambda in {512,1024,2048,4096}; epochs=50, iterations=64000, batch 1. On 8 MVC-CTC sequences (Balloons, Kendo, Dancer, Poznan_Street, etc.) it saves 20.44% bitrate over MV-HEVC (and 44.14% over MVD, 36.28% over DCVC, 23.86% over TSAN) at equal PSNR, with average BDPSNR +0.729 dB, at 0.204 s/frame encode on a single 2080Ti. Relevance: transport stage — the GAN-latent-as-SI scheme is directly analogous to sending a compact avatar latent and reconstructing the remote view; the spatio-temporal EPI structure suits a multi-camera cube capture rig. No code release is indicated, and the loss/entropy machinery is standard neural-coding practice.

### 2205.06511 — Analysis of Neural Image Compression Networks for Machine-to-Machine Communication

This ICIP paper analyzes four state-of-the-art neural image compression networks — b2018 (basic hyperprior-free analysis/synthesis autoencoder with GDN), bmshj2018 (adds hyperprior; Gaussian side-latent entropy model), mbt2018 (non-zero-mean Gaussians plus autoregressive context), and HiFiC (GAN-based decoder, discriminator plus LPIPS-style feature distortion) — for Video Coding for Machines (VCM): the decoded image is consumed by a Mask R-CNN (Detectron2, ResNet-50) instance-segmentation network instead of a human. All networks optimize the standard loss L = E[-log2 p_y-hat(y-hat;ψ)] + λ·d(x,x-hat). Evaluation uses 500 uncompressed Cityscapes validation images (1024x2048) with weighted Average Precision (wAP) per class-instance counts, plus PSNR/SSIM/VMAF, and Bjontegaard-delta-rate (BDR). Key findings: (1) replacing GDN with Leaky ReLU non-linearities in b2018 costs PSNR (+1.9% BDR) but saves 13.2% bitrate at equal wAP (activation alignment with the ResNet backbone); (2) training with SSIM instead of MSE distortion saves up to 6.3% (bmshj2018) and 1.0% (mbt2018) bitrate at equal wAP because structural fidelity matches segmentation needs; (3) in the full comparison vs JPEG, HEVC (HM 16.20), VVC (VTM 10.0), the GAN-based HiFiC is the best VCM codec with -52.8% BDR at equal wAP, outperforming VVC (-43.2%) despite VVC winning on PSNR/VMAF/SSIM (-66.8%/-71.3%/-60.5%). Relevance: transport stage — evidence that for a telepresence pipeline whose output feeds machine processing (e.g., a neural avatar decoder or optical-engine renderer), codecs trained with task-aligned activations and losses (GAN, SSIM, LReLU) beat standards at equal task accuracy; informs the cube's choice of neural codec over VVC/HEVC when downstream stages are neural. Uses pre-trained TensorFlow Compression models (Apache-2.0); paper methods are reproducible from published checkpoints.

### 2205.06754 — Slimmable Video Codec

SlimVC is a fully slimmable neural video codec in which every trainable module can discard channel width at runtime, jointly controlling bitrate, memory, compute and latency from a single model. It combines a slimmable compressive autoencoder (SlimCAE-style: SlimFE encoder, SlimFD decoder with switchable GDN/IGDN) with a slimmable motion-free spatiotemporal entropy model adopted from STEM — the hyperencoder SlimHE takes current and previous latents z_t, z_{t-1} (transmitting only residual latent z_res = z_t - z_{t-1}), and SlimTPM/SlimEPM predict distribution parameters; the expensive autoregressive spatial prior module (SPM) is deliberately dropped because it increases latency by two orders of magnitude. Five width factors [0.25,0.375,0.5,0.75,1] give five sub-models trained jointly by minimizing Σ_k L^(k)(θ^(k),φ^(k);X), each a rate-distortion objective D+λR with MSE distortion; training is two-stage (image SlimCAE+hyperprior on Open Images/CLIC, then video stage on Vimeo-90k with fixed SlimFE/FD, lr 5e-5, 256x256 crops). On HEVC Class B and UVG sequences (GOP 10/12), SlimVC matches independently trained codecs and H.264 on rate-distortion while being far cheaper: encoding 1080p costs 73-436 GFLOPs across widths (vs 643 for STEM, 3074 for DVC) with up to 20x speedup at low rates, and the entropy model dominates memory (SlimTPM up to 232.5 GFLOPs, 16.2M params at full width). Relevance: transport stage — directly addresses the cube's sub-150 ms, sub-1 Mbps, battery-constrained compute envelope: one codec instance can trade rate/latency on the fly (e.g., low width for motion-tracking handshake, high width for keyframes), and the motion-free entropy model removes optical-flow cost from the capture node. No code release mentioned; CVPR 2022 paper.

### 2205.07062 — An Interpretable MRI Reconstruction Network with Two-grid-cycle Correction and Geometric Prior Distillation

This paper proposes CGPD-CSNet, a deep-unfolding network for compressed-sensing MRI (CS-MRI) reconstruction that handles multiple sampling ratios (10-50%) with a single model by unfolding a two-grid multigrid iteration of the classical problem min_x {H(x;y) + γR(x)} with H(x;y)=½||Tx-y||². Each of 13 stages has three interpretable modules: a pre-relaxation module D_l implementing one gradient-descent step h_l = x_{l-1} - η_l T^H(Tx_{l-1}-y) with learnable step-length η_l; a correction module C_l computing k-space residual r = y - Th_l, restricting to the coarse grid via learned stride-2 convolution I^R, solving the coarse-grid error with a learnable solution operator S_l (4 ResBlocks + convs), converting back to image space with inverse FFT, prolongating via transposed convolution I^P, and concatenating (plus a noise-level map M^β); and a geometric prior distillation module P_l approximating the proximal solution x_l = (I-N)^-1(m_l) ≈ (Σ_{i=0}^k N^i)(m_l) using CNN blocks for the nonlinear geometric-feature operator N(x)=-λ∂R/∂x fused with 1x1 convolutions. A condition module (3 fully-connected layers, ReLU+softplus) maps the CS ratio α to [η,β], and the deep-supervised loss L = (1/n_t mn)Σ(||x_{n_s/2}-x||_1 + ||x_{n_s}-x||_1) is trained with Adam lr 1e-4, 500 epochs on brain (MICCAI 2013 SATA/OASIS, 100+50 images, augmented to 800) and cardiac (4180/300 images) datasets. Results: 34.23 dB PSNR / 0.9126 SSIM at 10% CS ratio on brain (3.18 dB over SOTA, avg 1.42 dB gain), 47.31 dB at 50%, robust to untrained ratios/noise, 0.0721 s per 256x256 image on a Tesla V100, ~4.4M params. Relevance: transport stage conceptually — an interpretable, physics-grounded, multi-condition single-model unfolding pattern (condition-module adaptation to bandwidth) that could inform compression of volumetric capture streams; but the MRI domain, model-based Fourier sampling, and 0.07 s/image decode do not map cleanly onto human-avatar reconstruction, so use is methodological only.

### 2205.10501 — Making Video Quality Assessment Models Sensitive to Frame Rate Distortions

This paper (UT Austin + Google) makes existing full-reference (FR) video quality assessment (VQA) models sensitive to frame-rate distortions via a generic feature-fusion framework, "GST" (Generalized Space-Time). The temporal component is TGREED, derived from the GREED model: the video is decomposed by a bank of K=7 temporal band-pass filters (biorthogonal-2.2 wavelet packet, low-pass subband discarded) at two scales s=4,5 (frames spatially downsampled by 2^s), band-pass coefficient distributions in 5x5 patches are fit with Generalized Gaussian Distributions (GGD) whose entropy h(X)=1/β-log(β/(2αΓ(1/β))) is variance-scaled as ε_kpt = log(1+σ²(B_kpt))·h(B_kpt); a "pseudo-reference" PR (the reference temporally subsampled to the distorted video's frame rate) debiases compression vs frame-rate effects in TGREED_kt = (1/P)Σ_p |(1+|ε^D-ε^PR|)·(ε^R+1)/(ε^PR+1) - 1|. One spatial feature from any FR model (SSIM, MS-SSIM, ST-RRED, SpEED, VMAF) is concatenated with the 14 TGREED features and regressed to subjective scores with an SVR. On LIVE-YT-HFR (480 videos, 24-120 fps), GST fusion lifts SROCC from 0.5566→0.7576 (SSIM), 0.5742→0.8128 (MS-SSIM), 0.6394→0.8029 (ST-RRED), 0.6051→0.8276 (SpEED), with the best gains at low frame rates (24 fps SSIM SROCC 0.266→0.386, PLCC 0.222→0.635); fusion versions also improve on legacy fixed-frame-rate databases (LIVE-VQA, LIVE-mobile, CSIQ-VQA, ETRI-LIVE STSVQ, AVT-VQDB-UHD-1). Relevance: perception stage — GST provides an open-source (github.com/pavancm/GST_VQA) objective metric for validating cube output quality when frame rate, bitrate and temporal subsampling trade-offs are tuned (e.g., avatar keyframe streaming at reduced fps), and its pseudo-reference trick quantifies pure frame-rate loss independently of compression; it cannot, however, evaluate reconstructed 3D/holographic fidelity.

### 2206.03468 — Rate Distortion Tradeoff in Private Read Update Write in Federated Submodel Learning

This is a pure information-theory paper (Vithana & Ulukus, UMD, Asilomar-style) analyzing the rate–distortion tradeoff of "private read update write" (PRUW) in federated submodel learning (FSL), where a model is partitioned into M submodels of size L over GF(q) symbols stored on N non-colluding databases. The setting: a user privately downloads (reads) and uploads (writes) one submodel while hiding both the submodel index and the update values from the databases, and is allowed controlled distortion in each phase. Distortion is Hamming distance per bit, D_r = (1/L)Σ1[W≠Ŵ] and D_w = (1/L)Σ1[Δ≠Δ̂], under budgets D̃_r and D̃_w. The main theorem shows achievable costs C_R = (2/(1-2/N))(1-D̃_r) and C_W = (2/(1-2/N))(1-D̃_w) bits-per-submodel-element, i.e., communication cost decreases linearly with allowed distortion. The scheme extends an earlier zero-error PRUW scheme by enlarging subpacketization ℓ (only ⌊N/2⌋-1 of ℓ bits are correctly read/written per subpacket); when ℓ* is non-integer, the model is split into two sections with optimized length ratio λ solved via a small linear program, achieving cost 2/(1-2/N)(1-D̃) exactly. No datasets, experiments, fps, PSNR, or Mbps are reported; results are purely achievable-rate characterizations. For our telepresence cube this is only marginally relevant: it formalizes the classical insight that allowing distortion (sparsification/quantization) in an avatar-parameter stream yields linear communication savings, and it can justify distortion-budgeted quantization of Gaussian-splat/avatar latents over a sub-1Mbps link. It has no direct bearing on the display architecture, imposes no hardware constraints, and there is no code or licensing consideration; its privacy machinery could optionally protect a user's identity/avatar data against a federated server, but this is far outside our pipeline's needs.

### 2206.04328 — Novel projection schemes for graph-based Light Field coding

This paper improves the super-ray projection step of graph-based light-field (LF) coding (the Rizkallah/Guillemot line of GFT-based coders with graph coarsening and partitioning) by fixing disparity-accuracy problems. In the baseline, the top-left view is over-segmented with SLIC, a median disparity per super-pixel is estimated, and super-pixel labels are shifted to all other views. The authors prove that if disparity error is Gaussian-like, the median-disparity error at the k-th view scales as k²·mse₁, so distant views on high-parallax (synthetic) LFs degrade badly.
