Two proposals: (1) for vignetted real LFs (plenoptic 13×13 arrays) the center view becomes the reference, projecting symmetrically outward and halving maximum projection distance, preserving accurate optical-flow disparity (Fountain_Vincent_2, Danger_de_Mort from EPFL); (2) for synthetic LFs (Greek, Sideboard, 9×9) multiple sparsely placed reference views are chosen by minimizing a Lagrangian objective min(k[x]+λ·SSIM[x]) over the lower convex hull of (distance, projected-segmentation-SSIM), partitioning the global graph into sub-global graphs processable in parallel. Encoding: GFT, adaptive 32-group quantization, CABAC arithmetic coding; segmentation masks via edge arithmetic coder. Reported numbers: projected-label SSIM ~0.87–1.0 per view; multi-view scheme cuts encoder+decoder time by more than half (Laplacian eigendecomposition is O(n³) and each sub-graph has ~¼ the nodes); both proposals beat HEVC-Serpentine and JPEG-Pleno 4DTM at low bitrates, and the center-view scheme beats them at all bitrates on vignetted real LFs, while HEVC remains best at mid/high bitrates on clean synthetic LFs; multi-view costs a slight bitrate increase for extra reference segmentation/disparity maps. For our cube this maps to the transport stage: if we ever carry a sparse multi-view (light-field) representation of the remote user, super-ray projection with center-view/multi-view references yields more accurate geometry and a faster, lower-rate graph transform coder; it is algorithmic (open literature methods, no license issues), CPU/Python-based (not real-time yet), and does not constrain the display architecture.

### 2206.05889 — Intra Encoding Complexity Control with a Time-Cost Model for Versatile Video Coding

Huang et al. (SJTU + ByteDance) build the first precise, one-pass encoding-time control framework for VVC (VTM10.0) All-Intra coding, so an encoder can hit a target frame-encoding time budget — relevant when a fixed per-frame latency/CPU envelope must be guaranteed. Core component: a CTU-level Time–PlanarCost model T_p = α·PlanarCost^β, where PlanarCost (the RDO cost of the luma Planar intra mode, available after the 64×64 intra search) strongly correlates with CTU luma-compress time; α, β are fitted offline per sequence/QP from the first 10 frames, and α is calibrated online with a CPU capability factor r_cpu = ΣT_r/ΣT_p, giving T̃_p = ᾶ·PlanarCost^β. A picture-level framework combines: (1) uneven complexity pre-allocation weighting CTUs by 8×8 Hadamard cost (SA8D), T_b = ω_CTU/Σω · T_PicBudget; (2) data-driven presets — frontier (time-saving vs BDBR) QTMT maximum-depth configurations, ranging from preset 0 (0% TS) to preset 5 (72.9% TS, 9.02% BDBR) — selected to approximate the target acceleration ratio r_CTU = T_a/T̃_p; (3) feedback that redistributes accumulated time error T_e = T_r − T_a over a sliding window N_Window = max(1, min(CTU_left, 20)). On JVET sequences (Classes A1, A2, B, C, E; QPs 22–37), with luma-compress time targets from 30%–90%, the average time error converges to ~3.21% (worst 6.68% at 30% target), time savings 9.96%–57.78%, with BDBR loss only 0.23%–2.71%. For our cube this applies to the transport stage if we adopt VVC/HEVC-class video coding for the avatar stream or a fixed-latency encode budget (<150 ms e2e): it provides a proven recipe (PlanarCost model + preset selection + feedback) for guaranteeing per-frame encode time on resource-constrained hardware; implementation is within the open VTM reference software (BSD-style licensing), and no display-architecture constraints are implied.

### 2206.08019 — Multi-View Imputation and Cross-Attention Network Based on Incomplete Longitudinal and Multimodal Data for Conversion Prediction of Mild Cognitive Impairment

Wang et al. (Southern Medical University) propose MCNet, an end-to-end multi-task network that jointly performs missing-data imputation and MCI→AD conversion prediction on incomplete longitudinal, multimodal (MRI + FDG-PET) ROI features (90 ROIs per modality). Architecture: (1) a data-imputation module using two stacked 3-layer MinimalRNNs (per-modality) whose cell update is h_t = g_t⊙h_{t−1} + (1−g_t)⊙z_t with z_t = tanh(Φ(x_t)) and g_t = σ(W_h h_{t−1} + W_z z_t); missing values are imputed from a "multimodal view" (PET-from-MRI at baseline via tanh FC: x̃_cs = tanh(W_cs h_MRI + b_cs)), a "longitudinal view" (concat of both modalities' previous hidden states via W_lg·Concat + b_lg), and a learnable weighted combination α·(multimodal) + β·(longitudinal) for t > 1; an adversarial discriminator (MLP+sigmoid, mask-supervised) forces imputed distributions toward real ones. (2) a prediction module with two multi-head (J = J' = 4) cross-attention blocks: block-1 fuses MRI/PET features per time point for longitudinal classification (cross-entropy), block-2 fuses across time points for conversion prediction with focal loss ℒ_pred = −µ(1−Ĉ)^γ log Ĉ (µ = 0.3, γ = 2). Total loss ℒ = λℒ_est + ζℒ_adv + ξ(ℒ_cls + ℒ_pred) with λ = 2, ζ = 10, ξ = 10, trained in three stages (Adam, lr 5×10⁻³, wd 5×10⁻⁴). Data: ADNI-1/2 (training; 1301 subjects), ADNI-3 and OASIS-3 (external testing); testing uses baseline-visit data only. Headline results: ADNI-1/2-A ACC 0.830 ± 0.019, AUC 0.842 ± 0.032; ADNI-3-A AUC 0.849; OASIS-3-A AUC 0.857; best imputation MAE/RMSE 0.322/0.468 (MRI), 0.415/0.513 (PET); outperforms SVM, MLP, LSTM-Robust, GRU-D, AJRNN, DRM. For our telepresence cube this is tangentially relevant to transport-stage robustness: its multi-view imputation + adversarial training pattern could fill dropped packet/lost-frame gaps in an avatar stream (predicting missing per-frame avatar parameters from past and cross-modal streams), but it is a clinical classification pipeline (no fps/PSNR/Mbps numbers reported; no latency claims), so expect only conceptual transfer. Code is public on GitHub (github.com/Meiyan88/MCNET).

### 2206.10083 — Memory-Efficient Learned Image Compression with Pruned Hyperprior Module

Luo, Sun, Liu and Katto (Waseda) present ERHP (Enhanced ResRep on Hyper Path), a structured-pruning method that shrinks the hyperprior module of learned image compression (LIC) networks so they fit portable/edge devices. Key observations: in the Hyperprior model the hyper path holds ~40% of parameters yet its latent z accounts for only ~1.7% of bitrate, and halving hyper-path channels barely hurts RD performance — evidence of over-parameterization. Method: adapts ResRep by inserting identity-initialized 1×1 "compactor" convolutions (R_i ∈ R^{C×C×1×1}) after hyper-path layers and training with ℒ = R + λD + β·Σ_i Σ_j ‖R_{i;j,:,:,:}‖₂ (rate-distortion plus group-Lasso on compactor channels; β = 1e-9, 70% pruning target), then merging compactors into preceding layers via O = I⊗W'_i⊗R'_i + B(b_i)⊗R'_i and W'_i = T(T(W_i)⊗R'_i). Novel components: compactor placement after PixelShuffle (channels grouped in α²-tuples, PS(I⊗W^ps + b)⊗R'_i with per-stride channel reindexing k::α²) and for deconvolution layers (W^de'_i = W^de_i ⊗ R'_i), since these are the building blocks of hyper decoders; pruned models are finetuned (lr 1e-4) to recover RD performance. Results on the Hyperprior model and Cheng et al.'s GMM model (trained on OpenImages, tested on Kodak): parameter reductions of 22.6%–34.3% of total model parameters (e.g., 11.582M→7.748M params at λ = 0.0483), with equal-or-better rate-distortion — e.g., 36.706 dB PSNR @ 0.936 bpp (vs 0.937 originally) at λ = 0.0483, and 28.233 dB @ 0.130 bpp at λ = 0.0018; ERHP also beats uniformly manual-pruned models at equal parameter counts. PSNR unchanged by construction (main path and context model are frozen); hyper decoder's last layer is unprunable (output dim fixed by y). For our telepresence cube this fits the transport stage: learned codecs for avatar appearance/texture latents can be slimmed by ~25–35% (memory, not compute or bitrate) for on-cube inference without quality loss — important because a 10 cm cube has tight flash/DRAM budgets; no bitrate/latency/fps claims beyond Kodak bpp; no license issues (research code patterns, standard open-source components).

### 2206.11599 — Universal Learned Image Compression With Low Computational Cost

Li, Xin, Bao, Meng, Liang and Tan (HIT Shenzhen + Peng Cheng Lab) build a low-energy learned image codec, replacing convolution-heavy CNN encoders/decoders with plug-and-play Shift–Addition Parallel Modules (SAPM-E for the encoder, SAPM-D for the decoder) so the model runs on compute/power-constrained devices. Design: a shift branch using DeepShift-Q weight quantization (W_q = sign(W)·2^{round(log2|W|)}) which executes as bit-shifts, extracting large-grained/coarse features; a parallel addition branch using AdderNet-style L1-norm "adder" layers (no multiplications), stabilized by 1×1 implicitly-deconvolution (ID) whitening based on the input covariance, Cov = (1/N)(X−μ)ᵀ(X−μ), D = Cov^(−1/2), folded into weights as Y = X·(D·W) − μ·D·W, extracting fine-grained features; average pooling shortcuts (SAPM-E) and 1×1-conv + PixelShuffle upsampling (SAPM-D) boost expressivity. Since the marginal distribution of latents is empirically Laplace-like, the entropy model is upgraded to a Laplace Mixture Model p(ŷ|ẑ) ~ Σ_k w^(k)·L(μ^(k), σ^(k)) to save bits. Training: 3 transform levels per side, GDN nonlinearities, hyperprior/context model per Cheng et al., MSE-optimized for low bitrate (λ ∈ {16,…,512}) and high bitrate (λ ∈ {1024,…,4096}), CLIC2020 (61,894 images) train, Kodak test; Adam, lr 1e-4 (900k iters) then 1e-5 (100k iters). Results: comparable or better PSNR/MS-SSIM than the CNN baseline and BPG (outperforming JPEG/JPEG2000), with unit energy cost cut from 4.60 pJ to 2.03 pJ per MAC-equivalent — a 2.2× reduction (0 multiplications, shift 0.13 pJ, fixed-point add 0.10 pJ at 45 nm); ablations show either branch alone underperforms and vanilla AdderNet fails outright for compression. No explicit bitrate table or latency/fps reported beyond Kodak RD curves. For our telepresence cube this is a transport-stage fit: a ~2×-lower-power neural codec that can run on the cube's edge SoC for avatar appearance/compressed streams, and the LMM entropy trick is directly transferable to our own learned latent codecs; no license issues, standard open-source research components.

### 2507.18371 — MVG4D: Image Matrix-Based Multi-View and Motion Generation for 4D Content Creation from a Single Image

MVG4D (Yin et al., Guangdong Lab of AI/Shenzhen University/Tsinghua/Dalian) turns a single still image into a dynamic 4D Gaussian-splat scene by first fine-tuning a view-conditional 2D diffusion model that, given a frame and a relative camera transform (Δθ,Δφ,Δr) in spherical coordinates around the object, synthesizes an "image matrix" of temporally-coherent multi-view frames (training objective is the standard diffusion noise-prediction loss E[||ε-ε_θ(z_t,t,c(x,R,T))||²]); a static 3D Gaussian point cloud is then optimized against this matrix with a Score-Distillation-Sampling loss ∇L_SDS = E[ω(t)(ε_φ(I;t,I^r,Δp)-ε)∂I/∂Θ]; finally the Gaussians are lifted to 4D via a spatial-temporal structure encoder plus a small deformation MLP trained with an MSE re-projection loss L_Ref against the generated image matrix. On Objaverse it reports CLIP-I 0.982 (vs. 0.954 for EG4D), PSNR 36.44 dB (vs. 35.07 for 4Diffusion), FVD scores of 241.99/201.71/134.58 (vs. 600-1100 for Consistent4D/SV4D/DreamGaussian4D), and end-to-end generation in 8 min 46 s on a single RTX 4090 versus 6.9 h for V4D; an ablation shows the multi-view-generation module alone raises PSNR from 30.54 (w/o) to 36.44 (full). For TAYF this is a capture/representation-stage technique for the degenerate case of "no multi-camera rig": it shows a single-image (or, by extension, single-camera) input can be diffusion-hallucinated into multi-view supervision for a compact 4D Gaussian avatar, which is attractive for a cube with only one or two onboard cameras, but the ~9-minute offline generation and reliance on a desktop GPU diffusion model make it unusable for live conversational capture — at best a one-time enrollment-avatar builder, not a real-time pipeline stage.

### 2510.10030 — P-4DGS: Predictive 4D Gaussian Splatting with 90x Compression

P-4DGS (NeurIPS 2025 submission) fuses an anchor-based spatial predictor (Scaffold-GS style: k child Gaussians per anchor generated from position offsets {x_i}=x_a+{O_i}·l_a and opacity/color/scale predicted by small MLPs conditioned on anchor feature f_a and view-relative distance/direction) with a canonical-space-plus-deformation-MLP temporal model (Δx,Δs,Δr=ψ_d(concat(E(x),E(t)))) and a full entropy-coding pipeline: anchor attributes are quantized with adaptive, content-dependent step size q=Q_0(1+tanh(ψ_q(h))) derived from a hash-grid-queried context feature h, and coded against a learned Gaussian prior p(â)=Φ_{μ,σ}(â+q/2)-Φ_{μ,σ}(â-q/2) whose μ,σ come from an MLP on the same context; total loss is L=λ_rate(R_anchor+R_hash)+L_render. On D-NeRF it matches D3DGS's 38.10 dB PSNR at 1.039 MB versus D3DGS's 39.45 MB (40x smaller) while rendering at 262 FPS vs. 149 FPS; on NeRF-DS it beats D3DGS in PSNR (24.18 vs 23.75) at 0.704 MB vs 59.38 MB, a >90x reduction, and an ablation shows spatial (anchor) prediction alone cuts bitrate from 56.7 MB to 7.27 MB (-87%), with quantization+entropy coding taking it to 0.85 MB. This is a strong TRANSPORT-stage candidate for TAYF: sub-1MB-per-sequence dynamic avatar representations at 250+ FPS decode speed on a desktop GPU are compatible with streaming a full dynamic scene over an ordinary network link, though the reported numbers are for offline per-scene overfitting (trained per sequence, not a live encoder), so realizing this on-device would require an amortized/generalizable encoder and lower-power rasterization for the 10 cm cube's compute budget.

### 2511.07122 — Sparse4DGS: 4D Gaussian Splatting for Sparse-Frame Dynamic Scene Reconstruction

Sparse4DGS (Shi et al., Hangzhou Dianzi Univ./Peking Univ./HIT, AAAI 2026) tackles the case where only low-FPS video is available for dynamic Gaussian-splat reconstruction — directly relevant to a bandwidth- or sensor-limited capture rig. It introduces a per-Gaussian "Texture Intensity" (TI) attribute derived from Sobel-operator gradient magnitude maps TI(i,j)=sqrt(TI_x²+TI_y²), aligned between rendered and ground-truth maps via a Pearson-correlation loss L_tex=1-PCC(TI_gt,TI_render) (PCC chosen over L1 because it tolerates the spatial inconsistency of per-frame-independent texture extraction); a "Texture-Aware Deformation Regularization" (TADR) applies the same PCC alignment to Sobel-filtered depth maps (rendered vs. DPT monocular-depth estimate) to constrain the deformation MLP in texture-rich, motion-ambiguous regions; and "Texture-Aware Canonical Optimization" (TACO) reformulates the canonical-Gaussian SGD update as Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics with a texture-dependent noise term ε_tex=σ(-k(TI-t))·Ση that continuously perturbs low-texture Gaussians toward texture-rich regions until they gain opacity. On their new iPhone-4D dataset at 5 FPS input (vs. 30 FPS full-rate), Sparse4DGS achieves PSNR 27.51 dB versus Deformable3DGS's 21.12 dB and 4DGaussians' collapse to 16.37 dB, and on NeRF-DS with 20 frames it improves PSNR by >1 dB over baselines (22.34 vs 20.81). For TAYF's capture stage, this is directly applicable if the cube's camera(s) can only sustain low frame rates under thermal/power/bandwidth constraints: it demonstrates that texture-guided regularization — not just more frames — is what prevents geometry collapse in sparse-frame human/scene capture, though it's evaluated on small synthetic/real object scenes rather than full-body human capture and requires per-scene optimization (15-20 min on an RTX 3090), not a feed-forward encoder suitable for live avatar tracking.

### 2512.24742 — Splatwizard: A Benchmark Toolkit for 3D Gaussian Splatting Compression

Splatwizard is an engineering/benchmarking paper (not a novel compression method per se) that provides a unified PyTorch training pipeline for 3DGS compression research: a scheduler-based architecture that lets arbitrary pruning/densification/quantization/entropy-coding modules be registered as "tasks" at specific training iterations (pre-scheduler, rendering, loss, post-scheduler, optimizer-update stages), with JIT/AOT-compiled CUDA rasterizer swapping so different rate-distortion methods (HAC, CAT-3DGS, LightGaussian, SpeedySplat, etc.) can share a common codebase; it separately reports geometric evaluation via TSDF depth-fusion (Chamfer distance on point clouds/meshes) in addition to the usual PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS/bitrate/FPS/peak-memory metrics. As a demonstration they build "ChimeraGS," which mixes SpeedySplat's Hessian-based importance score Ũ_i=(∇_{g_i}I_G)² for aggressive pruning with LightGaussian-style distillation-guided spherical-harmonic-degree pruning (learnable binary masks via straight-through estimator, distillation loss against a teacher-rendered pseudo-view at randomly perturbed camera position t_pseudo=t_train+N(0,σ²)), reporting rate-distortion performance approaching HAC while achieving top-tier Gaussian counts on Mip-NeRF 360. For TAYF this is not itself a technique to adopt but a research-infrastructure signal: it documents that the 3DGS-compression literature has fragmented into incompatible CUDA/rasterizer stacks, meaning any team building a compact-avatar transport pipeline should expect nontrivial engineering overhead consolidating pruning + quantization + entropy coding into a single low-power on-device encoder, rather than assuming methods compose cleanly.

### 2601.12814 — CSGaussian: Progressive Rate-Distortion Compression and Segmentation for 3D Gaussian Splatting

CSGaussian jointly compresses and semantically segments 3D Gaussian scenes by adding an INR-based (implicit-neural-representation) hyperprior for entropy coding: two compact MLPs take an anchor's positional embedding and predict Gaussian-prior parameters (mean μ, variance σ, quantization step q) for (a) color/geometric attributes and (b) semantic (open-vocabulary segmentation) features separately, with quantized-attribute probability estimated as p(â|x)=∫[â-q/2,â+q/2] N(μ,σ) da for entropy coding; a channel-wise autoregressive model (CARM) further predicts per-channel mean/variance corrections conditioned on already-decoded channels to exploit intra-feature redundancy, and a view-frequency-aware masking mechanism prunes primitives with low rendering contribution. Evaluated on LERF and 3D-OVS with rate-control factors λ_rate swept over {0.004,0.01,0.02,(0.05)}, it is compared against CAT-3DGS's triplane hyperprior and reports faster training/decoding while matching reconstruction PSNR and mIoU segmentation accuracy at competitive bitrate versus OpenGaussian/InstanceGS (which do not compress at all). For TAYF this targets the TRANSPORT stage but for a different payload than raw geometry — compressed semantic labels alongside compressed appearance — which is only tangentially useful for a person-telepresence pipeline (TAYF doesn't need open-vocabulary object segmentation of the human), though the grid-free INR hyperprior design (avoiding heavy hash-grid/triplane auxiliary structures) is a relevant "cheap, coordinate-driven entropy model" pattern that a battery-constrained decoder could reuse for its own attribute compression.

### 2601.13148 — ICo3D: An Interactive Conversational 3D Virtual Human

ICo3D (industry paper) builds a real-time interactive Gaussian-splat avatar by combining an audio-driven head model, HeadGaS++, with a dynamic body model, SWinGS++, plus a full LLM conversational stack. HeadGaS++ gives each Gaussian a learned audio-visual latent basis F∈R^{B×f} (B blendshape-like expression channels) that is blended per-frame with audio-visual weight vector e_i via f_i=F^T e_i+f_0, then decoded by a small 2-layer MLP into per-Gaussian color/opacity (c_i,o_i=φ(f_i,γ(μ))) — i.e., Gaussians appear/vanish/recolor rather than move, driven directly by speech features. SWinGS++ partitions long body sequences into motion-adaptive sliding windows (window boundaries triggered when accumulated optical-flow magnitude from a pretrained RAFT model exceeds a threshold), trains a per-window tunable-MLP deformation field with M blended "motion modes" (y=ψ(Σ_m α_m w_m^T x+α_m b_m)) augmented by a spatial-temporal hash encoder, and stitches windows together with a consistency fine-tune (L1 loss between overlapping frame boundaries, alternating 75%/25% with standard training). Head and body — trained on different datasets/capture rigs (RenderMe-360 and DNA-Rendering) — are rigidly aligned via a closed-form FLAME-canonical-space transform, then merged into one rasterizer pass with Gaussian pruning near the jaw/face-boundary to avoid interpenetration artifacts; the conversational front end runs Whisper-Large-V3 ASR, a quantized 0.5B-parameter Qwen2 LLM (AWQ), and OpenVoice V2 TTS. On DNA-Rendering, SWinGS++ reaches 30.17 dB PSNR / 0.970 SSIM, beating the SWinGS baseline (29.45 dB) by the spatial-temporal encoder alone (+0.7 dB) and beating skeleton-driven HuGS (25.04 dB) which supports novel poses but is blurrier. This is the closest thing in the batch to a full TAYF end-to-end demo (capture→avatar→conversational drive→render), but it is a two-way pipeline for a *stationary* studio-captured avatar reused across sessions, not a live two-cube telepresence link — the "dynamic state" that would need to stream between cubes in TAYF's architecture (audio-visual blend weights e_i, body pose/window parameters) is exactly the compact quantity ICo3D shows can drive photorealistic Gaussian rendering, making this a good reference for what the wire format between cubes could look like, while its 0.5B-parameter LLM and multi-window training pipeline are far too heavy for on-cube inference within a 10 cm power/thermal budget.

### 2602.03538 — Constrained Dynamic Gaussian Splatting

CDGS (Sec. reads as CVPR/similar submission) reformulates dynamic Gaussian-splat reconstruction as an explicit budget-constrained optimization: min_G L_render(G) s.t. |G|≤N_target, solved by relaxing each Gaussian's binary existence into a continuous activation c_i=clamp((M_i-0.5)/τ_c+0.5,0,1) (temperature-annealed hard-sigmoid gate) that directly multiplies rendering opacity (α̂_i=c_i·α_i), driven toward the target count via a quadratic budget loss L_budget=(N_p-N_target)² where N_p=Σc_i. Which Gaussians survive is decided by a "Unified Importance Score" M_i combining five geometric/motion cues (positional-gradient sensitivity, peak-not-average opacity to protect transient structures, inverse depth to favor foreground, largest covariance eigenvalue to protect large background-covering Gaussians, and a motion-magnitude term split by static/dynamic membership) with three perceptual cues (per-Gaussian removal-ablation photometric residual, screen-space pixel coverage, inverse cross-view opacity variance to suppress view-inconsistent Gaussians). A separate "Adaptive Dynamic-Static Allocation" module auto-detects the static/dynamic population split by finding the valley between the two modes of a smoothed histogram of per-Gaussian motion magnitude, then gives dynamic regions a denser budget; static Gaussians are compressed with KD-tree spatial reordering + predictive residual + entropy coding, dynamic Gaussians are quantized, reshaped into 2D attribute images grouped by channel, and compressed with a standard H.264 encoder (YUV 4:4:4, CQP=20, I/P-frames only, 3 reference frames — explicitly to keep decode fast). On N3DV they report their large variant (Ours-l, 300k target Gaussians) at 32.14 dB PSNR / 31.5 MB beating Ex4DGS's 32.11 dB / 115 MB, and a small variant (Ours-s, 100k target) at 31.83 dB / 6.8 MB — with precise count control validated to within 0.1-1.4% of the requested Gaussian budget. This is a strong TRANSPORT-stage match for TAYF: an explicit, differentiable "hit this exact compute/bandwidth budget" knob is exactly what a 10 cm battery-constrained cube needs (dial N_target down under thermal throttling or poor network conditions), and reusing an off-the-shelf video codec (H.264) for the dynamic-attribute stream means the transport layer can piggyback on existing hardware video encoders rather than requiring a bespoke entropy coder — the caveat is this is per-scene offline optimization (trained per multi-camera capture, not a feed-forward live encoder), so a real cube would need an amortized version.

### 2603.13699 — D-Compress: Detail-Preserving LiDAR Range Image Compression for Real-Time Streaming on Resource-Constrained Robots

D-Compress targets a different sensing modality — LiDAR point-cloud video compressed as range images for edge-server-assisted robots — combining intra/inter-frame prediction with an adaptive discrete wavelet transform for residual coding, plus a new rate-distortion-optimized (RDO) rate-control algorithm built on a fitted rate-distortion model for range-image compression specifically (rather than reusing perceptually-tuned image/video codecs, which the paper argues discard geometric detail that hurts downstream robotic tasks). On KITTI at 1.55 bits-per-point it reports 67.6 dB PSNR / 1.4% LiDAR mapping error / 53.6% 3D-detection average precision at 25.1 FPS on a low-power Intel i5-7260U mini-PC, beating G-PCC (63.7 dB / 2.0% / 45.5% / 1.8 FPS), H.265 (63.4 dB / 2.7% / 45.2% / 5.1 FPS), and Draco/JPEG2000/WebP, at compression ratios exceeding 100x, while also validating robustness of the RDO rate controller under dynamic (simulated) bandwidth. For TAYF this is not directly about human-avatar Gaussians but is relevant if the cube's capture stage includes any depth/LiDAR sensing for body geometry: it demonstrates that geometry-preserving (not perception-tuned) compression is achievable in real time (25 FPS) on genuinely low-power CPU-only hardware (no GPU), which is a closer power/compute analog to a 10 cm cube's likely compute budget than the RTX-4090-class numbers reported by most Gaussian-splat papers in this batch — the caveat is it compresses raw range/depth data, not an avatar representation, so it would sit upstream of (not replace) a human-representation encoder.

### 2606.22905 — InteractiveAvatar: Real-Time Streaming Video Generation for Consistent and Intent-Aware Avatars

InteractiveAvatar (Xi'an Jiaotong University / China Telecom AI, ECCV-style paper) is a diffusion-based (not Gaussian-splat) streaming avatar generator addressing two failure modes of causal-attention video diffusion: temporal drift over long interactive sessions, and shallow audio-only reactivity. Its Long-Short Visual Memory (LSVM) mechanism maintains two memory pools — short-term (recent frames, for local coherence) and long-term (a compact set of "key frames" selected by a Dynamic Key-Frame Selection strategy that promotes visually important content from short- to long-term memory during inference) — so the causal diffusion transformer attends to compressed historical tokens instead of only the last few chunks, mitigating the drift that plain causal-window methods (CausVid, Self-Forcing) suffer from. Its Reasoning-Reaction Module (RRM) uses an LLM for intent understanding, with a State-Cycling strategy for smooth listening↔execution transitions and a Cache-Switching mechanism that pre-computes/swaps KV caches to reduce perceived action-triggering latency; autoregressive distillation of a bidirectional video diffusion model into a causal one enables real-time infinite-duration streaming generation. No explicit bitrate/PSNR numbers are given in the excerpted method section (evaluation is qualitative/user-study-style for "visual consistency" and "interaction realism"), so its direct transport-efficiency comparability to the Gaussian-splat compression papers in this batch is unclear. For TAYF this is architecturally relevant to the low-latency conversational requirement rather than to bandwidth: the Cache-Switching idea for hiding action-transition latency and the short/long-memory split for avoiding drift over an arbitrarily long call are both applicable design patterns for a cube that must render the remote avatar continuously for a whole conversation without accumulating visible artifacts — but as a diffusion video generator it almost certainly requires a datacenter-class GPU per frame, making it unsuitable as-is for on-cube inference and more a decoder-side cloud-render architecture than a battery-constrained endpoint technique.

### 2606.28828 — Ground4D: Consistency-Aware 4D Reconstruction from Monocular Video

Ground4D addresses a core weakness of dynamic-Gaussian-Splatting-from-monocular-video methods: photometric optimization alone does not enforce multi-view geometric consistency, so per-frame geometry can drift even when rendered images look correct. Its two-stage pipeline first runs VGGT (a pretrained 3D foundation model) training-free to recover multi-view-consistent camera poses and coarse 3D geometry directly from the monocular sequence, which seeds the initialization of a dynamic-Gaussian representation; a second geometry-consistency-aware refinement stage then optimizes the Gaussians via differentiable rendering while explicitly constraining multi-view geometric consistency across both observed and novel/synthesized viewpoints, and the resulting representation supports continuous-time rendering at arbitrary timestamps rather than only the captured frame times. This is best classified as a HUMAN/capture-representation-stage paper that landed in the transport-track keyword match rather than a compression paper — its contribution is about using foundation-model geometric priors to prevent geometry collapse in single-camera 4D capture, directly relevant to TAYF if the cube has only one onboard camera per person (the common case for a compact device), since it substitutes an expensive multi-camera capture rig with a pretrained geometry prior; no bitrate, latency, or on-device inference numbers were found in the excerpted introduction, so its compatibility with real-time/low-power constraints cannot be assessed from what was read, and VGGT-class foundation models are typically large enough (hundreds of millions to billions of parameters) that they would need to run off-cube during avatar enrollment rather than live.

### 2607.08250 — On the Design of Mixture-of-Experts for Dynamic Gaussian Splatting

This IEEE TPAMI paper (Pusan National University / ETRI) studies why no single deformation formulation for dynamic 3D Gaussian Splatting (4DGaussians' HexPlane MLP, E-D3DGS's per-Gaussian embeddings, STG's polynomial motion, Ex4DGS's keyframe interpolation) dominates across scenes, spatial regions, or time, and proposes two Mixture-of-Experts integration strategies. MoDE (Mixture of Deformation Experts) runs several canonical-space deformation experts jointly on a shared set of canonical Gaussians G_i={X_i,r_i,s_i,sigma_i,C_i}; each expert extracts a feature f_i(t)=Psi(G_i,t) and predicts (Delta X, Delta r, Delta s), and a per-Gaussian cubic-Hermite-spline-interpolated gating vector w(t) picks a Top-K sparse softmax combination A(t)=sum_i G_i(t)A_i(t); gradients into the canonical Gaussians are restricted to a single baseline expert to avoid conflicting-gradient instability, plus a random-gating warm-up phase. MoE-GS instead trains heterogeneous experts (deformation-field-based, keyframe-interpolation-based like Ex4DGS, polynomial-based like STG, and static per-frame 3DGS-MCMC experts for rigid backgrounds) fully independently, then fuses them in image space via a "Volume-aware Pixel Router": per-Gaussian routing weights w_i^per=[w_i,w_i^dir,t*w_i^time] are rasterized to pixel-aligned 2D routing features, refined by a small MLP conditioned on view direction and time, then softmax-normalized to blend the experts' rendered images. On the Neural 3D Video dataset MoE-GS(N=4) reaches 33.27 dB average PSNR vs. the best single expert E-D3DGS at 32.33 dB, and on Technicolor MoE-GS(N=3) reaches 34.55 dB vs. best single expert Ex4DGS at 33.45 dB; the efficiency cost is real, however — N=2 MoE-GS runs at only 44 FPS / 878.7 MB vs. Ex4DGS alone at 120 FPS / 122.8 MB, though Gate-Aware Pruning at 75% recovers 101 FPS / 281.3 MB while only dropping PSNR from 32.82 to 32.45. For TAYF this is a rendering/representation-fusion technique rather than a transport technique per se: routing multiple cheap specialized deformation experts (e.g., one tuned for face/hand fine motion, one for torso rigid motion) could raise avatar fidelity on a fixed compute budget, but the 2-4x memory/compute multiplier and non-trivial two-stage training make it a poor fit for a battery-constrained 10 cm receiver unless aggressively pruned to a single low-cost expert at inference.

### 2607.21448 — GrainGS: Gradient-Decoupled Gaussian Splatting for Efficient Dynamic Novel View Synthesis

GrainGS attacks "gradient entanglement" in canonical-space dynamic 3DGS: when canonical geometry and the deformation field share a gradient pathway, deformation gradients leak back into the canonical Gaussians via the chain rule dL/dx_can = dL/dx(t)*(I + d(Delta x)/dx_can), corrupting the supposedly time-invariant reference. The fix is architectural: a Scaffold-GS-style anchor scaffold (sparse anchors from SfM points, each spawning k=10 child Gaussians via a Static MLP) is warmed up alone for T_w=3000 iterations before a DeformNet is enabled; critically, the DeformNet receives a stop-gradient'd canonical position sg(x_can), which algebraically zeroes the entanglement term (d(Delta x)/d sg(x_can)=0) so the scaffold receives only the direct photometric gradient. A second, separate stop-gradient pathway similarly decouples a "canonical View-MLP" (time-invariant color/opacity) from an "Appearance Residual Field" (a small time-conditioned MLP predicting bounded color/opacity residuals, residual scale eta=0.1) so that lighting/shadow changes are absorbed by the residual branch rather than misread as geometric motion. On D-NeRF (8 scenes) GrainGS reaches 36.98 dB mean PSNR, beating SC-GS (36.73) and MoDec-GS (36.45), with the largest gains on fast articulated motion (Jumping Jacks: 38.51 vs 36.83 dB for Deformable-3DGS); on DG-Mesh it is first in PSNR on all 6 real scenes. Efficiency numbers are the headline for TAYF: 435.6 FPS (1.4x D-3DGS, 1.7x SC-GS) at only 4.67 MB of storage per scene (7.0x smaller than SC-GS, 5.1x smaller than 4D-GS) on an RTX 4090; an ablation shows the stop-gradient operator alone is worth +1.34 dB PSNR. This is directly relevant to TAYF's avatar-representation stage: a 4.67 MB dynamic-human asset with 400+ FPS rendering is squarely in the budget for a battery-powered receiver decoding a streamed avatar, and the stop-gradient trick is a cheap, architecture-agnostic fix worth adopting in any canonical-space avatar deformation pipeline — the caveat is that D-NeRF/DG-Mesh are single rigid-object or bounded-motion benchmarks, not full-body human capture with clothing and hands, so gains may not transfer directly.

### 2608.11645 — Cloak of Invisibility: Real-Time Privacy-Preserving Volumetric Video Streaming

This paper (InViStream) addresses a privacy problem specific to multi-camera RGB-D volumetric capture: a bystander or private object missed or only partially masked in one camera view can reappear after 3D fusion even if 2D redaction was applied per-frame. The pipeline runs an off-the-shelf detector (Faster R-CNN ResNet-50-FPN or MobileNetV3, COCO-pretrained, not fine-tuned) only on the first frame of an N-frame "chunk" to keep detection off the critical path; for each sensitive box it computes a depth window mean/std (mu_i, sigma_i, tau_i=alpha*sigma_i) and masks pixels only where both the 2D box AND depth consistency |D(u,v)-mu_i|<=tau_i hold, avoiding both under-masking (box-only) and over-masking (whole-box removal). Same-class public/private disambiguation (e.g., the local participant vs. a background bystander, both detected as "person") is resolved by projecting a reference-view public-instance center through the calibrated extrinsic transform T_{r->w} into each other view and matching to the nearest same-class detection; anything unmatched is conservatively masked as private. Evaluated on 400+ synthetic and 150+ real RGB-D scenes: average synthetic Dice/Recall 0.799/0.891 (SSIM stays above 0.98, meaning public geometry is preserved), real Dice/Recall 0.792/0.908; a downstream re-detection attack shows private-person detectability (PODR) drops from 100% to 6.3% (synthetic) / 14.3% (real) after masking. Runtime: with MobileNet at chunk size N=5, masking is 17.4 ms and throughput reaches 57.5 FPS (12.9 FPS at N=1); ResNet-50 backbone trades accuracy (Dice 0.82 vs MobileNet's 0.80) for latency. For TAYF, this is a capture-stage privacy filter that would sit before the human-representation encoder: since the cube captures a home/office environment (not just the intended subject), source-side per-instance masking of background people/screens before any 3D reconstruction or network transmission is directly applicable, and the sub-20ms/57 FPS MobileNet operating point is compatible with real-time capture, though the system still depends on commodity RGB-D sensor quality (fails near mirrors, reflective/transparent surfaces) which a compact 10 cm sensor suite may struggle to avoid.

### 2502.08297 — BEAM: Bridging Physically-based Rendering and Gaussian Modeling for Relightable Volumetric Video

BEAM (ShanghaiTech/ByteDance/DGene, ACM MM 2025) converts a captured 4D Gaussian human-performance sequence into relightable assets usable by conventional CG/PBR pipelines. It first tracks joint Gaussians (coarse non-rigid motion) and dense skin Gaussians (fine geometry) in a coarse-to-fine optimization with a normal-consistency loss E_normal=sum_i omega_i(1-N_r^T N_d) between rasterized and locally-estimated normals (energy weights lambda_color=1, lambda_smooth=0.001, lambda_temp=0.00005, lambda_normal=0.03), then decouples physically-based-rendering material properties per Gaussian — roughness r, ambient occlusion A(x)=(1/pi)*integral V_env(x,omega_i)(n.omega_i)domega_i, and a simplified Disney BRDF base color rho via a diffuse+Cook-Torrance-specular split of the rendering equation. Roughness comes from a multi-view-conditioned diffusion model (CLAY); AO and base color are estimated per input view via Monte Carlo ray tracing (50 spp AO, 100 spp base color, Intel Open Image Denoise) using a tailored Gaussian ray tracer (3DGRT) that computes visibility as V_env=prod_i(1-o_i*G_i(x_i)) along the ray, then baked into ~140,000 geometry-aware Gaussians per frame. Captured with 81 Z-CAM cameras at 3840x2160/30fps, the pipeline costs 12 min/frame for geometry + 4 min/frame for materials on an RTX 3090, and the relit result achieves AO/base-color/relighting PSNR of 25.32/21.47/26.57 dB vs. 20.67/20.65/24.66 for the best baseline (Relightable-3DGS); real-time playback in Unity runs at 100 FPS at 1080p via deferred shading with GBuffer-baked base color/AO/normal/roughness/depth. For TAYF this targets the reconstruction/rendering stage rather than transport: BEAM demonstrates that a Gaussian-based human avatar can be made relightable and CG-engine-compatible at real-time framerates, which matters if the receiving cube's optical engine needs to composite the remote human into locally-consistent (or artificially chosen) lighting; however its 12+4 minute per-frame offline reconstruction cost makes it usable only for building a persistent enrolled avatar asset, never for live per-frame capture on a 10 cm battery-constrained device.

### 2602.02989 — SharpTimeGS: Sharp and Stable Dynamic Gaussian Splatting via Lifespan Modulation

SharpTimeGS (Tsinghua et al.) identifies that prior motion-based 4D Gaussian methods (4DGS, STGS, FreeTimeGS) use a single Gaussian-shaped temporal opacity/velocity formulation for every primitive regardless of whether it is truly static or transiently dynamic, forcing static points to learn near-zero-but-never-exactly-zero velocities that accumulate drift over long sequences. The fix introduces a learnable per-primitive "lifespan" radius r and variance sigma_t that jointly reparameterize both motion and opacity: position update is X_t=X+v/f(sigma_t,r)*(t-T) with f(sigma_t,r)=1.0+max(1.0,(sigma_t+r)^2), so for static points (large sigma_t+r) the effective velocity v/f->0 and the primitive freezes, while short-lived dynamic points (small f) retain full motion expressiveness; opacity becomes a flat-top function O_t=O*l(t) that is exactly 1 within |t-T|<=r and only decays as a Gaussian outside that window, replacing the smooth bell-curve decay that otherwise forces multiple overlapping Gaussians to approximate a step-like visibility. A velocity-lifespan-aware densification score s=lambda_e*E+lambda_o*O+lambda_l*(1-exp(-(||v||+1)/f(sigma_t,r))) preferentially clones short-lived, fast-moving primitives. On Neural3DV/ENeRF-Outdoor/SelfCap, SharpTimeGS beats FreeTimeGS across all three (PSNR 33.57/25.82/28.14 vs 33.19/25.36/27.50 dB), and an ablation shows removing the lifespan-modulated visibility alone costs ~0.6 dB and produces "temporal blur on moving details, oversmoothing on long-lived structures." It renders up to 4K at 100 FPS on an RTX 4090, but reconstruction is explicitly stated as not real-time (hours of multi-view-video optimization per clip). For TAYF's avatar/pipeline this is again an offline-enrollment-time representation-quality technique: the lifespan trick specifically targets the static-background-vs-moving-human split that a telepresence cube would want (sharp, non-drifting furniture/walls plus crisp fast hand/face motion), and the 100 FPS 4K rendering budget is compatible with a receiver's real-time decode step, but the multi-hour training precludes any on-the-fly per-session capture.

### 2607.01578 — MVFusion-GS: Motion-Variance Guided Temporal Attention for High-Quality Dynamic Gaussian Splatting

Built on top of DeGauss's dual dynamic/static Gaussian decomposition (a HexPlane-based deformation network for foreground Gd(t)=Gd+Delta Gd(t), a static background Gs, and a learned per-pixel blend mask M(t)), this paper's core observation is that standard spatiotemporal deformation grids provide no explicit signal about how much a given Gaussian actually moves, so low-amplitude or transient motion gets under-fit and "pseudo-static" foreground residuals leak into the background branch. Two plug-in modules are added without touching the deformation heads: Motion-Variance Guided Refinement (MVG) periodically (every 2000 iterations, K=64 sampled timestamps) computes a 13-D global trajectory signature per Gaussian v_i=[mean/std of position (6d), mean/std of rotation-magnitude theta (2d), mean/std of isotropic log-scale (2d), mean/std of anisotropic log-scale (2d), motion-intensity score s_intensity=0.7*sigma_pos+0.2*sigma_scale+0.1*sigma_rot (1d)], plus a cheap local-variance dictionary e_i(t) (sparse-sampled, ~30x compressed via linear interpolation) for instantaneous motion cues; MotionFormer Temporal Attention (MFTA) then applies query-centered cross-attention over a w=5-frame sliding window of deformation tokens z_{i,t}=[h_{i,t}, Phi(h_{i,t}), e_i(t)] to inject short-term temporal context. Both feed into the same unmodified deformation decoder: Delta G_d^MR(t)=Phi(h_base(t)+h_MVG(t)+h_MFTA(t)). On distractor-free reconstruction (NeRF On-the-go) MVFusion-GS improves LPIPS over DeGauss on every scene (mean 0.085 vs 0.113) while matching PSNR/SSIM, and on dynamic-scene reconstruction (Neu3D) it reaches 32.07 dB mean PSNR vs DeGauss's 31.52 and a foreground-region PSNR of 31.78 vs SpaceTimeGS's 30.85. For TAYF this is a foreground/background separation and motion-fidelity technique applicable to the capture-side human-segmentation stage: cleanly separating a moving human from a static room background (rather than conflating subtle motion like breathing or hand tremor with the background) would improve both the compactness of what needs to be streamed and the visual cleanliness of the reconstructed remote scene; it is a training-time refinement (30k iterations on an RTX 3090) rather than an inference-time technique, so it targets avatar/asset quality rather than live per-frame streaming cost.

### 2302.01532 — INV: Towards Streaming Incremental Neural Videos

This paper's central empirical finding — that MLP-based NeRFs naturally partition into early "Structure Layers" encoding geometry and later "Color Layers" encoding appearance, verified via layer-swap experiments where substituting only the first 1-7 layers between adjacent-frame models induces coherent structural change (head rotation, smile appearing) with no retraining — is exploited for extreme temporal compression. Incremental Transfer (I.T.) initializes each frame's NeRF from the previous frame's weights (motivated by lim_{Delta t->0} Delta Phi = 0), cutting 100k-iteration from-scratch training to a fraction while reaching comparable PSNR (31.31 dB at 10k iters/frame vs 31.75 dB for from-scratch NeRF at 100k on "cut roasted steak"), but naive I.T. still flickers and costs 40.1 GB for a 5-minute 30 FPS video. The INV representation resolves both: only 3 per-frame Structure Layers Phi_i^s are stored/retrained per frame (mapping coordinates to a latent v), while a single Shared Color Layer Phi^c (frozen after an initial warm-up) maps v to RGB-sigma for every frame, cutting weight size to 1.12 MB/frame (24.6% of a 4.43 MB full NeRF) and — critically — eliminating flicker because appearance is provably identical across frames. A further Temporal Weight Compression step concatenates consecutive frames' Structure-Layer weight matrices and runs 16-bit FPZIP floating-point compression, reaching 0.3 MB/frame after the one-time 3.29 MB Shared Color Layer transfer — i.e., only 72 Mbit/s at 30 FPS, and INV+TWC achieves 28.77 dB PSNR at just 8 minutes/frame of training vs. DyNeRF's much longer per-clip optimization. For TAYF this is a genuinely novel transport-layer idea for a NeRF-style (as opposed to explicit Gaussian) avatar representation: separating a per-frame-updated "structure" codec stream from a persistent, rarely-updated "color/style" codec stream maps directly onto TAYF's enrollment-vs-dynamic-state architecture (Sec. 14-15 of the project notes) and could in principle be adapted to Gaussian avatars (freeze appearance MLP/SH, stream only per-Gaussian deformation deltas); the caveat is 72 Mbit/s is still far above what an "ordinary network" conversational link can guarantee, and 8+ minutes of per-frame training makes this an offline/enrollment technique, not live capture.

### 2312.03461 — HiFi4G: High-Fidelity Human Performance Rendering via Compact Gaussian Splatting

HiFi4G (CVPR 2024) fuses traditional non-rigid tracking with 3D Gaussian Splatting via a "dual graph" design to get both high fidelity and streamable compression. A coarse deformation graph (Embedded Deformation, following DynamicFusion-style non-rigid tracking) parameterizes motion as dual quaternions dq_i on ED nodes x_i, with per-point transforms obtained by Dual-Quaternion Blending DQB(v_c)=sum_{i in N(v_c)} w(x_i,v_c) dq_i over NeuS2-derived per-frame mesh proxies; a fine-grained Gaussian graph (KNN=16 among Gaussians, KNN=8 to ED nodes) is initialized each frame by warping the previous keyframe's Gaussians via the ED motion field, then refined with a temporal-consistency loss E_temp (L2 penalty on appearance attributes vs. previous frame) and an as-rigid-as-possible smoothness loss E_smooth on relative Gaussian positions, both modulated by a motion-adaptive weight w_{i,t}=exp(-alpha||p'_{i,t}-p'_{i,t-1}||^2) that relaxes the constraint in high-motion regions (human vision is less sensitive to detail there) — alpha=50 empirically. A compression stage takes advantage of the resulting compact, temporally-smooth 4D Gaussians: keyframe attributes are retained in full, non-key frames store only residuals (subtracting motion-compensated warped keyframe positions/rotations), values are quantized (9-bit appearance/0-bit motion at keyframes, 7-bit appearance/11-bit motion at non-key frames), and RANS (range asymmetric numeral system) entropy-codes the resulting zero-centered residual distribution, achieving ~25x compression to under 2 MB/frame. Captured with 81 Z-CAM cameras at 3840x2160/30fps, HiFi4G reaches 36.2 dB PSNR / 0.989 SSIM before compression and 35.8 dB / 0.986 SSIM after, beating HumanRF (31.2 dB, 11.38 MB/frame) and Dynamic 3D Gaussians (30.2 dB, 4.5 MB/frame) on both quality and size, with the whole per-frame pipeline (matting, meshing, tracking, Gaussian optimization, compression) taking under 7 minutes on an RTX 3090. This is one of the most directly relevant transport papers for TAYF: sub-2-MB/frame compact 4D Gaussian human performance at 30 fps is in the right ballpark for a compressed avatar stream (2 MB/frame * 30 fps = 480 Mbit/s uncompressed-per-second equivalent — still too high for "ordinary network" real-time transmission without further temporal delta-coding across frames, but it establishes that per-frame Gaussian-attribute residual+RANS coding is a viable direction); the fundamental limiter for TAYF is that the ~7-minute-per-frame optimization is offline reconstruction, wholly incompatible with live capture, so at best this informs the codec design of a pre-baked playback avatar, not conversational telepresence.

### 2312.03704 — Relightable Gaussian Codec Avatars

This Meta/CMU paper (CVPR 2024 highlight) builds photorealistic, drivable, and relightable head avatars from 3D Gaussians anchored to a UV-parameterized coarse template mesh, decoded by 2D CNNs from a facial-expression latent z (via a conditional VAE encoder taking tracked mesh vertices V and an averaged texture T) and eye-gaze e_{l,r}; Gaussian position is t_k = interpolated-coarse-mesh-position + decoded offset delta_t_k, avoiding poor local minima under large expression changes. Appearance is split into a diffuse term computed via spherical-harmonic radiance transfer c_diffuse=rho_k (dot) sum L_i*d_k^i (up to 3rd-order RGB SH plus 4th-8th-order monochrome-only SH to keep shadow detail tractable) and a specular term using a normalized angle-based spherical Gaussian lobe G_s(p;q,sigma)=C*exp(-0.5*(arccos(p.q)/sigma)^2) whose axis q_k is the per-Gaussian reflection vector, with a learnable view-conditioned normal that can rotate along a hair fiber's tangent (unlike a fixed surface normal) so the same formulation handles both skin and hair-strand-like thin structures; because the specular integral reduces to a single environment-map mipmap lookup per Gaussian, all-frequency real-time relighting is achievable. An explicit two-sphere eyeball model (from prior EEM work) with frozen Gaussian positions/normals is needed because the cornea's near-delta-function point-light reflections otherwise collapse 3DGS optimization into poor local minima. Captured with 110 cameras/460 time-multiplexed LED lights at 4096x2668/90Hz (~144,000 frames per subject), the method beats a linear-neural-network relighting baseline and EyeNeRF-style SH-specular baselines on held-out illumination, expression, and view, and is demonstrated driving avatars live from a VR headset. For TAYF's HUMAN track this is the highest-fidelity relightable Gaussian avatar reference available: the diffuse/specular SH+SG decomposition and single-mip-lookup specular evaluation are compatible with real-time rendering on a receiving cube, and the CVAE-based expression latent is exactly the kind of compact "dynamic state" (256-D z plus gaze) TAYF's architecture wants to stream instead of raw geometry — but the enrollment cost (110-camera, 460-light capture rig, 4 A100 GPUs for 200k iterations) is a research-lab-only setup, and the eye/hair-specific machinery targets close-up facial fidelity rather than the whole-body capture a 10 cm cube would need.

### 2401.08742 — Efficient4D: Fast Dynamic 3D Object Generation from a Single-view Video

Efficient4D generates a 4D (dynamic 3D) object from a single fixed-camera monocular video by first synthesizing an M(time) x N(view) "image matrix" via an enhanced multi-view diffusion model (SyncDreamer-T), then fitting a 4D Gaussian Splatting model to the synthesized images. SyncDreamer-T's key modification adds a smoothing filter across per-timestamp 3D-aware spatial feature volumes V used to condition the synchronized N-view noise predictor: at each denoising step the volume is time-averaged over a window, Ṽ_i = sum_{j=-k}^{k} w_{i+j} V_{i+j}, which is training-free (built on a frozen pretrained SyncDreamer) and empirically shown to trade off temporal texture consistency against motion independence as the fusion ratio w approaches 0.5; video-frame interpolation (RIFE) then quadruples temporal resolution (M=4K-3 from K input frames) to fix low-frame-rate discontinuities. For reconstruction, since the generated image matrix has view/frame-dependent noise and inconsistency, the paper replaces plain L1+SSIM 4DGS loss with a confidence-weighted variant L_img-conf where per-pixel confidence C_rgb=1-|I-Î| and C_ssim=SSIM(I,Î) (Î being an optical-flow-unwarped neighbor-frame image) down-weight unreliable regions, plus a lightly-weighted SDS loss from a video diffusion prior for smooth novel-view transitions. The full pipeline takes only ~10 minutes to model a dynamic object end-to-end (2 min generation with parallel denoising + 8 min 4DGS reconstruction on an A6000) versus 120 minutes for the prior SOTA (Consistent4D) — a reported 10x speedup — and works from as few as two input images. For TAYF this targets the capture/reconstruction stage for objects or humans observed from a single camera, which is architecturally close to what a monocular consumer-facing cube camera would see; the ~10-minute reconstruction remains far too slow for live conversational capture, but the underlying idea — using diffusion-model view synthesis to hallucinate missing viewpoints from sparse/single-camera input, then fitting a fast Gaussian representation — is relevant to TAYF's stated hypothesis that a small number of physical camera views plus neural view synthesis could substitute for a full multi-camera capture rig, provided the generation step can eventually be pushed to real-time.

<!-- SECTION3-END -->

## Track 4 — Perception & Presence (thresholds, quality, psychophysics)

### 2207.09956 — Telepresence Video Quality Assessment

This paper builds the first large-scale Telepresence Video Quality Assessment (TVQA) dataset and a matching online multimodal quality predictor. The dataset comprises 2,320 real-world video-conferencing clips (Zoom/Webex/Meet-style, sourced from 80 countries via Internet Archive and geofenced YouTube search) rated by 526 AMT subjects, yielding 78,880 opinion scores on a continuous scale; a "soft" subject-recovery model (rather than ITU BT.500 hard rejection) is used to clean scores, and data-quality is validated via inter-subject SRCC=0.765 and intra-subject (golden-video) LCC=0.845. The prediction model, Tele-VQA, extracts frame-level features (MobileNetV3, 960-d, avg-pooled to 2880-d), patch-level features via RoIPool over a $2^d\times2^d$ grid for $d=1..4$ (concatenated to 341-d per scale, RoIPool defined by $(\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{p}_{RoI})(P)\approx(\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{p})(P_{RoI})$), clip-level spatiotemporal features (R(2+1)D pretrained on Kinetics, 1536-d), and audio features (YAMNet on STFT spectrograms); visual (4757-d) and audio (1536-d) pathways feed separate GRU-FCN time-series regressors so the model runs online, one frame/clip/audio-chunk at a time, with hidden state carried across steps. Audio-visual fusion uses the ITU-endorsed KPN model $S^{(a+v)}=1.12+0.007\,S^{(a)}+0.24\,S^{(v)}+0.088\,S^{(a)}S^{(v)}$, and missing-modality handling defaults each stream to 3.0 ("Fair") until a real prediction arrives. Tele-VQA improves SRCC by 9.8% over the best baseline on the new database (audio contributing +2.4%), and the ablation shows image-level features dominate over patch-level for telepresence content while clip-level (motion) features—critical for generic UGC video—contribute least for talking-head telepresence. For TAYF this is a Track D reference: it is a concrete, validated online (single-frame-latency) quality predictor architecture for exactly the video-call setting TAYF must eventually beat, and its finding that spatial/frame-level cues dominate over motion cues for telepresence content argues that TAYF's optical/representation budget should prioritize per-frame facial/appearance fidelity over motion-artifact suppression; the missing-modality fallback logic is also directly reusable for TAYF's audio/video desync handling.

### 2211.12046 — DP-NeRF: Deblurred Neural Radiance Field with Physical Scene Priors

DP-NeRF extends Deblur-NeRF by imposing two physical priors on the blur-formation process to keep the recovered NeRF 3D-consistent, rather than optimizing an independent 2D blur kernel per pixel. It formulates blurring as $\hat B^p=\hat C^p*h^p$ and models the kernel with a Rigid Blurring Kernel (RBK): a scene-wide $SE(3)$ deformation field $\mathcal{S}_s=(\mathcal{R}(E(l_s));\mathfrak{L}(E(l_s)))$ (screw-axis rotation via Rodrigues' formula, $e^{r_s}=I+\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}[r_s]_\times+\frac{1-\cos\theta}{\theta^2}[r_s^2]_\times$) transforms each pixel's ray by $k$ camera-motion hypotheses shared across the whole image ("Ray Rigid Transformation"), and Coarse Composition Weights $\mathfrak{m}_{s;0..k}=\sigma(\mathcal{W}(E(l_s)))$ (summing to 1) blend the $k{+}1$ rendered colors into the blurred pixel—both conditioned only on the scene/view latent code, not on 2D pixel position, which is the key departure from Deblur-NeRF. An Adaptive Weight Proposal (AWP) module then refines these weights per-pixel using depth features pulled from the NeRF's penultimate layer, aggregated through a motion-aggregation module with inter-sample/inter-motion correlation (CurveNet-style), because motion and defocus blur are depth-dependent. Training uses a coarse-to-fine loss $\mathcal{L}_{final}=\lambda\|B-\hat B\|+(1-\lambda)\|B-\tilde B\|$ with $\lambda$ exponentially decayed from 0.9 to 0.1, $k=4$ camera-motion hypotheses, 200k iterations. On the Deblur-NeRF synthetic/real benchmarks DP-NeRF improves PSNR from 28.77→29.23 dB (camera motion blur, synthetic, averaged over 5 scenes) and 28.37→29.33 dB (defocus), with consistent LPIPS gains (e.g., 0.1400→0.1184). For TAYF's capture stage, this is directly relevant to onboard, low-cost cameras with motion blur (a moving human, handheld or vibrating cube) or shallow-DoF optics: it shows that sharing a single rigid-motion prior across an entire frame — rather than solving per-pixel deblur — is both physically correct and yields cleaner 3D geometry, which matters more for TAYF than 2D image sharpness since the reconstructed avatar's geometry feeds a free-space optical engine; the 200k-iteration/per-scene optimization cost, however, is far from real-time and would need to be replaced by a feed-forward variant for a live cube.

### 2211.16630 — DINER: Depth-aware Image-based NEural Radiance fields

DINER targets sparse-view (N=4), generalizable head/object NeRF reconstruction by injecting depth priors from TransMVSNet into a pixelNeRF-style pipeline. For each sample point $\mathbf{x}$, it computes the signed distance to the estimated per-view depth $\Delta_z^{(i)}=\mathbf{D}^{(i)}(\mathbf{K}^{(i)}\!\circ\!\mathbf{x}^{(i)})-\mathbf{x}^{(i)}_{[z]}$ and feeds this as extra conditioning to the NeRF MLP alongside standard pixelNeRF features. To fix smearing artifacts in regions outside the four source-view frusta, source images are border-padded and given positional-encoded padding channels before feature extraction, so the encoder can regress distinctive (non-constant) features in extrapolated regions. The paper's headline contribution is Depth-Guided Sampling: candidate points along a ray are scored by a per-view surface-likelihood Gaussian $p^{(i)}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\int_{z-\delta/2}^{z+\delta/2}e^{-\frac12(\frac{z'-\mu}{\sigma})^2}dz'$ (using the predicted depth mean/std as $\mu,\sigma$), max-pooled across the 4 input views after backface culling, then an occlusion-aware termination likelihood $p_{oa}(\mathbf{x})=p(\mathbf{x})\prod_{\tilde x_{[t]}<x_{[t]}}(1-p(\tilde x))$ is fit with a Gaussian to bias additional samples near the expected surface — this concentrates NeRF evaluations on the actual surface (thin structures like ears) and lets sample count per ray drop 4×, freeing GPU memory to raise batch size 1→4 and stabilize training. Loss combines L1, a VGG perceptual term, and a novel anti-bias L1 loss on k-fold downsampled images to kill the color-shift the perceptual loss otherwise introduces. On FaceScape (400k images, 359 subjects, ~60-camera rig) DINER beats pixelNeRF/IBRNet/KeypointNeRF on LPIPS (0.099 vs 0.148–0.165) while matching PSNR/SSIM, and it generalizes cross-ethnicity/headwear to the Multiface dataset despite training only on Asian subjects in swim caps; per the paper, it also shows high temporal stability on continuous sequences despite training only on static frames. For TAYF, this is a strong candidate for the capture→representation stage with a small onboard camera rig (as few as 4 views): depth-guided sampling is a direct, non-learned way to cut NeRF/volumetric compute 4× on embedded hardware, and the extrapolation-padding trick addresses exactly the "camera can't see the back of the head" problem a single small cube will face; the explicit caution about ethnic/headwear bias in training data is also a concrete fairness caveat for a global two-cube product.

### 2303.15206 — Perceptual Quality Assessment of NeRF and Neural View Synthesis Methods for Front-Facing Views

This CVPR/TVCG-track paper is the most directly load-bearing Track-D result found so far: it builds two new datasets with reference *videos* (not just sparse images) — a controlled 6-scene "Lab" set captured on a 2D gantry inside a 30×41.5×38 cm box (specular/glass/metal objects, camera re-projection error 0.217 px) and a 9-scene in-the-wild "Fieldwork" set (COLMAP-calibrated, reprojection error 0.53 px) — and runs a rigorous pairwise-comparison psychophysics study (39 observers, Ishihara-screened, ASAP active sampling, 4K calibrated monitor at 200 cd/m², 70 cm viewing distance, 40 px/degree effective resolution) scored in Just-Objectionable-Difference (JOD) units via the Thurstone Case V model, where 1 JOD = a difference detected by 75% of viewers. Ten NVS methods (NeRF, Mip-NeRF, DVGO, Plenoxels, NeX, LFNR, IBRNet, GNT, cross-scene vs. per-scene fine-tuned variants) are benchmarked, and the key numeric finding is that even the best methods sit ~0.85–0.9 JOD below ground-truth reference video (0.94 vs 0.14 JOD on Lab, 1.9 vs 1.0 JOD-scale on Fieldwork), meaning the reference is still picked as better in ~70% of forced-choice trials — i.e., current SOTA NVS is measurably, reliably distinguishable from real video by naive human observers, a hard number for TAYF's "convincing enough" target. On metric validity: PSNR correlates with human judgment significantly better than SSIM or LPIPS for NVS-specific artifacts (LPIPS's patch-based training distribution doesn't match NVS blur/ghosting artifacts); crucially, video-aware metrics (VMAF-video, FovVideoVDP-video) beat the same metrics run per-frame, and correlation rises monotonically as more video frames are supplied instead of sparse images — demonstrating that *temporal* distortion, not per-frame sharpness, is what human observers actually penalize, with a documented failure case where GNT/IBRNet-C degrade over 1–4s (JOD≈−5) while PSNR stays flat at ~25 dB. For TAYF Track D this gives calibrated targets and warns against optimizing a single-frame PSNR/SSIM loss for the free-space reconstruction pipeline: the render/optical engine must be evaluated and tuned on temporal/video-domain perceptual metrics (or JOD-style human studies), because per-frame image-quality numbers can look fine while a moving avatar is perceptually falling apart frame-to-frame — exactly the "boiling"/flicker failure mode already flagged in TAYF's own notes.

### 2403.04508 — Finding Waldo: Towards Efficient Exploration of NeRF Scene Spaces

This paper formalizes "scene exploration" of an already-trained NeRF as a constrained search over the continuous 5D camera-pose space $f(\mathbf{x};\zeta):(x,y,z,\theta,\phi)\to(\mathbf{c},\sigma)$, seeking poses $(\hat x,\hat y,\hat z,\hat\theta,\hat\phi)$ whose rendered image satisfies $\nabla\mathbb{A}<\epsilon_A,\ \nabla\mathbb{G}<\epsilon_G$ for an arbitrary externally-supplied appearance/geometry scoring function $A(\cdot)/G(\cdot)$ (e.g. an aesthetics model, an IQA model, a saliency detector, an object detector). Two naive baselines are defined — Guided Random Search (GRS: samples origins uniformly within the training-pose bounding box, using averaged lookAt/up vectors) and Pose-Interpolation-Based Search (PIBS: SLERP-interpolates between top-scoring training-pose pairs) — against which the paper's contribution, Evolution-Guided Pose Search (EGPS), a genetic algorithm that crossover-averages up-vectors and uniformly interpolates origin/lookAt between two score-biased parent poses, then applies a bounded mutation $\delta\sim PMF(-\nabla,0,\nabla;0.05,0.9,0.05)$ where $\nabla$ is set to 10% of the parent-pair distance. Across three use cases — SAMP-Net photo-composition-rule scoring, CONTRIQUE no-reference IQA maximization, and BASNet saliency-pixel-count maximization — EGPS wins decisively in the "high-pose" regime (e.g. saliency CVIR of 60.8 vs GRS's 16.2 on the "horns" scene) and the CONTRIQUE experiment incidentally shows that even well-trained NeRFs reliably contain artifact-laden novel views the IQA model can detect despite CONTRIQUE never being trained on NeRF artifacts. For TAYF this is a pose/viewpoint-optimization tool rather than a capture or optical technique per se: once a person's dynamic NeRF/Gaussian avatar exists, EGPS-style evolutionary pose search over the renderer is a plausible mechanism for a cube to autonomously discover which physical view directions are cleanest (least NeRF-artifact) for its sparse set of optical emission channels, effectively answering "where should our few physical light-field views point" — though the per-scene evolutionary search (hundreds of new renders per epoch) is compute-heavy and was demonstrated only on static scenes, so it would need to be amortized/learned for TAYF's real-time dynamic-human case.

### 2405.02005 — HoloGS: Instant Depth-based 3D Gaussian Splatting with Microsoft HoloLens 2

HoloGS replaces the standard SfM (COLMAP feature-matching) pre-processing step of 3D Gaussian Splatting with direct depth-sensor streaming from a HoloLens 2, aiming for "instant" (no offline SfM) 3DGS initialization. Its pipeline streams RGB (1920×1080), camera poses/intrinsics, and depth images from the device's onboard tracking/depth sensors in real time; each depth image is back-projected pixel-by-pixel into a 3D point using the depth camera's known intrinsics, and per-frame point clouds are merged via the device's own SLAM poses into a single dense initialization cloud, which then seeds Gaussian centers exactly as in the vanilla 3DGS training loop (30k iterations, standard loss $L=(1-\lambda)L_1+\lambda L_{D\text{-}SSIM}$, $\lambda=0.2$). On two captured scenes (statue "Denker," plant "Ficus," 111 images each, masked beyond 3 m depth), the HoloLens-seeded point cloud is far denser than SfM's sparse cloud (620k vs. ~40k points) yet trains to substantially *lower* final quality: PSNR after 30k iterations is 20.17–20.55 dB with HoloLens initialization versus 26.21–27.54 dB with SfM, with HoloGS renders showing blurry edges, floater artifacts, and noisy/fuzzy point-cloud surfaces (especially failing on the fine branching structure of the plant) despite converging faster (~25k iterations) and more smoothly than SfM. This is a directly relevant negative capture-stage result for TAYF: an onboard consumer depth sensor (structured light / ToF, comparable to a HoloLens or a phone LiDAR) is attractive for a 10 cm cube because it removes the SfM bottleneck and gives instant geometric initialization, but this paper's numbers show depth-sensor initialization alone costs ~6–7 dB of reconstruction quality versus photogrammetric SfM — meaning TAYF likely needs either a hybrid initialization (sparse SfM refinement layered on top of depth-sensor point clouds) or acceptance of a real fidelity ceiling if it forgoes SfM for real-time onboard depth capture.

### 2408.04586 — Sampling for View Synthesis: From Local Light Field Fusion to Neural Radiance Fields and Beyond

Written by a co-author of the original LLFF paper for a Frontiers of Science retrospective, this is a rigorous derivation of the minimum camera-sampling rate for view synthesis and is exceptionally relevant to how many physical optical/camera channels TAYF actually needs. It shows that naive Nyquist-rate light-field sampling is intractable — for a subject at 0.5 m depth, a 64° FOV phone camera, and 1-megapixel target resolution, the required sampling density is ~2.5 million images per square meter — but that decomposing the scene into $D$ depth layers (a multiplane image, MPI) relaxes the required camera-sampling interval to $\Delta_u\le \frac{D}{2K_xf(1/z_{min}-1/z_{max})}$ (accounting for occlusion, which otherwise doubles the required rate by expanding the Fourier double-wedge spectrum into a parallelogram), combined with a field-of-view constraint $\Delta_u\le \frac{W\Delta_x z_{min}}{2f}$. Reformulated in terms of maximum inter-view pixel disparity $d_{max}$, the combined bound is simply $d_{max}\le\min(D,W/2)$ — i.e. a $D$-plane MPI tolerates $D$ pixels of disparity between adjacent captured views instead of the Nyquist-limit's 1 pixel — and because sampling is 2D, using $D=64$ layers gives a validated $D^2\approx4096\times$ reduction in the number of views needed, reducing "millions of images" to the 20–50 casually-captured photos LLFF actually requires; the paper's own validation plot confirms perceptually-matched (Nyquist-equivalent) quality is maintained up to $d_{max}=64$ pixels when plane count matches disparity. The article also explicitly names the field's biggest scientific gap: NeRF, Gaussian splatting, and generative single-image view-synthesis methods that superseded LLFF have *no* equivalent sampling guarantee — nobody has re-derived a Nyquist-style bound for how many physical views a neural/Gaussian representation needs before angular aliasing appears. For TAYF this is the closest thing in the literature to a formal answer to "how many physical camera/light-field channels does the cube need": the $d_{max}\le D$ relation is a design equation TAYF can apply directly to size its sparse capture rig and its sparse optical-emission channel count (both bounded by the same depth-layered light-field logic), but the paper's closing point is a genuine open problem the project would have to solve itself for its chosen neural avatar representation.

### 2408.04831 — AugGS: Self-augmented Gaussians with Structural Masks for Sparse-view 3D Reconstruction

AugGS targets 360°, 4-view (or 9-view) sparse-input 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstruction — directly relevant to a small cube's minimal camera count — via a two-stage, self-contained training scheme that avoids any external generative prior beyond a LoRA-fine-tuned ControlNet trained only on the input's own noised renders. Stage 1 trains a coarse Gaussian set $\mathcal{P}_c=\{p_i:\mu_i,q_i,s_i,\sigma_i,sh_i\}$ from the 4 sparse views to get rough global geometry; stage 2 renders novel views from these coarse Gaussians, adds 3D noise to Gaussian attributes and denoises the resulting degraded renders with a Stable-Diffusion-v1.5-based ControlNet whose LoRA weights are fine-tuned only on this scene's own noisy/clean render pairs (i.e., a self-augmentation/pseudo-labeling loop, not an external 2D prior), producing an augmented image set $X^{aug}=X^{ref}\cup\tilde X^c$ used to retrain finer Gaussians retaining only position $\mu_i$ and color $c_i$. Two structural masking regularizers stabilize densification under sparsity: point-based random masking (drop a fraction $r_c$ of Gaussian centers every $t_c$ iterations in stage 1) and patch-based masking (FPS + kNN patches of the point cloud, drop fraction $r_f$ every $t_f$ iterations in stage 2), plus a monocular depth loss with median/MAD normalization $D^*=(D-\text{median}(D))/\frac1M\sum|D-\text{median}(D)|$ against ZoeDepth-predicted depth. Compared against GaussianObject, 3DGS, RegNeRF, DietNeRF, SparseNeRF, ZeroRF, and FSGS on MipNeRF360 and OmniObject3D with only 4 input views, AugGS improves LPIPS by 5–30%, PSNR by 8–25%, and SSIM by 5–10% over the strongest baseline (GaussianObject) while training and rendering faster. For TAYF's capture stage, this is a strong candidate for the realistic case of 3–4 tiny onboard cameras (not a studio rig): the self-supervised augmentation loop (no external 2D diffusion prior needed at inference, only a one-time per-scene LoRA fit) and the structural masks for stabilizing sparse-view densification are both practical, hardware-light techniques for filling in unseen body/head regions from a cube's necessarily narrow-baseline camera set, though the reported training curves (many thousand iterations to converge) still imply per-capture optimization rather than a truly real-time, feed-forward telepresence pipeline.

### 2410.20686 — ODGS: 3D Scene Reconstruction from Omnidirectional Images with 3D Gaussian Splattings

ODGS re-derives the 3D-Gaussian-Splatting rasterizer for 360° equirectangular-projection (ERP) cameras instead of standard perspective cameras, which is directly relevant if TAYF's capture cube uses a single omnidirectional sensor rather than several narrow-FOV cameras. Because the standard 3DGS Jacobian $\mathbf{J}=\partial\pi(\mu)/\partial\mu$ assumes a linear perspective projection, the paper instead rotates a virtual unit-focal-length perspective camera to align its optical axis with each Gaussian's direction $\hat\mu=\mu/\|\mu\|$ (rotation $\mathbf{T}_\mu$ built from azimuth/elevation Rodrigues rotations), computes a simplified local Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_o=\mathrm{diag}(1/\|\mu\|,1/\|\mu\|)$ at that tangent point, then maps the resulting 2D covariance onto the equirectangular pixel grid via a distortion-correcting factor $\mathbf{Q}_o=\mathrm{diag}(\sec\theta_\mu,1)$ (correcting the $\sec\theta$ horizontal stretching that ERP introduces near the poles) and a pixel-scale matrix $\mathbf{S}_o=\mathrm{diag}(W/2\pi,H/\pi)$, yielding a closed-form Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_{omni}=\mathbf{S}_o\mathbf{Q}_o\mathbf{J}_o\mathbf{T}_\mu$ that the paper proves matches direct differentiation of the ERP projection function. Because Gaussians near the poles render disproportionately larger under ERP, the paper also proposes an elevation-adaptive densification threshold $\tau_\mu=\tau_{min}+(1-\cos\theta_\mu)(\tau_{max}-\tau_{min})$ to prevent runaway over-densification at the poles, and replaces perspective frustum culling with spherical-shell culling. The fully-differentiable CUDA rasterizer is otherwise a drop-in replacement for standard 3DGS training/rendering. For TAYF, ODGS is a directly usable capture-stage building block if the cube uses a single fisheye/360° sensor (a natural fit for a compact, all-around-aware 10 cm device rather than multiple perspective cameras): it removes the need to stitch/rectify ERP video into perspective tiles before feeding a Gaussian pipeline, at the cost of the pole-distortion-correction machinery above; the paper's own comparisons (evaluated at 10 and 100 minutes optimization time) still target offline per-scene training, so real-time adaptation would be needed for a live avatar capture use case.

### 2412.08029 — NeRF-NQA: No-Reference Quality Assessment for Scenes Generated by NeRF and Neural View Synthesis Methods

NeRF-NQA is a no-reference (no ground-truth needed) perceptual quality predictor for NVS-rendered scenes, built from two complementary modules that map directly onto the two artifact classes TAYF's rendering pipeline will produce. The Viewwise module runs a per-view IQA feature extractor (BRISQUE-style natural-scene-statistics features) followed by an EfficientNetV2-inspired stack (fused-MBConv + MBConv blocks with squeeze-and-excitation, global max-pooling for variable-length view sequences) along the camera trajectory, capturing spatial/per-frame degradation. The Pointwise module is the technical contribution: it back-projects synthesized views via COLMAP to sparse 3D surface points, and for each surface point collects all pixels from different views that observe it, computing a Normalized Spherical Gradient $NSG(x_i,x_j)=\frac{I(x_i)-I(x_j)}{\angle x_iox_j}$ between angularly-adjacent observing pixels — i.e., literally measuring angular (view-dependent) color inconsistency, binned along azimuth and polar angles into a Pointwise Normalized Spherical Gradient (PNSG) map, then compressed by 3D convolutions + PointNet into inter-point features; viewwise and pointwise features are fused by an MLP into a final quality score. On three benchmark datasets (Fieldwork, LLFF, Lab), NeRF-NQA beats every tested full-reference IQA metric (PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, DISTS, etc.), no-reference IQA metric (BRISQUE, NIQE, CLIP-IQA), video-quality metric (VMAF, FovVideoVDP), and light-field-IQA metric (LFACon), e.g. RMSE 0.591 / SRCC 0.902 on LLFF versus the second-best method's RMSE 0.907 / SRCC ~0.617 (LFACon) — a >30% RMSE improvement — and an ablation confirms the pointwise (angular) module alone drives most of the SRCC gain (0.74→0.90 on LLFF when added). This is a directly usable Track-D evaluation tool for TAYF: because it requires no reference video and explicitly quantifies angular/view-consistency error via the spherical-gradient formulation, it is well-suited to scoring TAYF's free-space light-field/multiplane reconstructions in the field (no ground truth available at a live two-cube call) and specifically to catching the "different views of the same rebuilt human disagree" failure mode that a sparse-view or sparse-emitter optical engine is most likely to produce.

### 2503.07828 — Neural Radiance and Gaze Fields for Visual Attention Modeling in 3D Environments

NeRG (Neural Radiance and Gaze Fields) augments a pretrained NeRF with a lightweight MLP that predicts 3D gaze probability density, decoupling the observer's viewpoint from the rendering camera. The model factors gaze into an "emit" term F_e(p_od, -r_o) evaluated at the visible surface and a "capture" term sigmoid(F_c(p_o, r_o)) evaluated at the observer position, combined multiplicatively as G(p_od,p_o) = F_e(p_od,-r_o) x sigmoid(F_c(p_o,r_o)); the sigmoid represents the probability that an emitted attentional signal is actually perceived by a given observer. Gaze occlusion (analogous to shadowing) is handled by comparing NeRF-estimated depth d_o from the observer's perspective against the expected depth d_o* = ||p_o - p_d||, with a soft falloff factor s = (d_o* - d_o)/d_f clipped to [0,1] to avoid hard shadow-like artifacts. Training uses 2.6M head-pose rays from a real retail skeleton-tracking dataset, aggregated into "gaze probes" (von Mises-Fisher KDE over local 360-degree ray bundles within R=0.1m) as supervision, with a combined KLD + linear-correlation + MAE loss.
