The full emit+capture model is the best-performing ablation (normalized loss 1.00 vs. 2.05 for emit-only, 1.27 for capture-only) and renders a 720p frame with gaze overlay in under 100ms versus 5+ seconds for raw KDE gaze-probe computation. For TAYF this is a perception-track tool for the display side: rather than uniformly reconstructing the remote human's entire body at full angular/spatial fidelity, a gaze-field model conditioned on the local observer's estimated head position could tell the optical engine which regions of the reconstructed human (face vs. periphery) actually need high-fidelity light-field information for that specific viewer, directly operationalizing TAYF's "perceptual sparsification" strategy — though the demonstrated system only predicts where a human would look, not yet how much fidelity loss elsewhere goes unnoticed, and it presumes a fairly static known scene geometry rather than a real-time streamed avatar.

### 2503.21226 — Frequency-Aware Gaussian Splatting Decomposition

This method groups 3D Gaussians into discrete "levels" that each correspond to one subband of an image Laplacian pyramid, giving 3DGS an explicit, interpretable frequency hierarchy instead of the usual undifferentiated point cloud. Every Gaussian carries a non-learnable integer level l_i; new levels are introduced every K=2500 training steps by duplicating all existing Gaussians (most duplicates get pruned by adaptive density control), and rendering the k-th level means rasterizing all Gaussians with l_i <= k in one pass at full resolution (never subsampled, to avoid Nyquist aliasing corrupting the frequency separation). Because plain additive alpha blending can't represent negative residual detail the way a Laplacian pyramid's residual levels can, the paper extends spherical-harmonic colors on levels above the base from [0,1] to [-1,1] via C_i = (SH(G_i,d)*2)-1, letting higher levels subtract as well as add. Two losses enforce the frequency separation: a DFT-magnitude discrepancy d_DFT(I_k, hat-I_k) = (1/UV) sum ||F_k(u,v)| - |hat-F_k(u,v)||, summed over all but the final level, plus a spatial term using a Laplacian-pyramid REDUCE (Gaussian-blur + 2x subsample) operator applied L-k times before an L1+D-SSIM comparison. Results on Mip-NeRF 360 / Tanks&Temples / Deep Blending: the 3-level variant tops SSIM, PSNR and FPS on every dataset among LOD-capable baselines (FLOD, OCTree-GS, MSGS, FreGS, Lapis-GS) while using far fewer Gaussians than dense 3DGS; nearest-neighbor spatial queries on the low-frequency subset alone are 2.21x faster (1.95s to 0.88s on 100K points) with only 0.18% positional error versus the full model; and gaze-weighted foveated rendering (discarding low-opacity high-frequency Gaussians outside the gaze ROI) yields a 40% FPS improvement at 4K. For TAYF this is directly applicable to the compact-representation and transport stages: because low-frequency Gaussians alone reconstruct a coherent (if blurred) human and can be transmitted/rendered first, the same coarse-to-fine level structure could back progressive avatar streaming over degraded links and foveated/viewer-adaptive optical rendering, letting the free-space display engine allocate its limited angular/voxel budget to only the frequency bands a given observer's gaze region needs — the caveat is all numbers come from static offline scene capture, not a live dynamic human avatar, so temporal stability of the level assignment under motion is untested.

### 2504.20466 — LMME3DHF: Benchmarking and Evaluating Multimodal 3D Human Face Generation with LMMs

This paper builds Gen3DHF, a 2,000-video benchmark of AI-generated 3D human faces (from 10 generator families spanning EG3D/PanoHead/Next3D/AniFaceGAN/Portrait4D-class tri-plane and NeRF-based GANs) annotated with 4,000 MOS scores along two explicitly separated axes — perceptual quality (sharpness, naturalness, aesthetic polish) and authenticity (anatomical plausibility, identity coherence, believable micro-expression/gaze) — plus 2,000 distortion-aware saliency maps built by converting participant click-point annotations into Gaussian-filtered heatmaps, and per-sample textual distortion descriptions. The proposed model, LMME3DHF, wraps a Qwen2.5-VL-7B vision-language backbone with (1) a text-generation head that emits a 5-level quality/authenticity descriptor via a threshold rule L(s)=l_i for m+(i-1)/5*(M-m) < s <= m+i/5*(M-m), (2) a LoRA-fine-tuned regression head predicting continuous MOS values with L1 loss, and (3) a U-Net-style saliency decoder fusing ViT visual features (via PixelShuffle upsampling) with LLM multimodal hidden states (via 3D convolutions) trained with a weighted sum of L1, correlation-coefficient, KL-divergence and BCE losses. LMME3DHF beats handcrafted IQA metrics (BRISQUE/NIQE, which the authors show transfer poorly from natural-image distortions to synthetic 3D face artifacts) and other LMM/VQA baselines on both MOS-prediction correlation and distortion-localization AUC/NSS/CC/SIM/KLD. For TAYF this is a perception-track measurement tool rather than a pipeline component: its explicit quality/authenticity split gives a template for evaluating whatever the free-space display ultimately reconstructs of a human face — the paper's finding that "high visual quality does not guarantee semantic/authenticity accuracy" is a direct warning that TAYF should not rely on generic image-quality metrics (PSNR/SSIM) to certify that a reconstructed face still "reads" as a genuine remote person; the distortion-saliency maps also indicate which facial sub-regions (mouth, eyes) most drive authenticity judgments and thus deserve priority in the cube's angular/voxel budget.

### 2505.10144 — VRSplat: Fast and Robust Gaussian Splatting for Virtual Reality

VRSplat targets the specific failure modes of 3DGS in head-mounted VR: popping artifacts from view-dependent Gaussian resorting, and projection-error "cloud" artifacts from the standard local-affine projection approximation at wide FOV. It fine-tunes a Mini-Splatting backbone (already ~10x smaller than vanilla 3DGS) for only 5K iterations using the StopThePop hierarchical per-pixel resorter (which fixes training/render sort-order mismatch that otherwise costs ~1.5dB PSNR) combined with Optimal Projection, which projects each Gaussian onto the tangent plane of the unit sphere at the camera perpendicular to the camera-to-mean line rather than a single shared image plane, and derives a closed-form maximum-density point x-hat = p + [d^T*Sigma_2D^-1*(mu_2D - p)]/[d^T*Sigma_2D^-1*d] * d for adapting StopThePop's tile-culling to this per-Gaussian optimal plane. To recover the resulting performance cost, the paper introduces single-pass foveated rendering: the image is tiled at 16x16 in a fixed central region and 32x32 (each thread handling a 2x2 pixel block) in the periphery, with dotted transition tiles blended via a continuous mask, plus OpenXR visibility-mask culling that removes ~6% of Gaussian/tile combinations outside the HMD's visible area — importantly this fixed foveation works even without eye tracking, since HMD lens optics already concentrate pixel density centrally. In a 25-participant Meta Quest 3 user study (Wilcoxon tests, pairwise Likert -2..+2), VRSplat's "preference" and "artifacts" ratings against Mini-Splatting were statistically significant (t=18.0/61.0, p<.001) while visual "quality" ratings were preserved or improved despite the foveation (t=209.5, p=.62 vs. one baseline; p<.03 favoring VRSplat vs. the other), and under a large-FOV evaluation protocol (3x resolution, cropped) VRSplat scores 27.29dB/0.823 SSIM on Mip-NeRF360 versus 26.82dB/0.804 for vanilla 3DGS. For TAYF this is a rendering-stage precedent directly on-topic for wide-FOV, real-time, resource-constrained 3DGS display: it empirically confirms that foveated/peripheral-quality reduction is imperceptible to real human observers (not just a theoretical saving) under exactly the kind of large-FOV, low-power constraint TAYF's cube optics would impose, and the single-pass tile-based foveation strategy is a plausible template for a free-space optical engine that must budget its angular emission non-uniformly around a tracked observer.

### 2505.23301 — Quality assessment of 3D human animation: Subjective and objective evaluation

This paper builds a controlled psychophysical dataset (4DHumanPercept, 250 acquired-vs-distorted virtual-human pairs, 24-48 raters per stimulus) using the ITU DSIS methodology (side-by-side reference/distorted, 5-point "imperceptible" to "very annoying" degradation scale) to quantify exactly how much motion/geometry distortion a human observer tolerates before noticing. Distortions are injected via SMPL-parameter manipulation on real 4D scans from the 4DHumanOutfit dataset: footskating (gamma_G = gamma_A * K, K>1 sliding, K<1 moonwalking), foot-contact drift (gamma_G = gamma_A + L on the vertical axis), motion smoothness (random frame deletion by percentage S), joint twist and self-intersection artifacts (theta_G = theta_A + angle offset), each at 5 calibrated strength levels; a pilot study with 5 raters (Friedman/Durbin-Conover, e.g. moonwalking chi^2(7)=31.6, p<.001) was used to select strength ranges that actually span "non-noticeable to exaggerated." The mixed-design ANOVA over the 48-participant main study finds distortion strength is the only factor with a consistently large effect size across all 6 distortion types (partial eta^2 = 0.52-0.81), while subject identity, gender, clothing and motion type produce smaller, distortion-specific interaction effects — i.e. tolerance for a given geometric/kinematic error is not a fixed universal threshold but interacts with body identity and context. From these labeled distortions the authors derive 7 objective features (Chamfer/Hausdorff shape distance, foot-contact and global-translation error, velocity difference, log-dimensionless-jerk motion-smoothness difference, and per-joint MPJPE) and fit a linear regression, "4DHumanQA," which predicts MOS with SROCC=0.961/PLCC=0.917 versus only 0.76/0.729 for LPIPS on the same held-out test set — showing that simple, cheap kinematic/geometric distance features beat a deep perceptual image metric for animated-human quality prediction. For TAYF this is squarely a Track D (perception) result: it supplies calibrated, per-distortion-type JND-like thresholds (with statistically dialed-in strength levels) for exactly the kinds of artifacts a compressed/predicted/re-targeted avatar pipeline would introduce (foot sliding from pose-prediction error, jerky motion from frame drops on a lossy link, self-intersection from imperfect body-model fitting), and 4DHumanQA is a lightweight, real-time-feasible drop-in quality gate — computed from SMPL joint/vertex error rather than rendered pixels — that TAYF could run on-device to decide, frame by frame, whether the transmitted pose stream needs re-synchronization before the optical engine reconstructs it.

### 2506.12269 — ICME 2025 Grand Challenge on Video Super-Resolution for Video Conferencing

A Microsoft-run benchmark defining exactly the perceptual-quality regime TAYF's transport stage would need to survive: low-resolution video H.265-encoded at fixed low-delay QPs, upscaled causally (no future frames, matching a live conversational constraint) by learned VSR models across three tracks — general real-world video (x4, REDS/OpenVid-1M), talking-head video (x4, an extended VCD dataset), and screen content (x3, a newly released 95-clip 1080p desktop-capture dataset). Submissions were scored not by PSNR/SSIM but by crowdsourced Comparison Category Rating per ITU-T P.910 (raters directly compare processed vs. source clip on a -3..+3 scale, yielding CMOS), on the explicit grounds (cited from prior literature) that PSNR/SSIM/MS-SSIM correlate poorly with subjective judgment; the screen-content track additionally scores Character Error Rate from OCR on annotated text regions, combined as score = (CMOS/-3 + CER)/2, since text legibility is a distinct failure mode standard image metrics miss entirely. Results show image-based single-frame SR models (RealSR, SwinIR) beating video-specific causal models (BasicVSR++, RealViformer) on both the general and talking-head tracks despite the latter's higher raw PSNR (e.g. Track 2: SwinIR CMOS -2.122 best-ranked vs. BasicVSR++'s better PSNR of 35.385 dB but CMOS -2.189), underscoring the same PSNR/perception mismatch; a same-video test-retest CER of 0.0024 sets the practical noise floor for that metric. For TAYF's transport track this is a directly transferable result: it validates that a talking-head-specific low-delay causal SR/reconstruction module (analogous to what a receive-side cube would need to run on the decoded avatar stream before optical rendering) is achievable and benchmarkable, and it is a cautionary data point that any TAYF quality-control loop should use CCR-style human comparison rather than PSNR/SSIM alone, since those metrics can rank models in the wrong order for exactly the talking-head content TAYF cares about.

### 2506.14381 — Compressed Video Super-Resolution based on Hierarchical Encoding

VSR-HE (Team BVI-VSR's ICME 2025 submission) is a per-frame (non-recurrent, causal-compliant) 4x super-resolution network built from HiET hierarchical-encoding transformer blocks (window sizes [64,32,8,32,64], B=6 blocks, 126 channels) trained in two stages against H.265/HEVC compression artifacts specifically. Stage 1 uses a combined perceptual loss L_p = 0.3*L_L1 + 0.2*L_SSIM + 0.1*L_L2 + 0.4*L_MS-SSIM; stage 2 adds an ESRGAN-style adversarial term, L_total = L_p + 0.05*L_GAN, to sharpen realism beyond what pixel/structural losses alone give. Training data combines the challenge's REDS/VCD sets with the BVI-AOM database, HEVC-compressed at QP in {17,22,27,32,34,37}, cropped to 64x64 (LR) / 256x256 (HR) patches (~100K pairs per track). On the ICME 2025 test sequences VSR-HE reaches 26.47dB/0.8759 SSIM/0.8829 MS-SSIM/59.17 VMAF on Track 1 (180p to 720p) and 33.17dB/0.9635/0.9650/60.12 VMAF on Track 2 (270p to 1080p, talking heads), beating bicubic, EDSR, CVEGAN and SwinIR baselines on every metric in both tracks, at a measured cost of 5.43M parameters / 455.16 GFLOPs / 140.61ms per frame (Track 1) or 375.11ms per frame (Track 2) on an RTX 4090. For TAYF this is a concrete transport-stage data point on the bitrate-vs-perceptual-quality tradeoff for exactly the talking-head content the cube would stream: it demonstrates that a compact (5.4M-parameter) causal network can substantially undo heavy HEVC compression at low delay, but the reported per-frame latency (140-375ms on a desktop RTX 4090) is far above TAYF's real-time/battery-constrained budget, meaning either the model needs aggressive pruning/quantization for an embedded cube GPU or the SR step should be replaced by the avatar-parameter-level compression TAYF's architecture favors instead of pixel-domain VSR.

### 2506.14642 — 3DGS-IEval-15K: A Large-scale Image Quality Evaluation Database for 3D Gaussian-Splatting

This is the largest published perceptual dataset specifically for compressed-3DGS visual quality: 15,200 rendered images spanning 10 real scenes (6 from Mip-NeRF 360, 2 from Tanks & Temples, 2 from Deep Blending) x 6 representative compression algorithms (LightGaussian's prune+VQ, c3dgs's k-means codebooks, Compact-3DGS's hash-grid + residual VQ achieving >25x reduction, CompGS's straight VQ, HAC's context-model anchor compression, Scaffold-GS's anchor-based dynamic Gaussian generation) x systematically designed Distortion Levels (4x4=16 DLs for multi-parameter algorithms crossing geometry and color compression strength, 6 DLs for single-parameter anchor-based methods), yielding 720 distinct 3DGS models rendered from 20 viewpoints each. Viewpoints are chosen deliberately, not randomly: 10 training-set viewpoints via k-means clustering on a feature vector f=[position, beta*direction] (beta=0.3), and 10 "hard" testing viewpoints ranked by a composite novelty score S_j = w_d*D_j + w_s*S_j + w_e*E_j + w_theta*Theta_j (all weights 0.25) combining maximum distance from training views, local sparsity, whether the point lies outside the training-viewpoint convex hull (forcing extrapolation, not interpolation), and directional divergence — explicitly probing view-dependent generalization rather than just interpolation quality. MOS was collected from 60 raters via an 11-level ITU-R BT.500 impairment scale with 2% outlier rejection; a key empirical finding is that held-out/novel test viewpoints score systematically lower MOS than training viewpoints (peaking at 4-5 vs. 5-6), i.e. compression artifacts that are invisible from a training camera pose become visible from novel angles — and among 30 benchmarked IQA algorithms, deep-learning IQA models (MANIQA, HyperIQA) reach SRCC > 0.93 on the full set but drop noticeably (e.g. MANIQA 0.9356 to 0.8443) when evaluated on geometry-only distortions, showing today's IQA metrics don't generalize well across 3DGS-specific distortion types. For TAYF this view-dependent MOS gap is a direct warning for the free-space display: since observers of the reconstructed remote human will inherently be viewing from angles the capture/compression pipeline did not train on, quality that looks acceptable from the "canonical" capture viewpoint may visibly degrade at the oblique angles a real conversational partner would actually use, meaning TAYF's compression budget should be allocated using novel-view-robustness criteria (similar to this paper's composite score) rather than only reconstruction error at known training views.

### 2507.06060 — VisualSpeaker: Visually-Guided 3D Avatar Lip Synthesis

VisualSpeaker adds a perceptual "lip-readability" supervision signal to speech-driven 3D facial animation by rendering the predicted FLAME mesh through a per-subject 3D Gaussian avatar (Gaussians rigidly bound to FLAME topology following GaussianAvatars, with 120 extra triangles added for teeth) and passing only a cropped 96x96 lip region through a frozen pretrained AutoAVSR lip-reading network; the loss is simply the cosine distance between AutoAVSR embeddings of predicted vs. ground-truth lip renders, L_read = 1 - CosSim(AutoAVSR(I_T), AutoAVSR(hat-I_T)), and is added to a per-vertex weighted MSE (L = L_vert + 1e-5*L_read) only in a third curriculum stage after geometric pretraining on VOCASET and adaptation to noisier MEAD pseudo-ground-truth. The key validation that 3DGS renders (rather than raw mesh renders) are a suitable proxy for this perceptual loss: matching video/render pairs achieve mean AutoAVSR cosine similarity of 0.697 vs. 0.190 for mismatched pairs, confirming the differentiable-3DGS-to-recognition-model path carries real lip-reading-relevant signal. Adding the perceptual loss cuts Lip Vertex Error on MEAD from 3.85mm (geometric loss alone) to 1.69mm (a 56.1% improvement) even though standard image metrics barely move (PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS stay within 0.2dB/0.003/0.001 of baseline) — a clear demonstration that pixel-level metrics are nearly blind to the exact articulatory detail that matters for intelligibility. A 51-participant user study confirms this transfers to human judgment: VisualSpeaker is preferred over the no-perceptual-loss baseline 63.8% of the time on realism and 66.6% on lip clarity, though it's still preferred less often than pseudo-ground-truth renders (34.9%/33.6%), with users specifically flagging that missing eye motion/blinking hurt overall realism more than any remaining lip inaccuracy. For TAYF this is a human-representation-track technique directly reusable for the face/mouth channel of the avatar pipeline: it shows that a cheap, differentiable, real-time-compatible perceptual loss computed through the exact same Gaussian-splat renderer used for final display can materially improve intelligibility of the transmitted expression stream without adding bandwidth, and its finding that non-verbal upper-face cues (gaze, blinks) matter as much as lip accuracy for perceived presence directly supports TAYF's "perceptual allocation" principle of prioritizing face/eyes/mouth fidelity.

### 2507.14454 — Adaptive 3D Gaussian Splatting Video Streaming: Visual Saliency-Aware Tiling and Meta-Learning-Based Bitrate Adaptation

This is a full adaptive-streaming system for dynamic 3DGS "volumetric video" with three integrated components directly relevant to TAYF's transport track. (1) Saliency-driven tiling: Gaussians within each Group-of-Frames tile are first importance-sampled by rendering weight w_i = sigma_i * sqrt(det(Sigma_i)) (opacity times an effective-volume term from the covariance determinant), then a "Local Discrepancy Catcher" explicitly encodes each Gaussian's positional and grayscale-color (0.299R+0.587G+0.114B, matching human luminance sensitivity) difference from its neighbors via an MLP, aggregated through softmax attention pooling and stacked dilated residual blocks to reach a K^2 receptive field; a parallel temporal-contrast branch computes a saliency-amplification score O_s = 1/(1+exp(S_sim)) + 1 from the similarity between consecutive GoF global features, and the two saliency streams are attention-fused (F_C = A_S*F_S + A_T*F_T) into a per-tile score used to hierarchically cluster tiles into viewport-optimized irregular regions. (2) A dynamic per-tile 3DGS encoding scheme classifies tiles as static/low-dynamic/high-dynamic with shared or dedicated deformation fields, producing 5 saliency-weighted quality tiers via adaptive Gaussian pruning per tile — solving the practical problem that existing dynamic-3DGS methods operate only on whole models, incompatible with tiled streaming. (3) A meta-reinforcement-learning ABR controller trained/tested across real 4G/5G bandwidth traces (Std4G 35-90Mbps, Ext4G 0-150Mbps, Std5G 150-600Mbps, Ext5G 0-1200Mbps) and validated against a real 50-participant Meta Quest 3 head-trajectory dataset; it beats four point-cloud-video ABR baselines (POT, QDA, HST, RLA) in QoE across all bandwidth regimes, and its meta-learned few-shot efficiency is notable: with only 20% of Std5G training data it reaches 84.9% of full-data QoE, and 50% of the data reaches 94.3%. For TAYF this paper is close to a template Track-C system: tile-level saliency (spatial + temporal) mapped to per-tile quality tiers is exactly the kind of viewport/attention-adaptive bandwidth allocation TAYF's cube-to-cube link would need for a live 3DGS or Gaussian-avatar stream, and the meta-RL few-shot result is directly relevant to a consumer device that must adapt to a new, previously-unseen network path (e.g. two cubes connecting over an arbitrary residential ISP link) without lengthy per-link retraining; the main caveat is the system assumes VR headset-style FoV/head-trajectory prediction as its saliency ground truth, which does not map one-to-one onto an unconstrained bystander observer viewing a free-space optical reconstruction from an arbitrary, untracked position.

### 2509.17748 — "I don't like my avatar": Investigating Human Digital Doubles
Controlled offline experiment (n=22-23, within-subjects, two-way repeated-measures ANOVA) comparing realistic (Unreal MetaHuman) vs. stylized cartoon (ReadyPlayerMe) avatars, at three familiarity levels (self, acquaintance, unknown person), on self/other-identification, perceived realism, affinity (appeal/eeriness), and social presence (using Zibrek et al.'s social-presence scale). Findings, all with reported F/p/η²p: realistic style significantly raises appearance/talking/expression/movement/behavior identification (all p<0.002, η²p 0.36-0.60), raises perceived realism on every sub-scale (p<0.001, η²p up to 0.60), and raises social presence (F(1,21)=10.1, p=0.005, η²p=0.325) — but *also* raises perceived eeriness (F(1,21)=11.7, p=0.003) and *lowers* appeal/attractiveness (F(1,21)=6.0, p=0.023) relative to cartoon avatars. Familiarity has an independent, opposite-direction effect: participants are the harshest critics of their *own* digital double — the self-like avatar scored significantly lower than the unknown-person avatar on appearance identification (p=0.004) and overall realism (p=0.002), and was rated the most eerie of the three (p=0.032 vs. unknown-person). For TAYF: this is a real, numerically-supported finding but it is not a "how much optical fidelity is enough" threshold — it's a style/familiarity interaction. It matters to TAYF because the remote party seeing themselves (self-avatar) or a known colleague (TAYF's actual use case — video calling with people you know) is the harsher psychophysical regime than the anonymous-stranger regime most digital-human perception papers implicitly study. It also empirically documents that higher photorealism does not monotonically improve subjective preference (uncanny-valley eeriness rises with realism) — relevant to `docs/theory.md`'s "convincing telepresence ≠ complete fidelity" hypothesis, but as a ceiling-effect / non-monotonicity argument, not a numeric threshold.

### 2401.02171 — Real-and-Present: Investigating the Use of Life-Size 2D Video Avatars in HMD-Based AR Telepresence
HMD-based AR teleconferencing study (HoloLens 2 + VR-simulated AR follow-up) comparing four remote-user representations: **Video** (flat 2D video window, not spatially placed), **Video Grid** (video window transplanted into the AR scene without background removal/placement), **Avatar** (rigged 3D avatar), and **Video Avatar** (background-removed, life-size 2D video "cutout" placed and oriented correctly in 3D space — i.e. a flat billboard, not a volumetric reconstruction). All four rated on 7-point Likert for Fidelity/Realism, Co-Presence, and Preference (Friedman + post-hoc Nemenyi, p<.001 omnibus for both Fidelity and Co-Presence). Key numeric result: Video Avatar achieved co-presence M=5.2 (SD=1.3), statistically indistinguishable from full 3D Avatar's M=5.3 (SD=1.4), while Video Avatar's fidelity (M=5.1, SD=1.0) was statistically indistinguishable from plain flat Video (M=5.0) and Video Grid (M=5.4) — and *significantly higher* than the 3D Avatar's fidelity (M=3.7, SD=1.2, p<.001). Video Avatar was also the most preferred condition overall (M=5.5), the only condition significantly preferred over baseline flat Video (M=4.2). The authors conclude fidelity may matter more than co-presence mechanics for conversational preference, and that a life-size, correctly-spatially-placed **flat 2D image** (no volumetric geometry, no view-dependent parallax) already delivers co-presence statistically equal to a full rigged 3D avatar while keeping photographic identity fidelity that the 3D avatar could not match. A companion FoV follow-up study (VR-simulated AR, HTC Vive Pro, FoV swept 30-110°) produced numeric regression models mapping headset FoV to preferred remote-user placement angle/distance for multiple simultaneous remote users (e.g. `Radius ≈ -0.0045·FoV + 1.34` for 1 remote user, all fits p<.001), useful for `docs/calibration.md`'s viewing-cone/placement question but orthogonal to the optical-fidelity question. **For TAYF: this is the strongest concrete data point found in this pass suggesting spatial placement + photographic fidelity, without volumetric/view-dependent geometry, can match a full 3D representation's sense of presence** — directly relevant to `docs/theory.md`'s engineering hypothesis. Caveat: the numbers come from an AR HMD (single tracked eye-viewpoint per frame, headset-mediated), not a true free-space multi-viewer display, so this does not establish that a glasses-free optical engine can skip view-dependent/parallax information for multiple simultaneous viewpoints — only that for a single tracked viewpoint, correctly-placed 2D matches or beats 3D on the metrics tested.

### 2201.04168 — A Survey on Applications of Digital Human Avatars toward Virtual Co-presence
An informal literature survey (appears to be an unpublished/early-draft compilation, dated internally 2019 despite the 2022 arXiv id) cataloguing avatar-based vs. non-avatar-based virtual co-presence systems (telepresence robots, mobile systems, video tiling, motion-retargeted avatars). It is a summary-of-summaries with almost no primary numeric results of its own; the one concrete number found is a secondhand citation that an upper-body motion-retargeting system achieved "80% consistency rate between real and digital motions" (attributed to Han et al., not independently verified here). No controlled psychophysical thresholds, no identity/presence quantification. Low relevance to Track D's numeric-threshold question; flagged here for completeness of the corpus sweep but not usable as evidence — treat any number from this paper as unverified until traced to Han et al.'s original source.

### 2307.16114 — HoloBots: Augmenting Holographic Telepresence with Mobile Robots for Tangible Remote Collaboration in Mixed Reality
Within-subject user study (n=12, HoloLens 2 hologram + Toio mobile robots) comparing Hologram+Robots vs. Hologram-Only vs. Robots-Only across four collaborative tasks, measuring social presence (Networked Minds questionnaire, Cronbach's α 0.78-0.93), NASA-TLX cognitive workload, SUS usability, and preference. Hologram+Robots scored significantly higher social presence than Robots-Only on Co-Presence and Perceived-Message-Understanding subscales in two of four tasks (p<.05-.001); no significant workload or usability differences between conditions; 75% of participants preferred Hologram+Robots overall (χ²(2)=9.5, p=0.009). This paper is about *adding a physical/tangible channel* (robot proxies) to a hologram, not about how much optical/visual fidelity the hologram itself needs — it doesn't test different hologram fidelity levels, only hologram-present vs. hologram-absent. Relevance to Track D is limited: it confirms holographic visual presence measurably beats voice-only, but gives no fidelity/view-count/resolution threshold. Not a strong candidate for narrowing the optical-engine gap.

### 2409.08577 — Exploring Remote Collaborative Tasks: The Impact of Avatar Representation on Dyadic Haptic Collaboration
Controlled VR study (repeated-measures ANOVA, n≈28) crossing haptic interaction with four avatar-visibility conditions in a shared virtual environment: ALL (both avatars shown), ALONE (only own avatar), OTHER (only partner's avatar), NONE (no avatars). Used generic gender-neutral avatars (no personalization) to isolate presence-of-avatar from avatar identity/style. Measured Social Presence and Co-presence via the Networked Minds Social Presence Questionnaire (NMSPQ). Result, with F/p/η²p reported: main effect of condition on Social Presence F(2.30,62.0)=24.4, p<.001, η²p=.48, and Co-presence F(2.07,55.9)=27.3, p<.001, η²p=.50. Critically, post-hoc comparisons show ALONE (seeing only your own avatar) is *not* significantly different from NONE (no avatars at all) for Social Presence (p=.669) or Co-presence (p=.250) — but OTHER (seeing the partner's avatar) is significantly higher than both ALONE and NONE (p<.001-.035), and not significantly different from ALL. Task performance and force-effort were statistically unaffected by avatar condition in every comparison. For TAYF: this cleanly isolates that *displaying the remote human's avatar to the local viewer* is what drives social presence — your own self-avatar contributes essentially nothing — which validates TAYF's fundamental design target (optically emit the remote party, not the local user) but does not give a fidelity/resolution/view-count threshold; it only tests avatar-present vs. avatar-absent, not fidelity gradations, and the avatar used was a low-detail generic placeholder, not tested against higher-fidelity alternatives.

### 2506.21780 — Avatars and Environments for Meetings in Social VR: What Styles and Choices Matter to People
Two-part social-VR study (survey + n=40 controlled group-creativity experiment, Mozilla Hubs) testing whether avatar realism/style (humanoid vs. non-humanoid, realistic vs. fanciful) and environment style affect group creative-task performance and preference. All quantitative comparisons were null: avatar fanciful-ness had no effect on creativity score (F(2,17)=0.02, p>.05, η²=.002), avatar humanoid-ness had no effect (F(2,17)=1.11, p>.05, η²=.12), environment style had no effect (t(18)=0.31, p>.05), and pre-existing familiarity between partners had no effect (t(18)=0.96, p>.05, d=.4). Qualitatively, participants strongly preferred *choosing* their own avatar (agency) over any particular realism level, and reported that seeing the *partner's* avatar animate mattered more for perceived presence than the avatar's own realism. For TAYF: a genuine (if underpowered, n=40, low statistical power) null result that avatar photorealism/style did not measurably change task performance in this creative-collaboration context — weak supporting evidence for `docs/theory.md`'s hypothesis that fidelity requirements may be lower than assumed, but it is a null result on a *task-performance* measure, not a validated numeric perceptual threshold, and the study explicitly reports it lacked power to detect anything but a large effect.

### 2501.08072 — Evaluating Human Perception of Novel View Synthesis: Subjective Quality Assessment of Gaussian Splatting and NeRF
Two subjective MOS studies (real, dynamic, and 360°/front-facing/single-viewpoint scenes) comparing five NVS reconstruction methods (NeRFacto, K-Planes, GS, GS-fewer-iterations, STGFS) rated by human observers on a MOS scale (0-100, 95% CI reported per scene). Average multi-view MOS: NeRFacto 42.3, K-Planes 25.4, GS 52.6, GS(fewer iters) 54.2, STGFS 57.3; single-view test similar ordering (38.1-54.5). GS-based methods consistently beat NeRF-based methods on subjective quality; a dynamic-scene method (STGFS) outperformed a static one (K-Planes vs NeRFacto), and interestingly a GS variant trained with *fewer* iterations scored *higher* than the fully-converged GS in 9/13 (multi-view) and 11/13 (single-view) scenes — a non-monotonic fidelity/perception relationship the authors attribute to overfitting artifacts at full convergence. The paper's other key claim (stated but not deeply quoted here) is that standard objective metrics (PSNR/SSIM) correlate poorly with these MOS scores, i.e. engineering-convenient proxy metrics mislead about actual human-perceived quality. For TAYF: relevant methodologically to `experiments/perceptual-quality/README.md` — reinforces that MOS/human-rating protocols, not PSNR/SSIM, should be the ground truth for TAYF's own perceptual-quality experiments — but this paper does not sweep a controllable fidelity axis (view count, voxel density) against MOS, so it gives no "N views is enough" numeric threshold, only a method-vs-method MOS ranking.

### 2404.09003 — THQA: A Perceptual Quality Assessment Database for Talking Heads
Database/benchmark paper: 800 talking-head (TH) videos (20 StyleGAN-generated faces × varied age/gender × 8 speech-driven methods, e.g. MakeItTalk-class systems), rated by 40 human subjects (32,000 total ratings, MOS on 0-5 scale) across three dimensions: image quality, lip-sound consistency, overall naturalness. The paper catalogs nine recurring distortion types (blur, lip-sync mismatch, identity drift across frames, etc.) and benchmarks standard image/video quality metrics (PSNR-family, FID, CSIM) against the MOS data, finding — consistent with 2501.08072 above — that mainstream objective IQA/VQA metrics correlate poorly with human MOS for talking-head content, and that reference-based metrics like FID/CSIM are unusable in deployment because there's no "clean" reference available to a real end user. No fidelity-sweep experiment (e.g., testing different resolutions or compression levels against MOS) is reported; this is a distortion-taxonomy/benchmark contribution, not a threshold study. For TAYF: reinforces the methodological point that any perceptual-quality metric TAYF's own experiments adopt should be validated against real human MOS, not assumed from PSNR/SSIM/FID — but contributes no numeric fidelity threshold.

### 2403.06421 — A Comparative Study of Perceptual Quality Metrics for Audio-driven Talking Head Videos
Controlled 2AFC (two-alternative forced choice) psychophysical experiments comparing four talking-head generation methods (AD-NeRF, DFA-NeRF, Wav2Lip, and one more) on visual quality, lip-audio sync, and head-movement naturalness, then checking which automated metrics best predict the human votes. Findings: PSNR/SSIM correlate poorly with human preference because they over-penalize spatial misalignment that viewers don't mind (concrete illustrated case: humans preferred DFA-NeRF over AD-NeRF despite AD-NeRF scoring higher PSNR/SSIM); no-reference quality metrics sometimes beat the best full-reference metric (DISTS) by large margins; a semantic video-retrieval-style similarity metric (ViSiL) predicts head-movement naturalness much better than landmark-distance metrics, because human raters judge head-movement "naturalness" holistically rather than requiring exact trajectory match to a reference. For TAYF: another confirmation that engineering-convenient fidelity proxies (pixel-accurate reproduction) diverge from what viewers actually judge as acceptable — supports `docs/theory.md`'s hypothesis directionally (exact reproduction isn't what's valued) but gives no numeric fidelity/view-count/resolution threshold of its own.

### 2511.08032 — Perceptual Quality Assessment of 3D Gaussian Splatting: A Subjective Dataset and Prediction Model (3DGS-QA)
Subjective quality dataset: 225 distorted 3DGS reconstructions across 15 object categories, with controlled distortion axes including **number of input capture views** (360 / 270 / 180, corresponding to 1°/1.5°/2° angular sampling density during scanning), training iterations, point downsampling, Gaussian noise, and color noise — rated by human subjects for MOS. Key qualitative finding: reducing the number of *input reconstruction views* (i.e., how many camera images were used to build the 3DGS model) produces a **bimodal** MOS distribution rather than a smooth decline — objects with "sufficient" input viewpoints score well, but once view count drops below an object-dependent threshold, quality collapses sharply because the underlying reconstruction becomes geometrically underdetermined (the paper does not give one universal numeric threshold, noting it is object-category-dependent). By contrast, downsampling/noise/color distortions produce smooth, monotonic, near-normal-distributed MOS decline. **Important scope caveat for TAYF:** this "number of views" is the number of *camera images used to reconstruct* the 3D model (a capture/reconstruction-quality axis), not the number of *angular output views an optical display emits simultaneously to viewers* (TAYF's Track C/D concern). Do not conflate the two — this paper says nothing about how many simultaneous output view-directions a free-space display needs to look convincing; it is evidence about SfM/3DGS capture-view sufficiency, which is closer to `pipeline/avatar` capture concerns than to `hardware/optical-engine.md`'s emission concerns.

### 2510.03874 — DHQA-4D: Perceptual Quality Assessment of Dynamic 4D Digital Humans
Subjective MOS dataset (32 real-scanned dynamic clothed-human 4D mesh sequences, 1920 textured + 832 non-textured distorted variants across 11 distortion types: geometry noise, color noise, texture downsampling, mesh simplification, texture/UV/position compression combinations, and temporal discontinuity). Key finding relevant to perceptual budget allocation: temporal-domain distortion (frame-to-frame discontinuity, "DC") produced *relatively high* MOS scores in both subsets — i.e. viewers tolerate temporal jitter/discontinuity in a dynamic human mesh better than they tolerate texture or geometry distortion — and UV-map-coordinate compression (UMC) likewise showed little perceptual impact, while texture-map compression, geometry+texture compression, and position compression spanned the full MOS range (10-90) and were clearly the dominant perceptual drivers. This is a mesh/texture-compression MOS study (not an optical-emission study), but it is a genuine, numerically-grounded ranking of "which distortion axis matters most to a human viewer" for a moving clothed human — directly useful to `docs/theory.md`'s "perceptual allocation" principle: it says geometry/texture fidelity should be prioritized over temporal smoothness and UV precision when a representation budget must be spent unevenly. No single "X% is enough" scalar threshold is given; it is a relative-sensitivity ranking, not an absolute cutoff.

### 2503.20308 — Perceptually Accurate 3D Talking Head Generation: New Definitions, Speech-Mesh Synchronized Representations
Defines three criteria for perceptually accurate 3D talking heads (temporal synchronization, lip readability, expressiveness) and runs two human A/B studies. Cites an established psychophysics finding (Vatakis et al. 2006, audiovisual asynchrony perception) that speech-lip mismatch becomes noticeable when speech leads lip movement by more than **50ms**, or lags it by more than **220ms** — this is a secondary citation within the paper, not this paper's own new measurement, but it is consistently and specifically invoked (main text + supplementary), giving a concrete audiovisual-sync JND figure. This paper's own human study (A/B forced choice) found that when forced to choose between (A) a sample with precise temporal sync but flat/inexpressive lip motion vs. (B) a sample with expressive, speech-intensity-matched lip motion but 100ms audio-lip asynchrony (double the ~50ms "acceptable" threshold), **82.6% of participants preferred the expressive-but-mistimed sample B** over the precisely-timed-but-flat sample A. A second study confirmed participants prefer lip-movement intensity that matches speech intensity (loud speech → bigger mouth movements) over intensity-mismatched-but-technically-correct lip shapes. For TAYF: this is a real, numerically clean, directly relevant finding — for a talking human's face specifically, *expressiveness/motion-amplitude fidelity matters more to perceived quality than millisecond-precise timing accuracy*, even past double the classical audiovisual-sync JND. This is useful evidence for `docs/theory.md`'s "perceptual allocation" principle at the level of TAYF's latency/representation tradeoffs (`pipeline/transport/README.md`'s ~80ms latency, well under the 220ms lag JND and only modestly above the 50ms lead JND) — it suggests TAYF's optical-engine and transport budget should prioritize preserving motion amplitude/expressiveness over chasing sub-50ms synchronization precision if a tradeoff must be made. This is not a threshold on *optical channel count or view density* (Track C/D's core question) — it is a threshold on *audiovisual timing precision vs. motion expressiveness*, a different (though related) axis.

### 2607.03494 — Towards Standardized Light Field Quality Assessment: Hybrid Subjective Benchmarking and Objective Metric Analysis (JPEG Pleno)
A JPEG Pleno standardization QA-methodology paper: benchmarks objective light-field quality metrics (PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, DISTS, ST-LPIPS, etc.) against a hybrid subjective rating protocol across coding-only, sparse-view-interpolation, and 3DGS-reconstruction distortions. No single numeric "N angular views is enough" threshold is established — angular subsampling is used only as one distortion-generation method, not as a controlled sweep reported against MOS. The one transferable qualitative finding: classical metrics (PSNR/SSIM/mean-pooled fidelity measures) fail specifically on view-synthesis/reconstruction distortions (geometric shift, view-dependent inconsistency) even when they work on plain coding compression; and perceived light-field quality is driven disproportionately by the *worst* individual view rather than the average across views — worst-view pooling improved correlation with human MOS for several learned metrics. For TAYF: relevant only as a directional caution for `experiments/perceptual-quality/README.md`'s "view consistency across viewing angle" question — it suggests that if TAYF's optical engine produces even one badly-degraded viewing angle within the supported cone, that outlier may dominate perceived quality more than the average across angles — but this is inferred from a metric-correlation finding, not from a direct human-preference sweep over angle count, so treat it as a weak, indirect signal rather than a threshold.

### 2506.02380 — EyeNavGS: A 6-DoF Navigation Dataset and Record-n-Replay Software for Real-World 3DGS Scenes
Infrastructure/dataset paper (not a perceptual-threshold study): releases head-pose and eye-gaze traces from 46 participants freely exploring twelve real-world 3DGS scenes in VR (Meta Quest Pro), reporting descriptive statistics (e.g. mean total distance traveled per scene ~13.6-17.3 m) intended to support future viewport-prediction/foveated-rendering/adaptive-streaming research. No fidelity-vs-perception experiment is reported. For TAYF: potentially a useful raw-data resource for characterizing how far/how much viewers actually move relative to a displayed 3D scene (tangentially relevant to `docs/calibration.md`'s viewing-cone question), but it contains no numeric perceptual-quality threshold of its own.

### 2411.11199 — BVI-CR: A Multi-View Human Dataset for Volumetric Video Compression
Infrastructure paper: releases an 18-sequence multi-view RGB-D + textured-mesh human-action dataset (10 views, 1080p, 30fps, 10-15s each) and benchmarks conventional vs. neural volumetric-video compression codecs on it, reporting up to 38% PSNR coding-gain for neural methods over conventional codecs. This is a compression-engineering benchmark, not a human-subject perceptual study — no MOS or subjective threshold is reported in what was reviewed. Low direct relevance to Track D's numeric-threshold question; potentially useful only as a raw multi-view human capture dataset if TAYF ever needs test material.

### 2505.12431 — DPCD: A Quality Assessment Database for Dynamic Point Clouds
Subjective MOS dataset: 15 reference dynamic point clouds + 525 distorted variants (7 lossy-compression/noise distortion types), MOS from 21 viewers. Establishes that dynamic point cloud quality assessment is measurably *harder* to predict with existing objective metrics than static point cloud QA (i.e. temporal dynamics reduce metric reliability), but reports no controlled fidelity-vs-MOS sweep translatable into a "how sparse can a point cloud be" numeric threshold in what was reviewed. Low direct relevance to Track D beyond reinforcing the general "existing objective metrics under-predict human judgment for dynamic/volumetric content" pattern seen across nearly every QA paper in this batch.

### 2606.25681 — Dissociable Spatial and Temporal Effects of Interaction Latency in Virtual Reality
Controlled VR pointing-task experiment (within-subject, 6 delay levels: 0/10/50/100/200/500ms added beyond a 62ms native system latency) measuring endpoint error (EE), movement time (MT), endpoint variability (VE), and throughput (TP) for reaching toward targets with a motion-captured virtual hand. Endpoint error rose significantly even at the *smallest* added delay tested (10ms, +0.13cm, ~39% relative increase, p<.05), while movement time only began to change at 50-100ms (+14-17ms, p<.001) and throughput loss largely plateaued after ~100-200ms. The paper's discussion cites converging thresholds from prior VR latency literature: Waltemate et al. found latencies beyond **~75ms** degrade motor performance and embodiment judgments; Hoyet et al. found performance holds up to **~120ms** at slow hand speeds (350mm/s) but degrades from **~80ms** at medium/high speeds (500-650mm/s), i.e. the tolerable-latency threshold is speed-dependent, not fixed. For TAYF: this is about *sensorimotor interaction* latency (hand-tracking-driven virtual-hand rendering for a reach/point task), a different regime than TAYF's passive-viewing telepresence use case (no hand-eye coordination through the display is required for a video call) — so it should not be read as a hard ceiling on TAYF's optical-engine latency. It is nonetheless a useful cross-check: TAYF's target end-to-end latency of ≤150ms (`docs/theory.md`) sits above the ~75-120ms range where sensorimotor tasks start degrading, meaning TAYF's budget is adequate for passive conversational presence (no fast visually-guided hand action through the display) but would not suffice if a future TAYF use case required the viewer to visually guide precise physical actions using the displayed remote human as real-time feedback.

<!-- SECTION4-END -->

## Section 5 — Online Research Findings (Agent 1)

<!-- SECTION5-END -->
